Helen’s call, reversing her own arrangement of 2026-10-13: in the yurt the deck and the record case have gone through the door, and under the phonograph there is a songbook instead, bound like Nature Notes, listing every song on the yarn a line at a time with its runtime; each line goes to that record in the Music Room. Nothing on the yurt’s own page plays the yarn, and make-yurt-yarn.py refuses it if anything does. And the Reading Room has a gramophone, at her ask: her Library Music playlist on it, switched on from its plate and off from the way back under it, and every song on it as a record on a shelf among the bookcases, the Music Room’s case in that room’s own clothes. The dial’s line there says MAX turns the record. rack.js grew an opt-in, data-rack-switch, which shows a screen’s way back once its own plate is pressed; no other room’s screen takes it. 2026-10-18 — Notifications for the CB

  • The CB can notify you now, even with the site closed, after several requests from our community and especially our staff, as more of our chat and work moves onto the street. Notifications, under Your radio, switches a device on for any of four things, each off until you switch it on: somebody @mentioning you, a message in a room you follow, a call starting in a room you follow or the World call, and every message on World. A notification shows who and where and the start of the message, unless you switch the words off; a call’s says only that it has started, never who is in it. Pressing one opens its room.
  • This reverses the CB’s “no alerts”, Ryan’s call, and Privacy, the Community Center and the CB guide say so. The radio itself still has no chime, no badge and no unread count, and still listens only while it is open. What is kept is one record per device: its push address, the handle and a scrambled form of the pass it was switched on with, and what it chose; nothing it is sent. Notifications travel through your browser’s own push service, encrypted so that service cannot read them. A private room tells only people who can hear it, a changed password stops a device at once, and signing off, Forget me and deleting your username switch it off. Changed: cb.js, cb.css, netlify/cb/lib.mjs, two functions, privacy.html, cb-guide.html, community-center.html; new: cb-sw.js, netlify/functions/cb-push.mjs, tools/check-push.py.

2026-10-18 — The World room: every room on the street, and who is in each

  • The World room is open, Ryan’s brief, past the Garden: knowledge gardens and our garden spider, webs connecting gardens around the world. The spider has strung a web between every room on the street, caught by the dawn, and every room is a garden on it, in a bed for each part of the Map. Anybody can read the list.
  • Signed on to the CB, it shows which rooms are active now: who has chosen Be seen here in each, which have somebody in their call, and when each was last busy today. Show every room, the active ones or the quiet ones, and put them in the street’s order, latest activity first, or most active first. Privacy says what it shows, including the one thing it shows that was not shown before: when a room’s channel last had a message.
  • Most active reverses the street’s no-headcount rule for this one order, Ryan’s call: the server counts the people seen in each room and in its call each time somebody asks, and sends the order and never the numbers. It is the one place the street counts people, and the Mission now says so.
  • The World room’s own channel is the World channel, and its own call is the World call, so there is no second channel or call to split people between. This reverses the rule that no page may be called world.html, which only existed so a room’s call could not collide with the World’s. Changed: cb.js, netlify/cb/lib.mjs, tools/make-sitemap.py, community-center.html, privacy.html, mission.html, cb-guide.html. New: world.html, world.js, netlify/functions/cb-world.mjs, tools/make-world.py, and §119 in love.css, which moves the small screens and print to §120 and §121.

2026-10-18 — Getting started at the Community Center, and no more meeting hall

  • Our community uses Stimpunks.World and its forums for everything now, Ryan’s call, so the Community Center no longer mentions Stoat or Discord. This reverses the meeting hall of 2026-09-29, which sent people to our Stoat server for anything worth keeping and for the CB’s password.
  • The room opens with Getting started in the community, from Ryan’s draft: ask to join on the Stimpunks Community page, which gives you a starter password; sign on to the CB with it; claim your username with a password of your own, step by step on the radio’s own buttons; and then some quests to go on, from an introduction in the forums to adopting a pet, collecting badges and patches, and making your profile your own.
  • The starter password is the community password by a friendlier name: the radio and the CB guide still call it that, and the room says so. Every page that said to ask for it in the meeting hall now points at Getting started: the guest bar on the radio, the CB guide, the suggestion box in The Sandbox, the Fishbowl’s call, and the privacy page. 2026-10-18 — Coping in the forums, a week at a time

  • Coping is a category in the forums now, Ryan’s call, because it is one of the busiest channels on our Discord and people moving over need somewhere to go. A topic there goes a week after its last post, where Discord keeps that channel for ever: a place for the hard days is not somewhere to keep them. Like every category, only claimed usernames on the CB can read it. Changed: data/forum.json, netlify/cb/lib.mjs, tools/make-forum.py, which now refuses a longer keep for it.

2026-10-18 — The forums, for what needs longer than the CB’s day

  • The forums are open: topics in categories, after Discourse, for conversations that need to last longer than the channel’s midnight, Ryan’s brief. The categories follow our Discord and Stoat’s channels: Announcements, Introductions, a Help desk, the Watering hole, Events, Mutual aid, Show your work, Work opportunities, Infodumps, Reading and watching, Education, Science and making, Nature, Artificial Intelligence, Operations and Editorial, and staff areas for our moderators, directors and board, by the same CB roles as the Town Hall’s rooms. The hope is that the CB and the forums together let us leave Discord and Stoat.
  • Only a claimed username can read them, and a category somebody’s roles do not reach is never sent to them. Each category says how long a topic stays after its last post, a week, three months, six months or for good, and each topic says the date it goes; three months is the default. A pinned topic stays while it is pinned.
  • Markdown, replies, quotes, reactions named and never counted, editing and taking down your own posts, search, and for moderators pin, close, move and take down. No notifications, no badges, no counts, and nothing kept about who reads: what is new to you is worked out in your own browser.
  • A link on a line of its own becomes a preview: our server reads the page once, when the post is made, for its title and description, from public addresses only and with no pictures; a YouTube video becomes a press-to-play button that says how long it runs; a room on the street shows its own name.
  • Take to the forums, under a message’s three dots on the radio, starts a topic with messages ticked off the channel. Our server copies them off the channel itself, exactly as they were sent, and whoever sent one can take it back out of the topic. The house norm now says to ask everybody first, as for a screenshot.
  • They wear the CB’s themes: your radio’s, or any of them chosen for the forums alone, with its colours, its face for headings and its drawing. Privacy says what they keep, and the CB guide how they work. New: forum.html, forum.js, forum.css, netlify/functions/cb-forum.mjs, data/forum.json, tools/make-forum.py. Changed: netlify/cb/lib.mjs, cb.js, cb.css, privacy.html, community-center.html, architecture.html.

2026-10-18 — Otters in the kelp: an effect, a theme, a frame and a decoration

  • A large, cute profile effect, Otters in the kelp, Ryan’s ask. Over the card’s banner, sunlit kelp rises to the surface with its floats and light falls through the water. One sea otter floats on its back with a strand of kelp across it and a shell on its chest. Another rolls over at the surface now and then, and a third swims back and forth below, trailing bubbles, over urchins and a starfish. Bubbles rise across the whole profile. When the profile opens, and once a minute, the kelp forest grows up through the whole profile, and a big otter swims up, rolls once, floats with its eyes shut and drifts away.
  • A Kelp Forest radio theme, in deep green water, foam, sea glass and the kelp’s sunlit gold, with the radio’s name in a round, friendly face, and kelp, bubbles, light and now and then a floating otter along its trims. Every word clears 4.5 to 1. It is a banner and a nameplate for profiles too. With it come a Kelp fronds frame, with floats, bubbles, starfish and scallops, and an Otters in kelp decoration, two little otters floating at the foot of a ring of kelp, both drawn in the card’s own colours.
  • It is after the Arcade’s Otterly Adorbs, and so after Helen Edgar’s Stimpunks Solidarity and Otters and Star Stuff’s zine No. 58, The Stone You Keep, and it keeps the cabinet’s rules: floating fills nothing, no otter is ranked, the kelp does the holding, nobody holds paws, and nobody keeps a stone in a pouch. The tools refuse each of those. Like every effect, it moves only at MAX GLITTER or after Let my theme move, and nothing flashes. The liner notes and the CB guide say so. Changed: profile.js, cb-themes.js, call.js, cb.css, love.css, netlify/cb/lib.mjs, tools/make-profile.py, tools/check-contrast.py, cb-guide.html, liner-notes.html.

2026-10-18 — A Halloween theme, frame and decoration, to pair with the Lagoon

  • Black Leather Lagoon is a radio theme now, in the street’s Lagoon’s own colours: black water, the screen’s bone, acid green and poster red, with the radio’s name in its dripping bill face. Along its trims, its dividers and down its sides there is a drive-in screen glowing over the water now and then, and reeds with cattails, and bats. It is a banner and a nameplate for profiles too. Every word in it clears 4.5 to 1, and like the room nothing in it flickers.
  • A Graveyard gate frame: iron railings with spear tips round all four sides, cobwebs in the corners, and a spider letting itself down from each top corner. A Jack-o’-lanterns decoration: a pumpkin vine round the avatar you wear, with lanterns whose carved faces glow, and two bats. Both are drawn in the card’s own theme, so in the Lagoon’s the pumpkins are its poster red and the light in them its acid green.
  • Together with the Black Leather Lagoon effect, the four make one Halloween profile, Ryan’s ask. Nobody real is drawn in any of them; the liner notes say whose world it is. Changed: call.js, cb-themes.js, cb.css, love.css, profile.js, netlify/cb/lib.mjs, tools/check-contrast.py, cb-guide.html, liner-notes.html.

2026-10-18 — The Lagoon effect rears up when a profile opens

  • The Black Leather Lagoon effect now plays across the whole profile when it opens, the way Discord’s do, and then once a minute while it is open. Black water rises across the foot of the profile, mist climbs off it, the sea serpent’s coils break the surface, its head rears up over the profile and looks about with a flick of its tongue, and a flurry of bats bursts out past it. Then it all sinks back, after about nine seconds. The scene in the banner carries on the whole time. Ryan’s ask.
  • It crosses the words, so it is there only where drawings move, at MAX GLITTER or after Let my theme move, like every effect’s pass, and every fade in it takes a second or more, so nothing flashes. Changing something else in Edit my profile card no longer starts an effect over. The CB guide says so. Changed: profile.js, cb.css, cb.js, tools/make-profile.py, cb-guide.html.

2026-10-18 — Black Leather Lagoon, a profile effect

  • A detailed profile effect after the street’s Black Leather Lagoon and The Cramps, Ryan’s ask. Over the card’s banner, a drive-in screen stands in black water with a giant bloodshot eye looking about on it, its glow breathing slowly. A sea serpent of our own rises and sinks in front of it, with its eyes on stalks and pondweed on its head. There are reeds, a lobby card on a post in the shallows, and the screen rippling in the water. Poison ivy creeps down both sides of the profile, leopard print runs along the foot, a finned hot rod is parked in the corner, and from time to time bats fly across the whole profile from the screen’s side.

The card carries a credit naming The Cramps, Lux Interior and Poison Ivy, and the poison ivy is a nod to her name. It is the one effect drawn in its room’s own colours rather than the card’s theme, and, like the room, nothing in it flickers. 2026-10-18 — A profile lists the patches on its jacket

  • The Battle jacket tab in somebody’s profile lists every patch sewn on, under the jacket, in the street’s order: each patch drawn on its own, its name, and the room it came from, linked to where it lies, so you can go and find one. A screen reader hears what each patch looks like. Ryan’s ask. Nothing is counted.

2026-10-18 — Frames and effects for profiles on the CB

  • A profile card can have a frame and an effect now, after Discord’s. A frame is a band of your card’s theme round all four sides of your profile, outside it: briar, ivy, brass and rivets, fairy lights, a stitched patch, a film strip, a circuit board, crystals, a flower garland or a starry night. An effect is drawn over your profile: butterflies, fireflies, falling petals or leaves, snow, bubbles, sparkles, shooting stars, music notes or hearts. Part of it stays along the edges and in the banner, and the rest crosses the whole profile from time to time. Both are in Edit my profile card, and both are drawn only in your card’s own colours.
  • An effect’s pass crosses the words, Ryan’s call, which is the one place the radio lets a drawing do that. So it is drawn only where the radio’s drawings move, at MAX GLITTER or after Let my theme move: a profile standing still has nothing on its words, and nothing in an effect flashes. The CB guide and privacy say so. Changed: profile.js, cb.js, cb.css, netlify/cb/lib.mjs, tools/make-profile.py, cb-guide.html, community-center.html, privacy.html.

2026-10-18 — A moderator can clear one profile card

  • The moderators’ desk can clear somebody’s profile card without deleting their username. Until now a card that broke our covenant could only go with the whole username, its password, pets, avatars, jobs and patches. Looking a username up at the desk now says whether it has a card, and Clear their profile card, which asks once more, takes the card and its nameplate on Who’s online exactly as Clear my card does. Everything else stays, and they can fill in a new one. Ryan’s call.
  • Nothing is kept about it: not who cleared a card, not when, not what it said, and the accounts checker refuses one that would. The desk still leaves a moderator’s username alone, card included. Privacy, the CB guide and the Moderators’ room say so. Changed: desk.js, netlify/cb/lib.mjs, netlify/functions/cb-account-admin.mjs, tools/make-accounts.py, privacy.html, cb-guide.html, town-hall-moderators.html.

2026-10-18 — Profile cards on the CB

  • Pressing a handle opens a whole profile now, over the page, after requests for profiles more like Discord’s. On the left is the person’s card; on the right, where Discord has Board, Activity and Wishlist, are tabs for their Avatars, Pets, Badges and jobs and Battle jacket, each shown as they have chosen. A name on Who’s online opens the same profile. The CB guide says how it works.
  • A claimed username can fill in its card, at Edit my profile card in Profile, with the card drawn beside the choices as they change: a theme from the radio’s own, a banner from their drawings, a decoration round the avatar you wear (flowers, ivy, stars, gears, flames, moons and more, drawn in the card’s own colours), a face for your name from the street’s typefaces, a nameplate under your name on Who’s online, your pronouns, a few words about you, up to five links elsewhere, each shown with its site after it, and your favourite room on the street, with a line on why.
  • Ryan’s calls: whatever you fill in is shown to anybody signed on to the CB, as the avatar you wear is, with no switch; and no card says when anybody joined, because a date beside a name invites ranking who has been here longest. Every colour on a card is a pair the contrast checker already holds for every theme, and every drawing on it moves only where the radio’s do. Clear my card, Forget me and deleting the username delete it. Privacy has the whole of what is kept. 2026-10-17 — Small is one press away on the CB

2026-10-17 — The CB’s worlds go down the sides and along the dividers

2026-10-17 — Colour schemes for the CB

  • The radio comes in colour schemes now, from a request: Colours, under Your radio on the CB, offers Amber, which it has always been, Daylight, dark on a pale case, High contrast, black, white and yellow, and three faceplates, Green screen, Red readout and Teal glow. Each shows a little picture of itself, and the call window wears the same. Your radio remembers which, like its size.

2026-10-17 — Follow puts a rack’s film on the screen

  • On a rack, Enter now puts the film up on the room’s screen. Follow, Catch up and a spot from the CB said to press Enter, and Enter played the film small in its card under the screen, which was confusing, at Ryan’s report. Now the keyboard goes on the card’s button for the screen, and the film starts on the screen from where the host is. Anything that is not on a rack is as it was.
  • A film plays in one place at a time. Putting a card’s film up on the screen stops it in its card, and playing it in its card takes it off the screen, so the same film no longer plays twice, out of step. Another card playing in its own place is left alone. The CB guide says so. Changed: cb.js, rack.js.

2026-10-17 — Watching a live video together on the CB

  • Add my spot and Host this film now come up for a live video too, at Ryan’s ask, so the channel can be told when one is being screened. Until now Watching together left live videos out, because a time means nothing in one.
  • A live video’s spot is @live: Add my spot writes @live in “the video” at #the-room, and pressing @live gets that video’s play button ready on your page, or says it is already playing. It presses nothing, and the room’s link in the message gets it ready when you arrive.
  • Hosting one says it is live instead of giving a place. Beside the host, Catch up gets the video’s play button ready, and there is no Follow, because there is nothing in a live video to keep in step. A moderator’s live video put up from Screen a video says on everybody else’s screen that it runs until you stop it. The CB guide and the privacy page say so. Changed: love-embed.js, cb.js, netlify/cb/lib.mjs and its tests, cb-beacon.mjs, and the sets in hermitage.js, looming.js and rave.js.

2026-10-17 — Quieter messages on the CB

  • The words Copy, React and Take off are off every message on the CB, from a proposal Ryan brought: a row of buttons on every message was a lot to look at. At the end of each message’s line there are two small icons now, a smiley for React and three dots for More, which opens Copy and Take off under the line.
  • They show while your pointer is on the message or the keyboard is in it, and always on a phone or tablet, which has nothing to point with. Out of sight they are still in their place, so Tab reaches them, a screen reader names them, and nothing on the line moves when they come and go. The CB guide says so. Changed: cb.js, cb.css.

2026-10-18 — The Emergent Future Worlds Meets Mountain Man, David’s cabin at the summit over the campgrounds

  • A new home on the campgrounds, and the first off the main campsite: The Emergent Future Worlds Meets Mountain Man, David’s, a log cabin at the top of the mountain that stands over the field, from his brief as Helen passed it on, by the name she gave; he is David Gray-Hammond, he/they, and his own photograph, with the Diversity Power List honours in front of the NeuroHub wall, is framed in Whose this is with its record. Inside, at night: a fire behind the glass of the stove, with the coffee pot on top and a shelf of cups; LED strips along the walls in a colour you pick; a corner sofa, a high-back sofa and two high-back armchairs round a table with the crisps, the sweets and a bowl of rainbow sweets, bright cushions on every seat and bright bean bags on the floor; a big screen with Future Mountain Man, his own playlist, put on whole in his order and every video on it one press each under it; a speaker either side; an oil diffuser; one wolf asleep on the rug. Through the window, the whole back wall: a lake under a full moon, the pack on the warm ground outside, and the hot spring’s steam.
  • One switch runs the cabin: the fire, the strips, the diffuser, the big screen’s plate and the speakers go on and off together, saying so in words, remembering nothing. The cabin is drawn on and still; from the switch’s press the fire burns and the wisps rise, at Regular and MAX, and at Gentle they stay lit and still. Next colour steps the strips through the cabin’s colours and then the whole rainbow; at MAX a second button lets them drift round it, slowly, until you stop them, and nothing flashes. Change the scent steps the diffuser through its shelf of oils, lavender to cupcakes, saying which is in and never what it does. Hear the wolves howl makes the pack howl for about five seconds, out of the browser’s own oscillators and no recording; beside it, at Helen’s ask, a recording of real wolves from Wikimedia Commons, made by the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service and in the public domain, played from Commons by its own address when pressed and never hosted here. The cushions wear whichever colour the strips are, and change with them, Helen’s ask too. The window opens onto David’s own photographs of Lake Geneva, taken at Lausanne in July 2026 and sent by Helen on his behalf the same day: nobody in them, described in our words, each with its record in the polaroid file and its file stripped of metadata, to scroll through one at a time.
  • It runs on the mountain’s own heat. A hot spring steams in a ring of stones outside the door, with a shelf of rock to sit on and a step a wheelchair transfers from; the spring’s water runs under the floor and a small geothermal plant behind the cabin makes the electricity. Ryan’s Solarpunk Hermitage runs on the sun; this runs on the ground. Beside the spring’s words, two photographs of springs like it, Fabio Sasso’s and Jeremy Mowery’s on Unsplash, chosen by Helen, credited and linked; the second has three people far off on a boardwalk, strangers to the street, and the caption says so, Helen’s call. A cable car goes up from the pond, level with the platform at both ends so a wheelchair rolls straight on: it is how Ryan, Helen and Jess visit. It hangs on its wire down the left-hand edge of the page, at Helen’s ask: Send the cable car runs it up and down the wire and stops it, it stays still until sent and at Gentle, and pressing the car takes you back down to the campsite. On the campgrounds’ board its sign stands first, and its light is the sixth: one lit window so far above the field it sits among the stars. On the map the mountain is one card folded to a point over the field. The neighbours line in every home on the campgrounds names it, and its own names them.
  • tools/make-mountain-cabin.py writes the screen, the rack, the wall, the window and the credits from data/mountain-cabin.json, requires a runtime on every video and refuses one on the playlist, refuses a photograph with no record, holds mountain-man.js to storing nothing, sending nothing and howling no louder than a ceiling, and sweeps the room’s voice for a cure, a conquest and a wolf drawn as a threat. A job marker (a carabiner), a share card, a fractal in the room’s own colours, Now Playing, the liner notes. Staatliches and Assistant arrive on the street. love.css: section 118 is the room; small screens and print move to 119 and 120, with every reference.

2026-10-17 — The Bough House, Jess’s tree house at pitch 05, and the pond between it and the Faery Yurt

  • A new pitch on the campgrounds, next to the Faery Yurt: the Bough House, Jess’s, from her brief as Helen passed it on. 2026-10-16 — The yurt’s fire glows, and you can get more logs

  • The stove in the Faery Yurt glows now, at Helen’s ask: a warm light round it from the start, which spreads and brightens with every log you pop on the fire. It changes only when somebody presses, and nothing flickers or flashes.
  • A Get more logs button fills the basket again whenever you like, and the fire keeps burning as it was. Pop a log on with an empty basket and the stove says to get more.

2026-10-16 — Pop a log on the fire in the yurt

  • The logs in the Faery Yurt’s basket go on the fire now, at Helen’s ask: press Pop a log on the fire under the stove, or press a log in the basket, and it lifts across into the stove’s window and the fire burns brighter, its flame taller, its heart lit and its glow warm on the floor. When the basket is empty the button fills it again and the fire settles back down.
  • The basket says it in Helen’s words: pop one on the fire and warm yourself up! What happened is said in words under the button at every setting of the dial; at Gentle a log moves straight to the fire rather than flying there. Nothing flashes, nothing is counted, and nothing is kept, so the basket is full again next time.

2026-10-16 — The Moon Spoon Pebbling Pot in the yurt

  • Through the window plays Moon Spoon, Vi Hart’s video, from her own Vimeo, 6:30, credited to her on the window and in the liner notes. The moon spoon is Vi Hart’s idea. Nothing plays until you press, and the player is asked not to track the visit. Vimeo is a new service on the street, so the security header and the privacy page name it.
  • Helen’s own photographs lie on the sill: her moon spoon with flowers she gathered in the local woods, the flowers on the table and in a basket, and her faery garden with a spoon hidden in the undergrowth. Each is recorded with the others, with no camera data kept and nothing done to the picture.
  • And the log basket says it in Helen’s words now: free to take if you want, all yours! Enjoy!

2026-10-16 — A wood stove and a log basket in the yurt, and a hollow stump by the door

  • The basket to take a pebble from is a log basket now, at Helen’s ask, beside a little wood stove with its flue up through the crown, a fire in its window and a kettle on top, the way a yurt is really heated. It takes the place of the picnic hamper. The stove glows the candles’ own gold, so nothing in her room changes colour, and nothing in it moves.

There are toadstools and ivy round the stove and the log basket too, in a toadstool red that is new to her room, at her ask.

2026-10-16 — Fantasy Fancy Dress, through the curtain at the back of the Dress-Up Den

  • A new shop behind the Dopamine Dress-Up Den, at Helen’s ask, with her name on the sign: Fantasy Fancy Dress. Faery wings from dragonfly to great faery wings, cat, fox, bunny, bear and mouse ears, tails, paws, bead necklaces, chunky beads, a pendant and a velvet choker, rings, bead bracelets and bangles, a onesie, and tiger, bear, dragon and unicorn suits. Bring your bodymind through from the Dress-Up Den and it is drawn there wearing whatever you pick; with nobody brought through, the shop draws somebody at random, a different somebody each time, so no body is the one it starts from. Take it back through and the Dress-Up Den keeps it as an avatar when you press Keep, wings and all. Nothing in the shop is kept or sent.

They are drawn in the same file as every avatar on the street, on its feet or sitting down: wings fold flat for sitting back in a chair, a tail lies to one side, and short tails sit at the side of the seat where you can see them. 2026-10-15 — The About page answers the questions people ask

  • Questions people ask, in Ryan Boren’s answers: what Stimpunks is and where the street fits in, how it started, why it looks like the old web and what AI makes possible, what the technology does for lived experience, what we hope and worry about with AI, and where to start.

2026-10-15 — What’s this all about? A page for anybody who does not get it yet

  • The street has an About page, for anybody who has been shown it and says they do not get it: what it is, what to do here, why a street, and a thirty-second film. The front page links it under the tagline, and so does the line at the foot of every page.

Its doors are the front page’s own, in their rooms’ own colours and lettered in their rooms’ own typefaces, from this site’s font files. It is silent, it plays only when you press play, and everything it shows is written out underneath it. It is the first film this site serves itself rather than from somebody else’s channel. How it’s made says so, and the liner notes credit every face in it.

2026-10-16 — The Music Room, behind the yurt, for anybody who only wants to listen

  • A door beside the phonograph in the Faery Yurt opens the Music Room, at Helen’s ask, for those who just want to listen and not scroll: the phonograph with her whole playlist on it and every song on the yarn as a record in an arched case, in a room with nothing else in it. The yurt keeps its own phonograph; the music is in both rooms, written into each by the one tool from the one data file, so they cannot say different things. Join the faeries and Grow a garden are in there too.

The record turns at MAX and nowhere else, as it does in the yurt.

The order and the colours of the spines are unchanged.

2026-10-15 — The yurt’s bookshelf becomes eight arched bookcases, with an art gallery under them

Deleuze and Guattari have a spine each, and Alice in Wonderland is shelved under Lewis Carroll, so every spine names an author.

Inside each, the books go alphabetically by surname. Cases standing side by side are the same height, so a shorter one has empty shelves waiting for what comes back from being lent out.

Every one is credited in the liner notes, with the painter, title and year read off the scan’s own record. The artists she named whose work is still their own — Goldsworthy, Rothko, Hepworth, Pollock, Flavin, Judd, Fontana, Knoebel, Morellet, Reinhardt — are spines on the Art shelf that open their Wikipedia pages, and nothing of theirs is shown.

2026-10-15 — The guest bar’s teleporter goes where you pick again

  • Picking a room in the teleporter on the guest bar did nothing, whether you clicked it or pressed Enter, ever since the bar learned to sign you on. The Sign on button and the teleporter’s way of going somewhere had the same name in guest.js, and the button won. It has a name of its own now, and both work. The radio’s teleporter, for anybody signed on, was never affected.

2026-10-15 — Every room in the Faery Yurt has its own address

Nothing on the page looks any different.

2026-10-15 — The yurt’s faeries come in three sizes and never fly over the words

  • Three sizes of faery, at Helen’s ask: little ones, ones three times as big, and the biggest four times as big.
  • No faery ever flies over a word or a picture, at her ask. They live on the page now rather than on the window, so the words never slide under them as you scroll; each one only goes where there is room for all of it, and flies round the words from one open space to the next, so the big ones keep to the margins and the little ones slip through the gaps between the sections. A petal or a star that drifts onto a word is gone at once.

2026-10-15 — Bright red and rainbow hair at the Bodymind Booth

  • Two new hair colours, at Helen’s ask: bright red, and rainbow, which runs red, orange, yellow, green, blue and violet from the roots down, on every hairstyle, long or short. A bodymind wearing either walks into the Dopamine Dress-Up Den with it, and keeps it as a CB avatar.

2026-10-15 — Woodland faeries in the yurt, and a new portrait by the door

  • The faeries are small woodland faeries now, at Helen’s ask: each a tiny figure with a flower crown, long hair, a mossy cloak to the ground, a posy held in front and gauzy wings, and some with little curled horns. Their skin, hair and cloaks vary so no two are dressed alike. They are drawn here, not taken from anybody’s artwork. They float more slowly, and each lets fall petals that drift down, sway and fade over three seconds; nothing blinks. At MAX GLITTER they still glow brighter and leave stars.
  • A new portrait of Helen beside the welcome, sent by her today: Helen in a sunlit wood. Her description of the old one still fits it, so it carries those words until she changes them. The earlier photograph stays on Enid’s wall. Not cropped, not filtered, and re-saved with no metadata.

2026-10-14 — The yurt’s garden grows round the words, and the faeries come in colours and leave stars

  • Grow a garden grows on the page now, at Helen’s ask: tangled ivy along the top of the page and along the foot, strands hanging down and climbing up, ivy down the margins either side, small flowers in every colour (yellows, blues, reds, pinks, lilacs, oranges and creams), red and brown toadstools growing in the ivy, and mushrooms coming up out of the bottom. It grows along the green sprigs too, and across the open space between the sections. Most of it has grown in about twenty seconds, and then for about two minutes more it keeps slowly adding more ivy, leaves, flowers and mushrooms. It never covers a word or a picture: it measures where they are and grows round them, and if the page changes shape it grows round the new shape.
  • The faeries are all different, at her ask: every one a different size and a different muted colour, dusty rose, sage, lavender, slate blue, old gold, mauve. At MAX GLITTER they are much brighter and leave a trail of stars, each fading out over a second and a half, so nothing blinks or flashes.
  • The nook’s coffee is coffee and tea: the tile reads a standing supply of coffee and tea, and Ryan’s “Ahhhh.” is still behind it.

2026-10-15 — The Live Room gets the glass, and the studios’ own month under the desk

  • The Live Room’s desk is a rack now. Every strip still plays where it hangs, and a second button puts it up on the glass over the desk instead, saying how long it runs before you press it. Taking it off puts the glass back as it was and plays nothing, and each studio’s desk folds away.
  • Under the desk, the last month off each studio’s own channel: Austin City Limits, KEXP and KCRW, their videos and their shorts, newest first, refilled every morning. Nobody chose these or watched them first, so there is no tape on them. Nobody has checked where each was played either: the rule that a session was played indoors is still the desk’s, and the room says so beside the month.
  • KEXP puts up each session whole and then every song from it on its own, more in three days than YouTube’s feed for a channel carries. So the new puller reads a channel’s own list page, past its first hundred, whenever the feed does not reach back to what an earlier morning covered, rather than printing a gap for a month while the mornings fill it in. It met YouTube’s new shape for an upload list’s rows the same night, and reads both.

2026-10-14 — Plath in the Zibaldone, as she printed it

  • Sylvia Plath’s line from The Bell Jar is in the Zibaldone, sent in by Betsy and read by Ryan in a reissue made from Faber and Faber’s 1966 edition. It came in joined by a colon. In the book the sentence ends at heart, and I am, I am, I am stands as a paragraph of its own, so the leaf sets them on two lines. The reissue is public domain in Canada and not everywhere, and Plath is still in copyright under the room’s test, so the line is quoted short and cited, and the leaf links the Faber edition rather than the reissue.

2026-10-14 — Your Room says what to do, how to send it, and what happens next

  • People told us they read Your Room and did not know what they were supposed to do next. It says so near the top now: build it with us or build it with your AI, then send it to us.
  • How to ask is Build it with us, and it lists the questions to answer: the same questions the prompt has your AI ask you. They are read out of the prompt every time the street is built, so the two can never ask different things.
  • How to submit your room says where to send it whichever way you made it, what to send, and what to leave in. What happens next says what we do with it, how soon we write back, what decides how long the rest takes, and how to change the room later.
  • The line comparing you to Enid is gone. It only made sense if you already knew who Enid is.
  • Changed: your-room.html, data/community.json and the service board in community-center.html, a new tools/make-your-room.py in tools/check-all.sh, README.md.

2026-10-14 — Patches for your battle jacket

  • Patches lie about the street now, and some are on a counter in a shop, free to take. Signed on to the CB, pick one up and it is sewn on your battle jacket, a denim jacket in Profile on the radio. Every patch is its own size, shape and design, drawn in its own room’s colours and lettered in its own room’s typeface, and all of them sew onto the same jacket.
  • The first ones: Stimpunks.World on the pavement, Pink Pony Peep on the dancefloor, Interdependence in the Town Hall, Kindness Is Punk on Club Chronic’s distro table, Plant-Powered on Vital Plant Living’s counter, Intermittently Social Lone Wolf in the Cave, and Synapse of the Soil at the end of Mycelium Munchies’ pass. Where the patches are, under your jacket in Profile, lists them all.
  • Show my battle jacket on the CB, with a claimed username, shows it in your profile, and Sticker on the radio sends a patch to the channel. The street keeps which patches are on your jacket and nothing about when, nothing counts who else has one, and Forget me takes them all off.

2026-10-14 — Rescue Ranger and Pet Parent, the Guild’s first badges that are not jobs

  • Rescue Ranger is for anybody who rescues an animal at one of the shelters, and Pet Parent for anybody who adopts one. Like every badge, each is worked out from what you have already done, and kept nowhere.
  • Rescue Ranger is only ever yours to see. The shelters promise that nothing on the street knows who rescued which animal, and the only list of the animals you rescued is in your own browser. So the badge is shown on the Guild’s board and in your own Profile, and never on your profile for anybody else.
  • Pet Parent comes from the pets in your Profile, so the board shows it while you are signed on to the CB, and other people see it in your profile only if you show your pets.

2026-10-14 — The Guild’s board filters, keeps your jobs in your Profile, and gives out badges

  • The jobs can be filtered: every job, the ones you have not handed in, or the ones you have. The filter hides rows and never says how many.
  • Signed on to the CB, the board can keep your jobs in your Profile. Keep my jobs in my Profile, beside the hand-in box, starts off. Switched on, it keeps the jobs this browser has stamped and every one you hand in after, so they follow you to any device you sign on with. It keeps which jobs, and nothing about when. Profile on the radio lists them, and with a claimed username Show my jobs on the CB shows them in your profile, the way pets and avatars are shown. Switching keeping off deletes them, and so do Forget me and deleting your username. Privacy has it all.
  • Badges, because people asked, after merit badges, Open Badges and video game achievements. A badge is a set of jobs that go together: the rooms down the lift, the tables on the street, the toys. It is worked out from the jobs you have handed in every time, and kept nowhere. Nothing counts them, none is rare or secret, none ever asks for more than it did when it was posted, and none certifies anything. Each one has its own drawing, stamped in the guild’s vermilion when it is yours.
  • This reverses part of one of the board’s own rules, on Ryan’s call. The tool that builds the board used to refuse the word badge on it. It still refuses it in a job’s own words, and it refuses a badge that is ranked, rare, unlocked, counted or certifies anything. No badge sends anybody to a room for resting in, because on this street rest is owed rather than earned, and a badge is earned.

2026-10-14 — The Faery Yurt: a field book, a picnic hamper, a turret window, faeries and a garden

  • Nature Notes is an old field book now, at Helen’s ask. Shut, it is bound in leather with its name in gold; press it and it opens onto ruled cream pages, each photograph mounted whole with photo corners and a note under it in her hand. The notes are Helen’s own words from the line above the book, on the pictures they fit; the other pages are ruled and waiting. Close the book shuts it again, and with scripts off the book is simply open.
  • The basket by the door is a picnic hamper, at Helen’s ask: pale woven willow, a gingham lining in her ivy, the lid propped open behind, and leather straps and brass buckles down the front. The pebbles sit in the lining, and nothing about taking one has changed.
  • Join the faeries, and grow a garden, two switches under the dial, at Helen’s ask. One sends a few small faeries drifting across the page; the other grows ivy down both edges of the window and toadstools in the bottom corners, very slowly. Each stays still until somebody presses it, at every setting, and pressing again clears it. Nothing flashes and nothing is remembered. The dial’s small print in the yurt now says nothing else moves unless you ask it to, where it used to say nothing else ever moves.
  • The glimmer window is a castle turret’s window, at Helen’s ask: a round arch in stone, and shutters of oak planks with iron strap hinges and studs that close into one arched door. Her photographs open behind them as before, each kept clear of the arch so none is cut.

2026-10-14 — Beauvoir in the Zibaldone, read in the book

  • The first line in the Zibaldone to come in by the new way is Simone de Beauvoir’s, from The Second Sex, sent in by Betsy. Ryan read it on page 273 of H. M. Parshley’s English, published by Jonathan Cape, where it opens the chapter called Childhood, and it came in exactly as printed. The leaf names Parshley as well as Beauvoir, because a translation is somebody’s work too, and it says the title page gives no year.

2026-10-14 — The Zibaldone takes its first lines from somebody else’s hand

  • Betsy sent lines in for the Zibaldone, and two are in the book, with her name on their citations: Louisa May Alcott’s storms, from Little Women, and a sentence of Whitman’s from Song of Myself. Both were read in the printings their cards name. Alcott wrote I’m where it came in as I am, and the line is Amy’s. Whitman’s line ends in a comma and the sentence goes on for two more lines, so the leaf carries all three.
  • This changes one of the room’s rules, on Ryan’s call. Until today a line still in copyright could only come in if one of our own pages already quoted it, which meant the room could never take one from anybody who sent it. Now it can also come in once a person has read it in the book itself, and its card says who read it and on which page. The checking did not get any looser: a line copied off a quotation site still counts as unread, and tools/make-zibaldone.py refuses a line with no record of who read it, or where.
  • The rest of what Betsy sent is waiting to be read in the book, and nothing goes on the page until somebody has. One of those lines is usually credited to a different writer from the one it came in under, which is exactly what reading it in the book is for.

2026-10-14 — Avatars in profiles, large, and in the lightbox

  • A profile on the CB shows the avatar its person wears, first and large enough to see its details, when you press their name on Who’s online or their handle on the channel. Press it to open it in the lightbox, as large as the window allows.
  • The rest of somebody’s avatars show only if they say so. Show my avatars on the CB, in Profile, is off until you switch it on and needs a claimed username, the same as showing your pets. The privacy page says what it keeps.

2026-10-14 — The Faery Yurt’s residents open onto their photographs

  • Every card under Who Else Lives Here in the Faery Yurt opens now, at Helen Edgar’s ask: the cats onto Cats Coven, every photograph of Harry, Zara, Ziggy and Tiggy together; Benji onto the Dog’s Den; and Gibbles onto the Hamster Haven. Press a card to open it and again to shut it; nothing loads until it is open.

2026-10-14 — Nature Notes is a notebook now

  • In the Faery Yurt, Helen’s Nature Notes became a notebook, at her ask: a bound cover in the yurt’s wood, cream pages, and one photograph to a page, shown whole. Swipe the pages, or press Turn the page and Turn back; a screen reader hears each photograph’s description as its page turns.
  • A shadow sweeps across the page as it turns, only when you press, and not at all at Gentle, where the page simply changes. The notebook stores nothing and never turns a page by itself.

2026-10-14 — The Bodymind Booth, and avatars on the CB

  • The Bodymind Booth is open, next door to the Dopamine Dress-Up Den: a photo booth with no camera in it, where you draw your bodymind. How you get about, on your feet, in a wheelchair or in a power chair; your height, shoulders, middle and hips; your skin, numbered rather than named, with four colours nobody is born in; your face and your hair; and everything you always have with you, from glasses, hearing aids, cochlear implants, ear defenders and masks to canes, crutches, walkers, AAC devices, fidgets, chew necklaces and prosthetics. No body comes first: the booth opens on somebody drawn at random. The idea came from our community, from somebody who hoped for characters to dress one day.
  • Take it next door and dress it. The Dress-Up Den now asks who is wearing it, the stand or your bodymind, and every garment on its rails is drawn again to fit whoever puts it on, standing or sitting.
  • Avatars, kept in Profile on the CB. Keep a bodymind, or an outfit on the stand, as an avatar; with a claimed username, wear one at a time beside your name on Who’s online and change which whenever you like; and send any of them as a sticker. A screen reader hears an avatar as what its owner wrote about it and nothing else.
  • Two things written down here before have changed, both Ryan’s calls. The Dress-Up Den had recorded any drawing of a body as not open; the Den still draws none of its own, and the bodies are drawn in a booth of their own, where nobody is handed one. And pets were the one thing the street kept under a person; avatars are the second, deleted by Forget me and by deleting your username, and never a picture: an avatar is a list of choices.

2026-10-13 — The Sandbox, its suggestion box, and building the street with us

  • The Sandbox is open, a shopfront on the street: a room thought up by somebody in our community and built with Claude, an AI made by Anthropic, and brought onto the street from the page they made. A laptop left open in the sand at dusk, its screen the only light in the playground: come in curious, try things, break things and tell us what you notice, with no deadlines and nothing to lose. There is a sandcastle you can build and burn down, and the ball bounces. Its credit is in the liner notes, with the other rooms people built, and Rubik Dirt, by NaN and Luke Prowse, is a new face on the street, for the sign on its box.
  • Its suggestion box works. Anybody signed on to the CB can drop in a suggestion, a feature request or a bug report, about the room or anywhere on the street, with their handle on it. Anybody can read what is in the box, and our moderators answer each one, done or not doing, always with a reply and their own username on it. It stays until whoever dropped it in or a moderator takes it down, and nothing in it is counted, ranked or voted on. Privacy says what the box keeps, and the community service board has a slot for it.
  • And you can build the street with us. Under the box are the steps: make your own copy of the code, check your computer with tools/setup.sh, hook up Claude, see the street on your own computer, make a change, check everything, and send it back as a pull request. The same steps are CONTRIBUTING.md in the repository, written from the same place so the two cannot disagree.
  • We made two small changes to the room’s page, so the street has somewhere to put its box and its steps: a space round its own link to us, which is still there with scripts off, and a space after its box. Everything else in the room is its maker’s, as they made it.

2026-10-13 — A call that follows you: Pop out

  • Pop out, on a call’s window, moves the call into a browser window of its own, which stays open while you go from room to room in the window you came from. It works for the World call and for any room’s call, signed on to the CB. You join again in the new window, because a call cannot be carried from one window to another without starting over.
  • The radio knows where the call is on every page: the call’s own button stays pressed, Show the call window brings the window to the front, and pressing the call’s button again hangs up and closes it. There is still one call at a time. The CB guide has it, and Privacy says what the window sends and keeps, which is nothing new.

2026-10-13 — The World call

  • A call for the whole street: Join World call, under Who’s around on the radio, opens one call that is no room’s, from any page, for anybody signed on to the CB. It opens in the same window as a room’s call, and you are in one call at a time. The CB guide has it.
  • Like every call, it hangs up when you leave the page, and going to another room is leaving the page, because every room is a page of its own. The radio says so when it opens.
  • Who’s online names it in Calls going now while somebody is in it, and nobody is shown who is in it before they join. Privacy says so.

2026-10-13 — The CB in parts, where you are, and calls on Who’s online

  • The radio is in parts now, each under a heading: Where you are, Who’s around, Channel, Watching together and Your radio, with room and a groove in the case between them. This came out of people trying the CB: it had grown one button at a time into one long column, and it was hard to tell where one part ended. The headings are real ones, so a screen reader can move between them. Small is unchanged. The CB guide has the parts in order.
  • The radio says which room you are in, at the top, in its largest words, because some people could not tell where they were. Tuned to World, you hear the whole street, and the radio now says where you are standing as well as what you are hearing.

Who is in a call is still shown only in its room, to people seen there. Privacy says so, and a room the base keeps or one that keeps to itself is not named.
  • Join this room’s call is under Where you are now, and Add my spot, Host this film and Screen a video are under Watching together, which is only there while one of them is.

2026-10-12 — More sticker sheets

  • The sticker drawer has more sheets: the days of the month, 1 to 31; the weeks of the year, 1 to 53; the words Week, Day, Month, Quarter and Year; this year’s number and next year’s, read off your own clock so they turn over on 1 January by themselves; the weather; and the moon.
  • The weather and the moon are drawn here: cloudy, partly sunny, rainy, stormy, snowy, windy, sunny, a tornado and flooding, and the moon’s eight phases on round stickers, as they look from the northern hemisphere. Pictures are never turned on their side to fit more, because a sideways moon is a different phase.
  • Every number is the same width, so a sheet of them reads as a grid, and a sheet of a set reads a set at a time: a moon sheet goes round the cycle, and a sheet of dates runs 1 to 31 and starts again.
  • The first sheets went through the printer and cut cleanly, so the spacing stays as it is. One thing changed for sheets already printed: a word’s size is now worked out across every word, number and year the drawer can print, so a Monday and a 12 in one face match. In most faces nothing moved; in a few display faces a day or month comes out a little smaller than it did.

2026-10-12 — Sharing a sticker sheet

  • The sticker drawer can share a sheet now, wherever your browser can share a file. Share hands it to your device’s own share sheet, where Save Image puts it straight into a phone’s photos, which is where the Liene Photo app picks from. Save still puts it in your downloads.
  • The site’s security headers were loosened by one word to do it: the share sheet was switched off for every page, and is now allowed to this site’s own pages, with the sticker drawer the only script that may open it. It sends nothing to us, and opens only when you press Share.

2026-10-12 — The Open Notebook’s sticker drawer

  • The Open Notebook has a sticker drawer: the days of the week and the months of the year, by name, on sheets for the PixCut S1, Liene’s photo sticker printer and cutter. Any typeface on the street, in three sizes, and the week or the year on one sheet, or a day or a month to a sheet.
  • As many stickers to a sheet as will go. The week’s and the year’s sheets take as many whole weeks or years as fit, then whatever more will go in the gaps, turning some on their side where that fits more, and every sheet says underneath it what is on it. Where a whole year will not fit at Large, the sheet says which size it fits at.
  • A sheet is a PNG the size of the paper at 300 dpi, clear everywhere but the stickers, so the Liene Photo app can cut round each one. It is drawn in your browser and saved to your own device, and carries the face’s designer and licence inside it.
  • A rule of the room was narrowed for it. The Open Notebook refused any list to choose from and any script of its own, so that there would be nowhere to write. The drawer’s face picker is one list, and it has one script; neither takes a word, keeps anything or sends anything, and the room still has nowhere to write.

2026-10-11 — Wide-Spread Chaos Gardens

  • Wide-Spread Chaos Gardens is open, east of the stile in Nature’s Contribution: food and flowers from a mix of seed thrown into the wind, on a clear windy day with everything in the garden leaning the same way. The drawing is thrown too, out of a fixed seed, so it is the same garden every time.
  • What chaos gardening is, and what it is not, in the words of a landscape specialist at Colorado State University Extension: a garden, not a restoration. The mix is half things to eat and half flowers, and the four noxious weeds Colorado State University Extension says to keep out of a seed mix are named and never in it.
  • It gives food to the café in the old dairy and the honesty box, flowers and seed, and a bench in the middle with no paths to keep to. The screen in the seed shed has films about chaos gardens, meadows from seed and plants that sow themselves.

2026-10-11 — The Honesty Box

  • The Honesty Box is open on the street, a farm stand at the kerb lit by its own solar lamp, with the wool, hemp rope and cloth, pasteurised milk and cheese, eggs, meat and produce of the land past the campgrounds. Prices on the board, money in the box, and nobody watching.
  • It is the one stall on the street with prices, because selling the land’s goods is the land’s half of the bargain with the street. Every item says what it costs and which place it came from, and the board is checked against what each place sends here.
  • Never in the box: cannabis, which leaves the land through licensed dispensaries only, because a box nobody watches cannot ask anybody’s age; raw milk; and anything from the sanctuary.

2026-10-11 — The Dairy

  • The Dairy is open, behind All Together Now Farms: the farm’s old dairy is a café now, white glazed tile to the ceiling with the light going round and round it, and the pasteuriser still on the counter.
  • Everything on the menu was grown on the land, and every dish says which places grew it, checked against each place’s own page; the salt and the baking soda are bought in, and it says so. Allergens are worked out from what is in each dish by Hey, Good Cookin’s engine, and each drink’s caffeine by Pekoe and Purrs’. Every milk and cheese is pasteurised.

2026-10-11 — All Together Now Farms

  • All Together Now Farms is open, north-east of the stile: a mixed farm where trees, crops and animals share the same fields, lit by the sun going down on one side and the full moon coming up on the other, so everything on it is warm on one side and silver on the other.
  • The five agroforestry practices in the USDA’s own words, and which the farm does: four of them, and the fifth says why not. The Three Sisters are planted in the alleys between the nut trees and credited to the nations that teach them, with their story left to them.
  • Sheep, goats and chickens, and the milk is pasteurised, with the CDC and Colorado’s law saying why. Everything the farm grows goes to the café in its old dairy and the honesty box on the street.
  • The screen in the barn has films about growing, grazing and the Three Sisters, and the last month of Farming While Beige, refilled every morning.

2026-10-11 — Gentle Help Cannabis Farm

  • Gentle Help Cannabis Farm is open, north-west of the stile: cannabis in flower in hoop houses that glow through their own skin, grown among basil, dill, alfalfa, marigolds and the other plants a grower’s guide says grow well beside it. What each companion is supposed to do is said as the guide’s, beside an extension service saying research mostly does not back it.
  • A table of strains, sativa, indica and hybrid, with Leafly’s lab-test figures for THC and CBD and the top terpenes. Leafly publishes no CBN, so the table says so. Under it, three papers saying the three words tell you little about what is in the jar, and that labels in Colorado read higher than the lab.
  • Harm reduction, from our Addiction and Harm Reduction entries and from public health: edibles, driving, mixing, lungs, vapes, concentrates, synthetics, CHS, storage, and Colorado’s law, each with where it was read. Nothing is sold at the farm, nobody is called a name, and nothing on the page promises what cannabis will do for anybody.
  • The screen in the potting shed has films about growing among companions, hemp to rope and cloth, and rosin.

2026-10-11 — Brighter Days Solar Farm

  • Brighter Days Solar Farm is open, north of the stile: panels on posts high enough to walk under, peppers, cherry tomatoes and basil in their shade, the farm’s sheep between the rows, and the power for the land and much of the street.
  • It is built on Star Stuff’s Two Peppers and the field experiment behind it, quoted and credited: two peppers answered the same shade in two different ways, and neither is the failure case.
  • The screen in the battery shed has films about crops, sheep and bees under solar panels, picked for the room and checked to play here.

2026-10-11 — All Needs Met

  • All Needs Met Wildlife Sanctuary is open, west of the stile in Nature’s Contribution: a wildlife preserve left to whatever was there first, and a home for good for animals who are disabled or were abandoned.
  • You see it on the night round, through the viewer the keepers carry instead of a torch, so nobody is woken: every resident white with their own warmth, the night black, and nothing in the room with a colour, because heat has none.
  • Eight residents, each with how they came, how they get about and what they like, and nobody called brave, sad or a lesson. They are this story’s animals, not real ones. The preserve’s wild neighbours have plain names, and nothing tells you how to find them.
  • The screen in the keepers’ hut has a month of films from the fourteen channels Ryan listed, refilled every morning, the oldest going off the end. Nobody here watched them first, and every one says how long it runs before you press.

2026-10-11 — Nature’s Contribution

  • Past the campgrounds and through its wood, there is land now: Nature’s Contribution, kept for wildlife, for food and for power at once, where every part feeds the others and the street. It is built on a paper Ryan brought, “Balancing land use for conservation, agriculture, and renewable energy” (Nature Communications, 2026), and quotes it under its CC BY licence: some of the land is shared between uses and some is left to wildlife outright, because the paper is careful about what sharing ground can do.
  • A fingerpost at the stile has an arm for every place: a wildlife sanctuary, a cannabis farm, a solar farm, a mixed farm with a café in its old dairy, and a chaos garden. The signs are up and the places open one by one; an arm with nothing behind it yet says so.
  • A table says what each place gives and where it goes, most of it to the street through an honesty box. Cannabis leaves only through licensed dispensaries, never the honesty box, and nothing leaves the sanctuary but its manure.
  • The light is an afternoon under broken cloud, and the shadows on the fields are the clouds’. You are standing in one at the stile. Nothing moves, at any setting.
  • The wood stays unmapped: the campgrounds still says so, and now says where the track through it comes out. The map has the land past the wood, and the Adventurer’s Guild has a job out there, still class III.

2026-10-10 — Her records play here now

  • Every record in Words, Bud, Beats has a TIDAL player on its sleeve, which Kristina Brooke asked for when she sent the room. It is press-to-play like everything else here: nothing reaches TIDAL until you press, pressing brings in TIDAL’s own player, and the song starts when you press play there. The links to each song are still there, for anybody who would rather hear it somewhere else.
  • Without a TIDAL account it plays a preview, and every button says so before you press it. The preview is not one length: a minute of one song and thirty seconds of two others, when we tried it, so no button puts a number on it. Each button gives the whole song’s length.
  • TIDAL is on the privacy page with the other services a press can load, including that its player also loads Datadog, which TIDAL uses to watch how the player is working, in about one visit in ten.
  • A room that comes in through Your Room can have TIDAL tracks as players now, and the prompt says how. The check that every video on the street still plays checks every TIDAL track too, and how long it runs.

2026-10-10 — Words, Bud, Beats

It is the second room to come in through Your Room. She designed it with Claude, following our prompt, and the words, the songs and the strains are hers.

2026-10-08 — Take off your own message

  • Anybody on the CB can take their own message off the air now, asked for by Ryan. Take off, which only the base station had, is on every message you sent, beside Copy and React. It takes the message off for everybody, with its picture and reactions, and it cannot be put back. The base station still has it on every message, and clearing the whole channel is still the base’s alone.
  • Yours means the handle on it, and two marks narrow that. A message marked CLAIMED comes off only for that username’s own password, so a name somebody else signs on as later cannot reach back to what was said under it, and one marked BASE only for the base. The server decides, every time; the radio only decides where to draw the button.
  • A moderator can only take things off a channel they can hear. Taking a message off never asked whether a moderator was let into a private room at the Town Hall; it asks now, like everything else that touches a room’s channel.
  • The keyboard is not dropped when a message goes. The button that had it goes with its message, so it moves to the message box instead of to nowhere. The privacy page, the house norms and the CB guide say so.

2026-10-07 — A second rack on the Green: the newest from the search

2026-10-07 — Calls can use your camera and microphone in Chrome

  • In Chrome, a room’s call could not switch on its camera, its microphone or a shared screen, and said you had refused them although nothing had ever asked you. That was this site’s fault, not 8x8’s and not yours: our security header gave those three to the call and deliberately not to our own pages, and a browser will only hand a frame what the page round it already has. So Chrome switched them off inside the call before it could ask.
  • The header now gives them to our pages as well, so that the call can have them, and Chrome asks you the first time a call wants them. Because the call sits inside our page, Chrome’s question names stimpunks.world. This reverses the rule written down when calls arrived, that the page itself gets none of the three. That rule could not work.
  • Our pages still never ask for any of the three themselves. The header used to keep that promise, and now the tool that writes the header does: it reads every script this site serves and refuses one that asks for a camera, a microphone or a screen. The privacy page’s sentence about it stays true, and Architecture now says how.

2026-10-07 — Who’s online, on the radio

  • A Who’s online button sits beside World and This room on the CB radio. It lists everybody who has switched on Be seen here, in alphabetical order, with the room each of them is in; a room’s name takes you there. Ryan asked for it because people want to know who is around and are having a hard time connecting. Anybody signed on can open it.
  • This changes what Be seen here means. It used to show you only to people seen in the same room. Now it also puts your handle and your room on Who’s online for everybody on the CB. If you switched it on before today, it does more than it did when you pressed it, and switching it off takes you off the list at once. The privacy page says so.
  • @ in Teleport finds people by part of their handle now, from the same list, and goes to their room. What you type after the @ stays in your browser. The Let people find me switch from earlier today is gone, because Who’s online does what it did.
  • CLAIMED sits beside a claimed username on Who’s online and in the room’s seen line, the way it does on the channel, so somebody signed on under a name that is not theirs cannot pass for its owner. Names come in alphabetical order, nobody is counted, rooms kept for the people they are for are not named to anybody else, and nothing records who looked.

2026-10-07 — Find somebody by name with an @ in the teleporter

  • Type @ and somebody’s handle into the radio’s Teleport, press Enter, and it takes you to the room they are in. Helen Edgar asked for a way to find where people are, and Ryan suggested the teleporter.
  • Nobody can be found until they choose to be. Be seen here only ever showed you to people in the same room, and that is what everybody who pressed it agreed to, so being found from anywhere is a second switch under it, Let people find me. It is off until you press it, your browser remembers it, and it only works while you are seen.
  • Only a claimed username can be found, because an unclaimed handle is anybody’s who signs on with it, and somebody could sign on with your name and have people sent to them. Only somebody who can be found can look: the same one switch, both ways, that Be seen here and the Slake keep, and the street checks it, not only the radio.
  • One whole handle, and never a list. Part of a handle finds nobody, and nothing offers names as you type, because a list of names to pick from would be a list of who is on. The answer is the room and nothing else; anybody who cannot be found, for any reason, is “nobody by that whole handle can be found right now”, and a room kept for the people it is for is never named to anybody else. Nothing records who looked for whom.
  • The privacy page has a new section, Being found by name, and the CB guide and the Community Center describe the switch. The guest bar and the front page’s search say where an @ works.

2026-10-06 — Rolls on the player piano at A Quiet Pint

  • The pub’s player piano has a shelf of rolls now, at Ryan’s ask: rags and popular songs from the 1900s to the 1920s, and three rolls played for the Welte-Mignon reproducing piano. Two were played onto the paper by the composers themselves: Scott Joplin, in 1916, and Alexander Scriabin, in 1910.
  • A roll plays as a strip of sound, not on a screen, so the pub still has no screen anywhere. It plays only when you press it, one roll at a time, and every roll says how long it runs before you press. Each is a recording of a real roll on Wikimedia Commons, public domain there, and nothing is fetched from Commons until you press. Nothing musical is hosted here.
  • Every composer on the shelf died more than seventy years ago, so the music is free everywhere and not only in the United States. Several rolls were left off for that, and several files that turned out to be notation software playing sheet music rather than rolls. Every roll names its composers with their years, what roll it is, how the recording was made where its Commons page says so, and that page. Every runtime was measured off the file.
  • The privacy page lists Wikimedia Commons among the services a press loads.

2026-10-05 — A Quiet Pint: a pub under the street, made like McAnally’s

  • A Quiet Pint is a new shopfront, at Ryan’s ask: a pub a few steps down from the street, made like McAnally’s in Jim Butcher’s Dresden Files. Thirteen tables, thirteen stools along a bar that meanders, thirteen carved columns, thirteen windows, thirteen mirrors and thirteen ceiling fans, as in the books, and no two of any of them alike. There is no television, no games machine and no jukebox, only a player piano.
  • The only light is the street’s, through small windows at the level of the pavement, passed round the room by old mirrors, so it lands in patches, none the same shape, a little dimmer and a little green. Nobody lights a lamp and the stove is shut. Nothing in the pub moves, at any setting, the fans included.
  • A table that fits you. The tables are all different heights, so the room has a plan of the floor and a list of every table in words, with boxes to tick for what you want: room under it for a wheelchair or your knees, a tall table, a low one with soft seats, your back to a wall, out of sight of the door, away from the bar, by the stove, in a patch of light or out of it. Every table can be reached from the door by a way at least 36 inches wide, and the room’s own tool walks the floor to make sure. Sit at a table, order from the bar, and look down to see your table.
  • Where it parts from the books, and says so: a ramp beside the steps, as wide as they are; the ceiling still low, but the fans up between the beams; and your order fetched from the bar or brought to you, whichever you say, and nobody asks why. The measurements are the 2010 ADA Standards’, read on the Access Board’s page.
  • Every pint comes alcohol-free first, in the same glass, at the same size, so nobody can tell from across the room which one anybody has. Ale at cellar temperature, never cold; lemonade with lemonade ice; a mushroom sandwich first and a steak sandwich after it, from the stove. Nothing has a price, and nobody has to order anything to sit down, which is our own Third Places page’s line.
  • Butcher’s words are quoted, not retold: short passages, each naming its book and pages, held to a cap. McAnally’s, Mac and the Dresden Files are his; no wizard drinks here, and nothing is drawn from the fan wiki. Set in Amarante and Brygada 1918, read off their own records.
  • Two shared tools changed for it. table.js can now say how an order reached you, which only this room uses. Big Steep Fermentables’ list of words that press somebody to drink refused the pub’s round tables on its first run, so “round” there now means the round somebody buys. love.css: section 100 is the pub; small screens and print are 101 and 102.

2026-10-05 — Four lots where the buildings stop: the Run, the Truck Stop, the Green and the Pile

  • Open ground at the far end of the street, at Ryan’s ask: four outdoor lots side by side behind a fence, with a gate into each from the front page, and each lot’s gate through to the next. None of them is a building, so none of them is a shopfront, for the reason the Garden’s gate is not one. On the Map they lie flat down one side of the road, next door to each other.
  • The Run is a dog park with a covered porch of swings, rocking chairs and plush beds, a row of trees and a field for fetch, painted in the colours a dog can see: blues, yellows and greys, and nothing red or green, which the room’s tool checks colour by colour. The ball is blue. Every dog from Rescue A Dog comes out here, put where their mood takes them: press one to meet them, throw the ball, shake the treat tin, or ask one to come and lie by your chair. Dogs who want to be left alone are never asked. Television made for dogs is on the dog telly, one channel of it live.
  • The cats at Pekoe and Purrs and the dogs on the Run now share one script, roam.js, which reads a shelter’s list and puts each animal on a perch by its mood; each room keeps its own words. Checked in the browser: the café works as it did, lap and all. A list of names in what the toys did no longer capitalises a name in the middle of a sentence.
  • Truckin’ Food Court, which everybody calls the Truck Stop: tacos, bánh mì, ramen, treats for dogs and a cooler of cold drinks, each truck doing one thing several ways and leading with a vegan way of doing it, and nothing with a price on it. Every item says what is in it and its allergens, and every drink its caffeine. The oral rehydration solution is the World Health Organization’s recipe to the gram, and every dog treat is checked against the ASPCA’s list of foods to keep from pets. The light comes through the trucks’ striped awnings.
  • The kitchens’ shared allergen engine learned two words: a baguette is wheat bread, which three bánh mì would otherwise have been told had no gluten, and oat flour is oats, not wheat.
  • The Green is a wooded green with a pond, just after the rain: nothing casts a shadow, and everything wet holds the sky along its top edge. An accessible boardwalk goes round the pond, built to the Access Board’s standard for trails, with a trailhead sign giving every figure that standard asks for. You can walk it stop by stop, with beds of herbs, flowers, ferns and vegetables anybody may pick.
  • The Pile is a junk playground of pallets, plywood, rope, an old piano, an old slide and the rest of Ryan’s list, with a den roofed in bubble wrap. Take anything off it and say what it is now, paint it, knock it down. With where junk playgrounds come from, read on the pages named, and our own Play Is Learning.
  • All of Ryan’s films were asked of their own watch pages first. Every one plays. NHK’s RAMEN JAPAN is a door to YouTube, because its channel has switched off showing it on other sites. Set in eight faces, each read off its own record: Lilita One and Mukta, Sansita and Familjen Grotesk, Della Respira and Alegreya, Rubik Wet Paint and Shantell Sans. love.css: sections 96 to 99 are the four lots; small screens and print are 100 and 101.

2026-10-05 — Screen a video at Looming Rocks and the Rebellion Rave Room

  • A moderator’s Screen a video now works on Looming Rocks’ stage and on the Rebellion Rave Room’s big screen, as it does on the Hermitage’s television. It plays for the moderator who pressed it, and the desk goes back to the bill or the channel list.
  • Catch up and Follow beside them put it on your stage or screen and leave it dark, on the panel every act or channel uses, saying who is hosting it and how long it runs; nothing plays until you press play. At Looming Rocks a video that is on the bill puts that act on the desk. At the rave it is always cued on its own, so it can start where the host is, and it says it may flash.
