2026-10-05 — Screen a video on the Hermitage’s television

  • A moderator’s Screen a video works on the Solarpunk Hermitage’s campfire television now, as it does on a rack’s screen. It plays on the set at once for the moderator who pressed it, and the remote tunes back to the listing.
  • Catch up and Follow beside them tune it on your set and leave the set off, on the same panel every channel uses, saying who is hosting it and how long it runs; nothing plays until you press play, which is the set’s rule. If the video is one of the listing’s own channels, that channel is what gets tuned.

2026-10-04 — Screen a video, from the CB

  • Moderators can screen a video in a room without building it into a rack first. Screen a video on the radio takes the address of one YouTube video and puts it up on the first screen of the page you are on, in place of what the screen had, the way a card’s second press does; the button under the screen puts back what was there. It plays on your page and is sent nowhere. It shows only to moderators, and only on pages with a screen.
  • Host it, and the room can follow. Press Host this film once it plays. Anybody who presses Catch up or Follow beside you gets that video’s play button on their own first screen, never the film itself: it says how long the video runs and who put it up, and nothing reaches YouTube until they press it. Only a moderator’s hosting does this, and the server says whose hosting is a moderator’s, so nobody else can put a video on somebody’s screen. Following a host’s link from another room gets it ready too.
  • A host’s beacon now says how long the film runs, so that play button can say it before the press. The privacy page and the CB guide say all of this.
  • Fixed: following a spot’s room link did nothing on most pages, because the page looked for the spot before the radio was there. It looks once the radio is there now.

2026-10-04 — Brew and Stew, a woodland tea room

  • A new pitch on the Campgrounds: Brew and Stew, pitch 04, a tea room in a cabin at the edge of the trees, and nobody lit it. The light grows in the wood: a pale glow in the joints between the logs, in the moss on the sill and in the little mushrooms at the foot of the walls. The stove is shut iron, giving the room its warmth and none of its light; there is no flame in it anywhere, and nothing in it moves, at any setting.
  • The board: herbal teas, soups and stews, and breads, and everything on it is vegan. Every card says what is in it and its allergens, worked out by Hey, Good Cookin’s engine; every tea is a tisane with no caffeine, worked out by Pekoe and Purrs’ engine; there is no honey, so every tea comes with maple syrup on the side. Nothing on the board is a remedy, and nothing in the room was picked in the wood.
  • Flowers, grasses and twigs on the shelf by the door, each in something to stand in, to take to your table: lavender and oat grass in a jug, birch twigs and catkins in a jar, honesty and willow in a bottle, and more. Every one was grown in the cottage garden or dried from last year’s.
  • Look down at your table, a slice of log with its rings, to see what you asked for. This is the same behaviour as Pekoe and Purrs’ table, moved into one file, table.js, that both rooms use in their own clothes; the café works exactly as it did.
  • The looking-glass by the window, with nothing in it until you put a film up, and a rack of other people’s woodland tea rooms and cottages that Ryan picked. Every one plays here and says how long it runs before you press it. One is held off the rack for now: it has the same title, word for word, as another video on the rack, runs one second longer, and went up two years later on a different channel.
  • On the Campgrounds’ board the tea room’s sign is a cabin with its window dark and its light in its own logs, the fourth kind of light on that board. Set in Macondo, by John Vargas Beltrán, and Livvic, by LV= and Jacques Le Bailly, both under the SIL Open Font License. A job marker (a wooden spoon), a share card, a fractal, the map, Now Playing and the liner notes. In the stylesheet the tea room is section 95, and Small screens and Print are now 96 and 97.

2026-10-04 — Pekoe and Purrs, a cat café

  • A new shopfront on the street: Pekoe and Purrs, a cat café with a window at every height, each cut to the size of a cat. Coffee, tea and tea sandwiches, and every cat from Rescue A Cat, waiting for a home. The café keeps no list of its own: it reads the shelter’s every time it opens, so a cat adopted this morning has already gone home and one brought in an hour ago is already finding somewhere to sit.
  • Press a cat to meet them: their name and the shelter’s own words about them, and a link straight to that cat’s card at the shelter, where anybody signed on to the CB can adopt them. Pick a cat up and put them on your lap, and look down to see your lap, the cat asleep on it, and your table with whatever you asked for from the menu. A cat the shelter says wants to be left alone is never picked up: there is no button for it.
  • Where a cat goes is its mood’s business, the mood the shelter wrote down when it came in, and never its markings: a cat with three legs gets to the top of the tree like anybody else. At MAX GLITTER the cats wander about by themselves; at Gentle and Regular they stay put until you wave the feather wand, roll the crinkle ball or put down a box.
  • The menu: coffee, not coffee, tea and tea sandwiches, with plenty that is vegan and vegetarian. Every item says what is in it, whether it is vegan, vegetarian or which animal, and its allergens, worked out by the same engine as Hey, Good Cookin’; every drink says how much caffeine, worked out from what is in it. The house milk is oat, the house tea is orange pekoe, which is a grade of black tea and not a flavour, and every cup, mug and pot is the same teal mug.
  • Rescue A Cat: every cat in the shelter now has an address of its own on the page, so a link from the café lands on that cat’s card. The cats themselves are drawn by the same file as always, which can now draw one in daylight and one curled up asleep on a lap.
  • Set in Cherry Bomb One, by Satsuyako, and Gabarito, by Naipe Foundry, both under the SIL Open Font License. A job marker (a felt mouse), a share card, a fractal, the map and the liner notes. In the stylesheet the café is section 94, and Small screens and Print are now 95 and 96.

2026-10-04 — Three small things the CB guide turned up

  • Move answers when you press it. On the radio and on a call’s window, pressing Move without dragging used to do nothing you could see: the arrow keys were only described to a screen reader, and a click did not even put the keyboard on it. Now a press puts the keyboard on Move, so the arrow keys work straight away, and says in a line how moving works. Dragging is unchanged.
  • The button is called Forget me, and Privacy, the shelters, the job about it at the Adventurer’s Guild and the README now call it that rather than Forget Me. The same job also lost a doubled word: Profile is on your CB radio, not your CB radio radio.
  • A note in the code still said the Faery Yurt had no guest bar. Helen said yes on 2026-09-30, and the note says so now. Nothing a visitor sees changed.

2026-10-04 — The CB guide

  • A guide to the CB, the street’s radio, part by part and top to bottom, with every button named the way the radio names it: the guest bar and signing on, the bar and its sizes, World and this room, Be seen here, reading and writing messages, reactions, Markdown, pictures, the row of tools, the teleporter, watching films together, calls, Profile and pets, and what moderators see. It ends with every key in one table and a short account of what the radio sends and keeps.
  • It is linked from How the radio works at the Community Center, from the foot of the radio beside the house norms, and from the guest bar for anybody not signed on yet.
  • The Community Center still said links on the channel were only rooms on the street, which stopped being true on 2026-09-29. It now says what its own house norms already did: a link’s words are always its address.

2026-10-04 — Architecture

  • A new page describing how the site runs, beside How this site is made: that page is the why and this one is the how. It covers the deploy from a push to main, the stylesheet and scripts, the data files and the generators that write parts of pages from them, every checker and what it refuses, the CB’s server side and what it keeps for how long, the morning timers, and every outside service with when it is contacted. Secrets are named and never shown.
  • Its map of what talks to what is boxes and words rather than a picture, so it reflows on a phone and reads aloud in the order a change travels. It is paperwork rather than a room, so it is pinned to the edge of the map with the others, carries no job marker, and takes the plain pages’ quiet clothes.

2026-10-04 — The security policy catches up with the CB

  • SECURITY.md described the CB as it was on its first day: a handful of functions, one blob and no accounts. It now says what is there: claimed usernames with their own passwords and recovery codes, moderator roles, the private rooms in the Town Hall, pictures on the channel, the Brass Tacks Board and its feed, and 8x8’s calls and webhooks. The list of what is worth reporting grew with it, and so did the list of secrets kept in Netlify’s environment, by name only.

2026-10-04 — Hey, Good Cookin’

  • A community kitchen on the street, at Ryan’s ask and in his words: come if you’re hungry for food or for knowledge, no one is turned away, you don’t have to pay and you don’t have to volunteer. Cafeteria-style self-service, sit-down seating, and a communication badge placard on every table: green for open seating, yellow for ask if you know somebody, red for please don’t sit here, each with its shape and its word so colour is never the only sign. It is a hall on a cold day with every pot on, its windows steamed up, and the only sharp light in it is where a drop has run down the glass.
  • An omnivorous menu with plenty of vegan and vegetarian: a hot line for every day of the week, posted whole, with the vegan pan first every day, and stations from the soup kettles to the plain things that never change. Every dish card says what is in it, whether it is vegan or vegetarian or which animal it came from, and which of the FDA’s nine major allergens it has, and gluten, worked out from the ingredients so the two lines cannot disagree. No calorie counts, and nothing about what a dish does for you.
  • The line and every table are built to work from a wheelchair, to the 2010 ADA Standards’ numbers, each linked to its section; the standard asks for one seat in twenty and here it is every seat. Help is offered and never assumed, and anybody who cannot do the line today can sit down and have the board and the plate brought.
  • Cooking classes written in full, each saying how long it takes, what it sounds and smells like, and that it can all be done sitting down, with FoodSafety.gov’s temperatures beside anything that needs them. No sign-up, no register, no grades.
  • A rack of the last month from cooking channels Ryan chose, videos and shorts, which plays where it hangs or on the teaching screen, and refills itself every morning on the same timer as the other racks. The room says plainly that nobody here watched the videos first. Nothing loads from YouTube until you press.
  • Set in Corben, by Vernon Adams, and Parkinsans, by Red Stone, both new to the street. A job marker is on the floor, and every source is in the liner notes.

Learning, making, and knowledge gardening, together. It is a glasshouse at night under the old red and blue grow lamps, and nothing in it is green, because under that light a leaf has no green to give back.

Every quotation is checked word for word against our page on every build, and says where it was read.

2026-10-01 — Search every word on the street

  • The search box on the street and on the Map searches every page now, not only the rooms’ names, as all our other sites already do. Rooms found by name still come first. Under them come the sections of pages that hold every word you typed, each linked to the section rather than the top of the page, with a few lines round the first match, and the changelog after everything else.
  • A quotation is never cut down in a result. A match inside a quotation shows who it is credited to and where it is, and not a trimmed piece of somebody else’s words. A snippet that would start or end inside a quotation in our own sentences is widened to hold all of it.
  • It reads every page’s own words, written into search-index.json by a new tool each time the site is checked. It leaves out the parts the morning timers refill (the Doom Scoop’s cabinet, The Feed’s board, the kitchen telly and the Now Playing bill), the job markers, and the lyric printed in full at The Small Hours, which is there with permission and not under this site’s licence.
  • Why not Pagefind, which one of our sites uses: it runs WebAssembly, which would loosen the part of this site’s security policy that decides what scripts may run; its index is a folder of many small files that would change in every morning’s commit; and both of the machines that publish here would need it installed. A file of our own words, searched in your browser, is how most of our sites do it.
  • The file is fetched from this site the first time you type, and what you type never leaves your browser. The privacy page says so.

2026-10-01 — Finding a room by what it is about

  • A search box near the top of the street and of the Map, for Helen Edgar, who asked for a search bar at the top. It is on those two pages and nowhere else, Ryan’s call, because the teleporter already searches from every page and a bar across the top of every room would lay the street’s furniture over all of them. What it finds are ordinary links, and Enter goes to the first.
  • Typing “fire” in the teleporter finds The Campfire now, as Helen Edgar asked. It looks inside the words of a room’s name as well as at their starts, so a part of a word is enough.
  • It also searches each room’s own description, the summary at the top of its page, so a keyword finds the rooms it is about. A room found that way shows the words it was found in. Rooms found by their name come first, then rooms found by their description, each group in walking order. Every word you type has to be found, and a plural also finds the singular.
  • The box, the radio and the guest bar all search this way, and a new check makes sure they never find different rooms. Nothing about a search is sent anywhere.

2026-10-01 — Share cards that stay put between two Macs

  • The share cards stopped trading places between Ryan’s Mac and Helen Edgar’s. The same Chrome on two versions of macOS draws the soft edges of letters a little differently, so each run of the checks rewrote every card on the street with a near-identical copy. Helen’s Claude found it, and measured it.
  • The tool that makes the cards now records what each one is made from: its words, every style on it, and every typeface and picture it uses. When none of that has changed, the card already committed stays. A changed word, colour, face or image still makes a new card. We tried comparing the pictures pixel by pixel first, and that could not tell a Mac’s edges from a small contrast fix, so it was not kept.

2026-10-01 — The Brass Tacks Board on The Feed

  • The Feed has a board for the Brass Tacks Board now, right after this street’s own, which is the one place in stimpunks.org’s order it can go without re-sorting anybody else. Ryan’s ask.
  • It is a second feed of this street, not a site of its own, so the hall says “one wire per feed” now, and the Garden, which plants one bed for every site we publish, leaves it out.
  • It may be empty. A board with every post taken down is a board, not a broken feed, so it shows one blank flap saying so, and the morning’s board is still set. Its titles are a moderator’s own words, so a “<” typed in one does not stop the board being set either. Changed: data/arrivals.json, tools/pull-arrivals.py, tools/make-arrivals.py, tools/make-garden.py, tools/make-og.py, the-feed.html.

2026-09-30 — The Brass Tacks Board

  • New on the pavement, beside the Pebble Board: the Brass Tacks Board, for things meant to last. News, notices, events, celebrations and posts, each on a brass plate tacked to a wall of old copper gone green, kept until it is taken down. Anybody can read it, and it has a feed of its own. Ryan’s brief.
  • Only our moderators post on it, and every post carries the username of the moderator who put it up, public on purpose. The author can edit it, and the post says it was edited and when; any moderator can take any post down. Ryan’s calls.
  • A post is Markdown, as fully as we could do it safely: headings, bold, italic, strikethrough, code and code blocks, lists inside lists, quotes, tables, rules and links. The server reads it once into a plain structure, and the board and the feed build that, so nothing typed into a post can become code on the page. A link goes to the web, an email address or somewhere on the street, and a link off the street shows where it goes.
  • A post can carry a picture, redrawn in the moderator’s browser first by the radio’s own redraw, which takes off everything a camera writes into a file; it needs a description, and the moderator says everybody recognisable in it has said yes. The privacy page says what the board keeps.
  • No new database. It is Netlify Blobs, like the rest of the CB, with the boards’ conditional write, tested with fifteen moderators posting at once. It is set in Bellefair and Merriweather, both new to the street. Changed: netlify/cb/lib.mjs and its tests, three new functions, brass.js, cb.js (it shares its redraw now), love.css (the board is §90, and Small screens and Print are §91 and §92), the map, and a new tools/make-brass.py.

2026-09-30 — More reactions, and any emoji at all

  • Our usual ten are in the quick row now: dog, cat, otter, rainbow, unicorn, sign of the horns, hedgehog, hugging face, people hugging and crying face. Ryan’s list.
  • Any other emoji works too. A page cannot open your system’s emoji picker itself, so under the quick row is a small box your picker can type into, and the radio says how to open it on the device you are on: Control, Command and Space on a Mac, the Windows key and full stop on Windows, the emoji key on a phone. The server takes exactly one emoji, skin tones and flags included, and refuses words, markup or two at once.
  • A message holds up to 24 different reactions; one already there can always be pressed. Still names and never a number. Changed: netlify/cb/lib.mjs and its tests, cb-react.mjs, cb.js, cb.css.

2026-09-30 — Reactions on the CB

  • React, beside Copy on every message, opens a small set: a heart, a thumbs up, a laugh, a party, eyes, sparkles, and a penguin, for a pebble. Press one and it goes under the message with your handle; press it again and yours comes back off. Ryan’s brief.
  • A reaction shows who, never how many. The line under a message says “👀 Bo and Zed”, names in alphabetical order, and nowhere on the radio is there a count, which is the street’s rule that nobody is counted, the one “Be seen here” keeps. A screen reader hears the reaction’s name and who pressed it; reactions changing are not read out as they land, because a reaction is not a message.
  • Reactions are part of their message and go with it, at midnight at the latest, or when the base takes the message off. The privacy page and the house norms at the Community Center say so. Tested with fifteen people reacting to one message at once, and nothing told it worked went missing.
  • Changed: netlify/cb/lib.mjs and its tests, a new netlify/functions/cb-react.mjs, cb.js, cb.css, privacy.html, community-center.html.

2026-09-30 — Rescue Small Animals

  • A new shopfront: Rescue Small Animals, beside the cat and dog shelters. Rabbits, rats, mice, hamsters, gerbils, guinea pigs, chinchillas, ferrets and hedgehogs turn up at random under the floorboards of a shop; the first person signed on to the CB to press brings them in and names them, and anybody can adopt one into Profile on their radio, rename them, and send a sticker of them. Ryan’s brief. Birds and reptiles may come later.
  • No animal is rarer than another. Every species is equally likely, each comes in three coats it really comes in, so there is no ginger hedgehog, and the words say what each animal is: a sable ferret, a hooded rat, a ginger and white guinea pig.
  • The light is the day coming down through the cracks between the boards, seen only where it lands on the dust in long pale strips, and every animal is seen standing in one. It is set in Carter One, by Vernon Adams, and Balsamiq Sans, by Michael Angeles, both new to the street.
  • Changed: netlify/cb/lib.mjs and its tests, the shelter functions, animals.js, shelter.js, cb.js, love.css (the new room is §89, and Small screens and Print are §90 and §91 now), tools/make-rescue.py, which now reads every kind’s coats and caught itself reading none of the new ones on its first run, and privacy.html.

2026-09-30 — Pet stickers, and pressing a handle to see somebody’s pets

  • Stickers. Sticker, beside Picture on the radio, lists your pets; press one and a drawing of them, with their name, goes to the channel you are tuned to. The server finds the pet among your own, so nobody can send a sticker of somebody else’s, and it goes at midnight with the rest of the channel. Everybody sees its words, a screen reader hears them and speaking aloud reads them. Ryan’s brief.
  • Press a handle on the channel to see their pets, if they have made them public. It opens on the readout under the messages; Close or Escape puts you back on the handle you pressed.
  • Showing your pets is off until you switch it on, with Show my pets on the CB in Profile, and only a claimed username can, because an unclaimed handle is anybody’s. Shown pets are their drawings, names and what they are; never when they came in or were adopted. The answer is the same for a handle that is not shown, not claimed or nobody at all. The privacy page says what that keeps, and that showing your pets lets people find them on the list of everybody adopted.
  • Changed: netlify/cb/lib.mjs and its tests, netlify/functions/cb-transmit.mjs, cb-pets.mjs, a new cb-pets-of.mjs, cb.js, cb.css, tools/make-rescue.py, both rescue rooms and privacy.html.

2026-09-30 — Rename your pets

  • An adopted cat or dog can be renamed, from Profile on the radio, as often as you like, or named if they came in without one. Ryan’s call. An animal still in the shelter keeps the name its rescuer gave.
  • The list of everybody adopted shows the new name and the name they came in under, so the rescuer’s naming is not written over. It still never says who adopted or renamed anybody. The base station taking a name off takes both.
  • Rescue A Cat, Rescue A Dog and the privacy page say so. Changed: netlify/cb/lib.mjs and its tests, netlify/functions/cb-pets.mjs, cb.js, shelter.js.

2026-09-30 — Moderators can move onto passwords of their own

  • A moderator can sign on with a password of their own now, instead of the moderators’ password every moderator knows. Signed on as the base, Profile on the radio offers Move onto my own password; from then on the shared password no longer signs on as that moderator, so nobody else can be them. Ryan’s call, starting with himself; it is each moderator’s choice.
  • Roles stay on the moderators’ list, read on every request as before. The account is the same record as anybody’s and holds no role, so taking somebody off the list leaves them an ordinary claimed username, and a list that cannot be read still gives nobody the base.
  • The moderators’ desk does not reset or delete a moderator’s username, because a reset code would let one moderator sign on as another, with the other’s roles. A moderator keeps their recovery code; failing that, Ryan or Helen moves them back.
  • No password of your own can be the community password or the moderators’ one, for anybody claiming. The privacy page and the Moderators’ room say all of this.
  • Changed: netlify/cb/lib.mjs and its tests, the sign-on, account, recovery and desk functions, cb.js, desk.js, the scripts that read the base mark off a pass, tools/make-accounts.py, privacy.html, town-hall-moderators.html.

2026-09-30 — CLAIMED on the channel, beside BASE

  • A message from a claimed username is marked CLAIMED, the way a moderator’s is marked BASE. Ryan’s call, after claiming his own. The mark comes from the pass that sent the message, read by the server, and never from anything the sender sends, so nobody can wear it without the username’s own password. Your own line on the radio says it too, from the moment you claim.
  • The two marks look different on purpose: BASE is a filled amber tag, CLAIMED an outlined one, so they are told apart by shape as well as by word. A moderator’s message wears BASE and only BASE.
  • The house norms at the Community Center and the privacy page say what the mark means and what it does not: it says a username is claimed, never who anybody is. A message already on the channel keeps the mark it was sent with until midnight, even if the account is deleted.
  • Changed: netlify/functions/cb-transmit.mjs, netlify/cb/lib.mjs and its tests, cb.js, cb.css, tools/make-accounts.py, tools/check-contrast.py, community-center.html, privacy.html.

2026-09-30 — Claim your username, with a password of your own

  • You can claim your username on the CB. Signed on, press Profile on the radio and claim the handle you are on as, with a password of your own. From then on it signs on with that password and nothing else, and the community password stops working for it, so nobody else can sign on as you. It is optional: an unclaimed handle works exactly as before. Ryan’s brief.
  • There is no email. When you claim, you are shown a recovery code, once, which is the way back in if you forget your password; a moderator can also give you a reset code, after making sure it is you. Either is used under Forgotten the password at the Community Center.
  • Profile has your pets in it too, where the Pets tray was, with Forget Me. Once your username is claimed, only your password reaches them. It also changes your password, which signs off every other device, and deletes your account, which deletes the username, its password and your pets.
  • Moderators have a desk in the Moderators’ room: look up one username by typing it, give it a reset code, or delete it. There is no list of everybody’s username, for moderators or anybody else.
  • What the street keeps is the username, a salted scrypt hash of its password and of its recovery code, when it was claimed, and a count of wrong passwords (five lock it for fifteen minutes). The privacy page says so, and it changed first; its short version no longer says there are no accounts.
  • Every function on the CB now checks a pass against the store as well as its signature, so a password change, a reset or a deletion signs every device off at once. Tested: the community password refused for a claimed username, the lock, every way of signing devices off, deletion taking the pets, and fifteen people claiming one username at once.
  • Changed: netlify/cb/lib.mjs, netlify/cb/lib.test.mjs, every CB function (the pass is checked against the store now), three new ones for your account, recovering it and the moderators’ desk, cb.js, cb.css, desk.js, guest.js, community-center.html, town-hall-moderators.html, privacy.html, the shelters, tools/make-accounts.py and tools/make-rescue.py.

2026-09-30 — Adopt a cat or a dog, and a Pets tray on the CB

  • You can adopt. Signed on to the CB, press Adopt on a cat at Rescue A Cat, or a dog at the new Rescue A Dog, and they are yours: they live in a Pets tray that pops up from your CB radio, on any device you sign on with. Ryan’s brief.
  • Every room keeps a list of everybody ever adopted, for good, a month at a time, and it never says who adopted anybody.
  • This is the one thing the street keeps under a person, and Ryan said he was fine with that. Your pets are filed under a scrambled form of your handle, not the handle itself. A handle is not an account, so anybody who signs on with yours sees the same pets, and the tray says so. Forget Me is in the Pets tray: it asks once more, then deletes your pets, and the animals stay on the list of everybody adopted with nothing connecting them to you. The privacy page says all of this, and changed first.
  • A new shopfront: Rescue A Dog, the park the morning after snow, where you follow the prints to a dog who needs bringing in. Same game as the cats, different world: the ground brighter than the sky, and every shadow blue.
  • Changed at Rescue A Cat: a cat no longer goes to a home off the street after a week. Cats stay in the shelter until somebody adopts them, and when it is full the next one waits outside. The list of the ones you rescued is now kept under love-rescues, for cats and dogs, and an old love-cats list is moved across. Its job at the Guild asked how long a cat stays, which is no longer a week, so it asks where an adopted cat goes.
  • Set in Paytone One, by Vernon Adams, and Open Sans, by Steve Matteson. The shelters share one mechanism and one set of drawings, and nothing else. Tested with fifteen people adopting one animal at once, and with an adoption stopped halfway: nobody gets it twice and nothing is lost.
  • Changed: rescue-a-dog.html, rescue-a-cat.html, shelter.js (was cats.js), animals.js, cb.js, cb.css, netlify/cb/lib.mjs, netlify/cb/lib.test.mjs, the shelter, adoption and pets functions in netlify/functions/, tools/make-rescue.py (was make-cats.py), love.css, privacy.html, and the tools and data every new room touches.

2026-09-30 — Rescue A Cat, and nobody keeps them

  • A new shopfront on the street: Rescue A Cat, the end of the street where the lights are out, a torch, and now and then a cat that needs bringing in. It is after the catch-a-cat game in our Discord’s Collaborative Nonsense channels, and Ryan renamed it: cats are rescued, not caught.
  • Cats turn up at random, and nothing, not even the shelter, says when the next one is due. Signed on to the CB, the first to press brings a cat in and can give it a name.
  • The shelter shows every cat rescued this week, with its name, and never who rescued it. After a week each cat goes to a home. The cats you rescued are kept in your own browser, under love-cats, the way the Guild keeps your jobs, and the shelter never knows which were yours; Forget my cats clears them. The privacy page says so, and it changed first.
  • No cat is rarer than another. Every coat, marking, place and mood is as likely as any other, which the tests check across thousands of cats; some have three legs or one eye, and they are cats.
  • Set in Fuzzy Bubbles, by Robert Leuschke, and PT Sans, by ParaType. Three new CB functions, tested with fifteen people pressing for one cat at once. A job marker, a share card, the map and a street door.
  • Changed: rescue-a-cat.html, cats.js, netlify/cb/lib.mjs, netlify/cb/lib.test.mjs, three functions in netlify/functions/, tools/make-cats.py, love.css, privacy.html, and the tools and data every new room touches.

2026-09-30 — Count Me In, a stair everybody climbs, with a lift

  • A new shopfront on the street: Count Me In, a fire door onto the stairwell of a public building that goes up and never stops. It is after the counting game in our Discord’s Collaborative Nonsense channels. Signed on to the CB, you put the next number, and then somebody else puts the one after.
  • There is a chairlift, Ryan’s idea, so that everybody can join: riding it takes you up one step with no number to get wrong, it counts exactly the same, it can never send the stair back to one, and its button sits beside the step’s at the same size. Nothing records which way anybody came up.
  • A wrong number sends the stair back to one, and nobody is named, because anybody’s number can slip. A number at or below the step we are on is somebody whose page was behind, and sends nobody anywhere.
  • The stair keeps the highest step it has reached, and that changes a published aim. It is the first number on the street that is a record to beat, and it belongs to nobody; Ryan’s call, made knowing that. The Mission said the one number on the street was Pando Calrissian’s, which stopped being true: it now says the numbers on the street belong to nobody, and names both.
  • Corrected: the privacy page and the README said Pando Calrissian’s tree was the one thing the CB keeps for good, which stopped being true when the Fridge of Sighs started keeping sentences. They say what is kept for good now. And the privacy page has a section on the stair.
  • Set in Allerta Stencil, by Matt McInerney, and Hind, by the Indian Type Foundry. Two new CB functions, tested with fifteen people putting the same number at once and fifteen riding the lift at once. A job marker, a share card, the map and a street door.
  • Changed: count-me-in.html, stair.js, netlify/cb/lib.mjs, netlify/cb/lib.test.mjs, two functions in netlify/functions/, tools/make-stair.py, love.css, privacy.html, mission.html, pando-calrissian.html, README.md, and the tools and data every new room touches.

2026-09-30 — The Fridge of Sighs, a sentence one word at a time

  • A new shopfront on the street: The Fridge of Sighs, a kitchen and a turquoise fridge door covered in word magnets. It is after the sentence builder in our Discord’s Collaborative Nonsense channels. Signed on to the CB, you put up one word; nobody puts up two in a row; a word ending in a full stop, a question mark, an exclamation mark or an ellipsis finishes the sentence.
  • Finished sentences are kept for good, on the door and then in the drawer by month, and no word says who put it up. The one thing kept about anybody is a scrambled mark saying the last word was theirs, which is what stops two in a row, and the next word replaces it. The privacy page says so, and it changed first.
  • Word magnets on a fridge were Dave Kapell’s idea, in 1993, and the room says so. Set in Noticia Text, by JM Solé. Three new CB functions, tested with fifteen people putting up words at once and with a filing stopped halfway. A job marker, a share card, the map and a street door. It began as a printer’s composing stick and moved to the kitchen, because the Foundry already sets words in type.
  • Changed: fridge-of-sighs.html, fridge.js, netlify/cb/lib.mjs, netlify/cb/lib.test.mjs, three functions in netlify/functions/, tools/make-fridge.py, love.css, privacy.html, and the tools and data every new room touches.

2026-09-30 — Pando Calrissian, one tree that is all of us

  • A new shopfront on the street: Pando Calrissian, a gap between the shopfronts with a hillside of aspen behind it after sunset, and one tree on the hill that is everybody’s. It is after the grow-a-tree game in our Discord’s Collaborative Nonsense channels. Signed on to the CB, you water it, and every watering sends up another stem from the one root; the stems grow over the waterings after them, the old ones fall, and the root spreads along the hill. Named for Pando, the aspen in Utah that is one tree, and for Lando Calrissian, whose name is the only thing taken from Star Wars.
  • The tree keeps a count, and it never expires. That reverses the Mission’s aim that nothing is counted, and it was narrowed rather than dropped: the Mission and Broadside No. 2 now say nobody is counted. The tree counts the water and never who poured it: it keeps the number and the time of the last watering and nothing else, so nobody has a total, and there is no leaderboard, no streak and no goal.
  • The ground soaks for a minute after every watering, from anybody, before it takes more. It is the whole tree’s wait, which is how it can wait without a record of who watered when.
  • A fence round the new growth holds notes, on the chalkboard’s rules: anybody reads it, the CB writes on it, and a note comes down after seven days.
  • The privacy page changed first: a section on what the tree and the fence keep.
  • Set in Averia Serif Libre and Averia Sans Libre, Dan Sayers’s type made by averaging every font Google Web Fonts carried in 2011. Four new CB functions, tested with fifteen waterers at once. A job marker, a share card, the map and a street door.
  • Also: the Board room’s Governance & Ethics section was re-copied, because our Accountability page now says the Bylaws were adopted on 29 September 2026.
  • Changed: pando-calrissian.html, pando.js, netlify/cb/lib.mjs, netlify/cb/lib.test.mjs, four functions in netlify/functions/, tools/make-pando.py, love.css, privacy.html, mission.html, data/broadside.json, data/town-hall.json, and the tools and data every new room touches.

2026-09-30 — A mission and a manifesto for the street

  • What the street is for is the street’s mission. It opens with Ryan’s own words on being told we live in our own worlds, and Anaïs Nin on making one. The rest is laid out the way Star Stuff’s and Queering Earth’s are: every aim names the page where it is already being kept, and the failure that would show we had stopped. It also says plainly what the street is not: it is not mutual aid, which the foundation does. It is pinned to the edge of the map with the other paperwork, and every page’s sign-off links to it.
  • The manifesto is Ribald Songing, sheet No. 2 at the Broadsheet Broadside. It argues the lines over the front door one at a time, and it is printed both sides and CC0 like the press’s other sheets. Its two inks are the wordmark’s own pink and cyan, darkened until they print.
  • Corrected on sheet No. 1: it said every play button says how long the thing runs. Playlists and live cameras cannot say that; their buttons say they run until you stop them. The sheet says both now.
  • Changed: mission.html, data/broadside.json, broadsheet-broadside.html, data/fractals.json, data/map.json, tools/signoff.py, tools/make-sitemap.py, tools/make-guild.py, and every page’s sign-off.

2026-09-29 — Norah’s tray at Samefood Cafe

  • A new regular at Samefood Cafe: Norah, whose tray is written exactly as Norah gave it, brands, “etc.” and all. Norah’s samefood of the moment is Knorr Chicken Broccoli Rice, drawn in its bowl from above with the broccoli all through the rice.
  • Changed: data/samefood.json, samefood-cafe.html, tools/make-samefood.py (it can draw a floret of broccoli now), love.css (two new inks in §47), tools/check-contrast.py.

2026-09-29 — NeuroHome, the first room somebody built for themselves

  • NeuroHome is Isha Snow’s room (she/they): her tiny home at the NeuroHomes Community in Washington state, by the Columbia River, with daylight through the firs and candles in every room. It has her bookshelf, a desk with a radio and her instruments, a creativity corner with her books, her podcast and her talk show, her collages on the walls, and a garden and bee yard out the front door. It is the first room to come in through Your Room. She designed it with Claude, following our prompt, and chose everything in it, so the room and its words are hers.
  • Her six collages hang in the frames she drew for them, in photographs she took herself. Like every photograph here they are outside the site’s licence, and each comes down when she says so.
  • The import tool learned three things from her room. It can put a photograph into the space a room left for it, once the photograph has its consent record. Until today it refused any room waiting for a photograph, although the prompt tells people to leave a space and send the photo separately, which is exactly what she did. It also reads :root[data-intensity] as the dial, the same as html[data-intensity], and it drops a room’s own color-scheme line, which the street already works out.
  • The street hosts IBM Plex Mono now, for her labels and her map.

2026-09-29 — The fires in the pit at Swaying Sweetgrass

  • The fire at Swaying Sweetgrass has a rack of fires under it now: fires from its playlist, each saying how long it runs. Each one burns where it stands, or goes up on the fire in place of the whole playlist. Taking it off puts the plate back, unlit.
  • They are the same fires as the Campfire’s at Cavendish Coworking. The Campfire always said it took its fires from this pit, and now it reads them from here, so the two rooms cannot drift apart.
  • The whole playlist lights on the first press now; it used to want a second one inside the player.

2026-09-29 — A screen in every tub at The Doom Scoop

  • Every morning’s tub in The Doom Scoop has a screen of its own at the top now. Any video in the tub plays where it is, as before, or goes up on the screen, bigger, with a second button that says how long it runs. A short goes up upright.
  • The screen starts empty, because a morning is not one list you could put on whole, and it says so. Taking a video off leaves it empty again. Nothing loads from YouTube until you press.
  • This is the same rack behaviour as the Lightbulb Picture House, Mycelium Munchies and Club Chronic, in the parlour’s own ink and frost.

2026-09-29 — Club Chronic’s records go up on the stage

  • Every record along the back wall of Club Chronic has a second button now, with its runtime on it, that puts it up on the stage’s YouTube deck in place of the playlist. Or play it where it hangs, as before. Taking it off puts the playlist back, and plays nothing until you press it.
  • Each rack folds away, and each comes open. The second button is a strip of paste stuck to the sleeve, because in this room a black plate in a marker ring means it plays right there, and a dashed one is a door.
  • The playlist still starts somewhere random after a record comes off the stage. Without a fix it would have gone back to the top: the part that picks the starting point listened on the old button, and putting the playlist back makes a new one.

2026-09-29 — Mycelium Munchies’ rack plays on the big screen, and holds more

  • Derek Sarno’s rack in Mycelium Munchies works the way the Picture House’s racks do now. The masterclass plate is the room’s big screen, and every video has a second button, with its runtime on it, that puts it up on that screen in place of the whole masterclass. Or play it where it hangs, as before. Taking it off puts the masterclass plate back and plays nothing.
  • Ten more of his videos are in the rack, from April 2025 back to December 2023: the next most recent by upload date, read off the playlist again today. Each was checked to be on his own channel, to play in a frame and not to ask anybody to sign in. The newest ten had not changed.
  • The rack folds away, and it comes open. It is dressed in the kitchen’s own steel, cream and lamp red, and the behaviour is the shared rack.js.
  • The masterclass now plays on the first press. Its button was missing the autoplay the room’s other buttons carry, so you had to press twice.

2026-09-30 — The street’s conventions reach every room, and the guest bar signs you on

  • Everybody’s own space takes the street’s conventions as they develop, the dial, the sign-off, the job marker, the CB bar and whatever comes next, as part of being a citizen here, without being asked first. It never touches a room’s own look or words, and a convention somebody does not like is for the Town Hall. Ryan’s call, after Helen Edgar said yes to the guest bar in the Faery Yurt and said she does not need to approve future additions. Your Room says so.
  • The guest bar is in the Faery Yurt now.
  • You can sign on from the guest bar if you already know the community password: the front desk’s own two boxes, and the radio takes the bar’s place on the page you are on.

2026-09-30 — A CB bar for everybody not signed on

  • Every page now has a folded CB bar in the corner for visitors who are not signed on, with the teleporter to go to any room on the street, and Get on the CB, which says what the CB is, that the community password is asked for in the meeting hall, and where to sign on. It is also where a member who signed off will find the way back.
  • It is not the radio: it sends nothing to the channel and shows nothing of it. It stays out of the rooms the radio stays out of, and out of the Faery Yurt until Helen Edgar says yes, because the yurt is hers. The privacy page lists the one thing it keeps, that you left its panel open.

2026-09-29 — Follow and Catch up take you to the film

  • Follow, Catch up and a time in a message now get the film ready for you when it is not already playing: its rack opens, the page goes to it, its play button is marked and has the keyboard, and it will start at the right place when you press it, or press Enter. Before, they only wrote a sentence under the message box, which people missed, and left them to find the film on a rack full of similar names.
  • A host’s film is found by its video, not only by its title, so a rack of lookalike names cannot send you to the wrong one. If the radio is in the way, it goes small for the moment and says so.
  • The radio’s answers are under its readout now, beside the log and the host’s buttons, rather than under the message box.

2026-09-29 — Who is in a call, in the call window too

  • The call window says who is in the call, under its own bar and just above 8x8’s Join button, for anybody signed on and seen in the room. The window’s note no longer says nothing about the call is kept.

2026-09-29 — Who is in a call, on the room’s own panel

  • A room’s call panel says who is in the call now, under the Join button, for anybody signed on who has chosen to be seen in that room, the same as the radio. The panels’ small print no longer says we keep nothing about a call, which stopped being true when the list arrived.

2026-09-29 — Be seen here, and who is in a call

  • The radio has a Be seen here switch in every room, the Slake’s own, at the community’s asking. Switched on, you are shown by your handle to anybody else who has switched it on in the same room, and they are shown to you: names only, in alphabetical order, and no count. It is one switch both ways, so while it is off nobody sees you and you see nobody. Your browser remembers it once you press it.
  • Seen in a room, you can see who is in its call before you join: everybody in it, guests included, from 8x8. A guest’s notice on the public calls says so before they type a name, and the privacy page says what is kept, which is a name while somebody is in the call and nothing after.

2026-09-29 — Downloading a file shared in a call

  • A file shared in a call downloads in Firefox and Safari now. The call said “Download started” and nothing arrived, because this site’s own security policy refused the way Jitsi hands a file to the browser; Chrome never asked, so it worked there. The policy allows it now, and still admits no other site.

2026-09-29 — Links to other sites on the CB

  • An address on another site in a CB message is a link now, where before only rooms on this street were. It opens in a new tab with the site it goes to written after it, like ↗ example.com, sends no referrer, and a link’s words are always its address. This reverses the street-only rule of 2026-09-25, at Ryan’s call, and the house norms and the privacy page say what it does.

2026-09-29 — Longer messages on the CB

  • A CB message can run to 2,000 characters and keep its lines. The message box is a box of several lines now, which grows as you type and can be dragged taller: Enter sends, and Shift and Enter starts a new line.
  • A little Markdown works: bold, italic, code, a block of code, a quote and a list. It is drawn by the radio as ordinary text, never as markup, and links are still only rooms on the street.
  • Copy on every message puts its words, as they were typed, on your own clipboard.
  • Size on the bar makes the radio Small, Normal or Large. Large is wider and taller, for long messages and pictures.

2026-09-29 — A picture on the CB opens larger

  • Press a picture on the CB to see it as large as the window allows, with its description and a Download button; Escape or Close puts you back on the picture. Every picture also has a small Download link under it.
  • A picture can no longer push the radio wider than its window, which was cutting off Transmit in Safari, and the Picture and Transmit buttons keep their full labels in Firefox.

2026-09-29 — Pictures on the CB

  • A message on the CB can carry a picture now: press Picture beside Transmit, pick a screenshot or a photo, and say what is in it if you like. The radio redraws it in your own browser before it goes, which takes off where it was taken and anything else a camera writes into a file.
  • A picture goes when its message goes: pushed off by an eleventh, taken off by the base, or at midnight, and one that was never sent within the hour. It has no public address, and a picture sent in a private room is only for the people that room is for. The privacy page says the rest.

2026-09-29 — Sharing files in a call

  • Everybody in a room’s call can share a file now, a screenshot or a photo, from the call’s own Share file button, which used to be greyed out. The file goes to 8x8, who run the calls, and never to us; the privacy page says what that means. The CB’s channel stays words only.

2026-09-29 — Executive Session, off the Town Hall

  • A new door off the Town Hall opens onto Executive Session: the board without staff, in a dark room lit only by the EXIT sign over its shut door, with what an executive session is, from BoardSource’s guide.
  • Its radio and call are for the board role and nothing else. It is the one room the administrator key does not open, because an executive session is the board meeting without staff. Anybody can walk in and read it; the server keeps the rest.

2026-09-29 — Directors join the Board room

  • The Board room now takes moderators with the director role as well as the board role, because directors join board meetings. Its radio, its call and anything hosted in it follow, and the server checks, every time.

2026-09-29 — Moderators have their own handles and roles

  • The moderators’ password now only works for a handle on the moderators’ list, and the community password refuses any handle on it, so nobody else can sign on as a moderator. The list is kept in the site’s settings and not published.
  • The Town Hall’s rooms follow the roles on it. The Moderators’ room is for any moderator, the Board room for moderators with the board role, the Directors’ room for moderators with the director role, and administrators can go into all of them. The server checks, every time.

2026-09-29 — The Moderators’ room, and every door off the Town Hall is open

  • The last door off the Town Hall opens onto the Moderators’ room: a room for Stimpunks Moderators to meet, above the street at night, lit only by the shopfronts’ own colours on its ceiling. It says what the base looks after and where to report a concern.
  • Anybody can walk in; the radio and the call in there are for the base, and the server keeps it that way.

2026-09-29 — Today’s board meeting, in the Board room

  • The Board room carries the outline of today’s board meeting: the decisions required, the order of business with its timings, and how to take part from somewhere else. The papers behind it go to the board.

2026-09-29 — The Board room, off the Town Hall

  • A door off the Town Hall opens onto the Board room: a room for Stimpunks Board Members to meet, at one long table with the same small lamp at every seat. It lists the board as our Board & Governance page does, and carries the Governance & Ethics section of our Accountability & Transparency page, word for word.
  • Anybody can walk in; the radio and the call in there are for the base, and the server keeps it that way.

2026-09-29 — The Directors’ room, off the Town Hall

  • A door off the Town Hall opens onto the Directors’ room: a room for Stimpunks Directors to meet, lit only through the frosted pane in its door. It lists the directors as our About page does, each linked to their own section there.
  • Anybody can walk in; the radio and the call in there are for the base, and the server keeps it that way.

2026-09-29 — The Fishbowl, off the Town Hall

  • The first door off the Town Hall opens onto the Fishbowl: a room for community fishbowl meetings for anyone with CB access. A few people talk in the middle, everybody else listens round the outside, and one seat in the middle is always kept empty for whoever wants to step in.
  • It is a glass bowl of water on a sunny sill, lit by the sun bent through the water into bright lines on the gravel, with a ring of stones inside that always has one gap in it. The call is for people signed on to the CB, and anybody can look round.

2026-09-29 — The Town Hall

  • A new shopfront on the street: the Town Hall, a round hall under a dome that is open to the sky, where the sun lies on the floor as one disc. Doors round its wall go to the Fishbowl, for community fishbowl meetings on the CB, and to rooms for our directors, our board and our moderators. The rooms behind the doors open one at a time.
  • Those three rooms are the base’s. Anybody can walk into them; their channel, their call and any film hosted in them are handed to people signed on with the moderators’ password and to nobody else, by the server rather than only by the page, and the radio waits outside for everybody else.

2026-09-29 — The meeting hall, at the Community Center

  • The Community Center now starts with the meeting hall: our community’s group chat, which is moving from Discord to Stoat. It says what Stoat is, how to get an invite through the join form on stimpunks.org, what the channels are, and how the hall and the CB differ: the hall keeps things and has proper sign-in, and the CB forgets everything at midnight.
  • The hall is a link, not part of this page. Stoat’s app does not let other sites frame it, and nothing on the street sends anything to Stoat.

2026-09-28 — The Cave, upstairs at Cavendish, and Proton has gone

  • The Cave door at Cavendish Coworking opens onto the Cave: a tunnel in glacier ice, for quiet solo work with other people nearby. Nothing in it gives off light; the daylight comes through the ice itself, blue.
  • Caves to leave on, chosen by Ryan from a shortlist: water dripping in a cave on Mount Cucco first, then sea caves, ice caves, cenotes and lava tubes, each with how long it runs. The call is for people signed on to the CB, and anybody can look round.
  • Every door at Cavendish opens a room of ours now, and Proton Meet has gone from the site. The meetings are open to anybody: type a name and wait in the lobby. The page says how the calls go, and the privacy page says what reaches 8x8.

2026-09-28 — The Campfire, upstairs at Cavendish

  • The Campfire door at Cavendish Coworking opens onto the Campfire: a fire in a clearing at night with a ring of logs round it, for small groups working on something together or watching and listening together. The fire is the only light, so every shadow points away from it.
  • Real campfires to leave burning: the fire in the middle is the playlist the fire pit at Swaying Sweetgrass keeps, and round it are fires from that playlist, each with how long it runs. The call is for people signed on to the CB, and anybody can look round.

2026-09-28 — The Watering Hole, upstairs at Cavendish

  • The Watering Hole door at Cavendish Coworking opens onto the Watering Hole: a pan of water in the desert at noon, for come-and-go chat with no agenda, with every shadow straight under the thing that casts it. Its norms are on the wall.
  • A rack of cams to leave on: the Jungle Room’s desert, otters and jellies, read out of that room’s own list. Each plays in its card or on the screen by the water.
  • The call is for people signed on to the CB; anybody can come in and look round, and the room says how to get on the CB. It is the first of the Hang Suites to replace its Proton room.
  • Racks now share one script, rack.js, which the Lightbulb Picture House moved onto without changing anything you can see.

2026-09-28 — The Editorial Room, behind Cavendish’s Editorial door

  • The Editorial door at Cavendish Coworking opens onto the Editorial Room: a designer’s light table in a dark studio, the proofs lit from underneath and the only light there is, where we work on the words, design and everything we put into the world. The meeting’s time is on the proof, in our events page’s own words, with the hour on your own clock.
  • All three of our open meetings now have a room and a call of their own, open to anybody who knocks. The Hang Suites still open Proton until theirs are built.

2026-09-28 — The Operations Room, behind Cavendish’s Operations door

  • The Operations door at Cavendish Coworking opens onto the Operations Room: a building’s plant room, where we run the thing, with every main painted the colour of what it carries and one caged lamp throwing its cage’s shadow across everything. The weekly task scrub’s time is on the log, in our events page’s own words, with the hour on your own clock.
  • Its call is open to anybody, the same way as the Events Room’s: type the name you want to be called, knock, and wait in the lobby for a moderator.

2026-09-28 — The Events Room, behind Cavendish’s Events door

  • The Events door at Cavendish Coworking opens onto a room of its own now: the Events Room, a lighthouse keeper’s watch room, lit by one column of light from the lantern above. Our recurring events are its light list, in our events page’s own words, with the hour on your own clock under each one.
  • Its call is open to anybody. If you are not signed on to the CB, type the name you want to be called and knock, and you wait in the lobby until a moderator lets you in. It is the first of the rooms that replace the Proton links, one door at a time; the other doors still open Proton until theirs are built.

2026-09-28 — Follow the host

  • When somebody is hosting the film in your room, the CB’s radio has a Follow switch beside Catch up. Press it and your own film keeps pace with the host’s: it plays when they play, pauses when they pause, and moves when they jump, while its volume and captions stay yours. That is the thing Jitsi’s shared video cannot do, since only the person who shares one gets a volume.
  • Pausing your own film stops following, which is how you take a break, and pressing Follow again picks up where the host is. It is off until you press it, it is never remembered, it sends nothing, and it stops when the host stops.

2026-09-28 — Share a YouTube video in a call

  • A room’s call can share a YouTube video now, from the call’s own menu, for everybody in it to watch in step. It was missing because one setting of ours, which kept Jitsi from contacting anybody else, also switches that feature off. That setting is gone, and the three other things it covered (Jitsi’s analytics, its avatar lookups and its animated-picture search) are each switched off on their own instead.
  • The person who shares a video plays and pauses it for everybody, and Jitsi mutes your microphone while it plays with the sound on. When somebody shares one, YouTube loads in everybody’s call window, and the privacy page says so.

2026-09-28 — The radio’s answers no longer run under its buttons

  • A long answer on the CB’s radio, like the one Catch up gives when no film has been started, ran underneath the buttons below it on a full radio. Every part of the radio keeps its own height now, and the radio scrolls instead.

2026-09-28 — A call moves like the radio

  • A room’s call can be moved anywhere on the screen, the way the radio can: drag its bar, or put the keyboard on its Move button and use the arrow keys, and Home puts it back in its corner. It never goes off the edge, and Large still fills the window and then goes back to where you had it. Where you put it is not remembered, because nothing about a call is kept.

2026-09-28 — A small size for a call

  • A room’s call has a Small size now, beside Large: a tile in the corner, so there is room to watch a film on the page while you are in the call. Changing size never interrupts the call. Small and Large each go back to the middle size when you press them again.

2026-09-28 — A call for every room

  • Join this room’s call on the CB’s radio opens voice and video for the room you are in, in a window of its own at the foot of the page, for people signed on to the CB. It runs on 8x8’s Jitsi as a Service, and it replaces nothing: the channel and the Proton rooms are still there.
  • Camera and microphone start off, and you arrive on a screen where you choose before joining. The base station moderates and is the only one who can record or transcribe a call. Leaving the page hangs up; folding the radio away does not.
  • It is the first thing on this site allowed to ask for a camera or a microphone, and the only thing: our pages never ask for either themselves. The privacy page says what goes to 8x8, and that we keep nothing about a call.

2026-09-28 — The radio fits the window

  • The CB radio is never taller than your window now. With a full channel, a host and the teleporter open, it could grow past the top of the screen and take its own Fold away and Small buttons with it. The bar along the top stays where it is and everything under it scrolls, and the radio moves back onto the screen whenever it grows, wherever you have put it.
  • For the base station, Take off now sits at the end of each message’s name-and-time line instead of on a row of its own under every message, which roughly halves the height of a full channel. A screen reader still hears each new message’s words before the button.

2026-09-28 — A channel for every room

  • The CB tunes to World or to this room. World is the channel it always had. Every room on the street now has a channel of its own too, with the same rules: ten messages, and gone at midnight, Colorado time. The radio hears one at a time, says which at the top of its readout, and remembers which you chose, so walking between rooms tuned to rooms hears each room’s channel in turn.
  • Listening to World tells us nothing about where you are. Listening to a room’s channel has to say which room, and the privacy page says so. Nothing records who listened where.

2026-09-28 — Hosting a film on the CB

  • Host this film is on the radio once a film on your page has been started. Press it and everybody on the channel sees which film you are watching, in which room, and where it has got to, as it plays and pauses.
  • In the same room, each radio has a Catch up button that moves that person’s own film to where the host is. Nobody else’s film moves, so anybody taking a break can catch up when they come back. From another room, the host’s room is a link that arrives with the film ready to start at the host’s place.
  • One person hosts a room at a time. It is never remembered, it only sends while the radio is open and the tab is in front, and folding the radio away or leaving the page stops it. The radio never tells us which room you are in to find your room’s host: every radio is sent every host. The privacy page says exactly what is kept, and for how long.

2026-09-28 — A spot reaches the big screen

  • Arriving at the Lightbulb Picture House by a spot on the CB, the film starts at the spot whether you press its card or put it on the big screen, and the line under the screen says where it started from.

2026-09-28 — A spot’s room link takes you to the film, ready

  • Following the room’s name in a spot takes you to that room with the film’s play button ready to start at the spot. The keyboard lands on it, the radio says what it is, and nothing plays until you press it.
  • If the film has no play button of its own there, because it is part of a whole programme, the radio says so and tells you what to do instead.

2026-09-28 — A spot on the CB says which film and which room

  • Add my spot writes the film and the room now, like @34:12 in “An introduction to neurodiversity” at #lightbulb-picture-house. The room shows as its name, linked, so anybody can go there. Before this, a spot dropped from one room moved whatever happened to be playing in another.
  • A spot that names a room or a film only moves that film in that room. Anywhere else, or with a different film playing, nothing moves, and the radio says where that spot is. A time typed bare, like @34:12, still moves whatever is playing on your page.

2026-09-28 — A place in a film is a button on the CB

  • @34:12 in a CB message is a button now. Pressing it moves the film on your own page to that place. It does not press play, so somebody back from a break can catch up with the room and still keep their own pace, their own volume and their own pauses. This replaces pasting timestamps into the channel by hand at a watch-together. A time without the @, like 7:30, stays words.
  • Add my spot appears on the radio once a film on the page has been started. It writes where your film has got to into your message box, and sends nothing until you transmit.
  • It works in every room with a YouTube player, and it does not load YouTube’s own script: the page asks the player it already framed. We tested that the player takes a jump before building anything. Live cameras are left out, because a place means nothing in a live picture. The privacy page and the house norms say how it works.

2026-09-28 — One campfire film the playlist cannot hold

  • Jason Arday’s second talk, Things Can Only Get Better, is on the campfire rack at the Lightbulb Picture House and not in Screen Three’s programme, because YouTube would not let it be added to the playlist. It still plays, from its card or up on the screen from there, and the card says why the programme skips it.
  • The two April campfires whose posts had not been tagged Campfire Learn Together on stimpunks.org are tagged now, so the tag page holds every campfire on the rack.

2026-09-28 — Screen Two’s rack, and racks that fold away

  • Screen Two at the Lightbulb Picture House has a rack now, like the others: a card for every film saying who made it, how long it runs, what it is about and what is in it before you press play, written from the films’ own captions. Each card can play its film in place or up on Screen Two.
  • Every rack folds away. They start open, so nothing on the page is hidden until you choose to fold it, and folding one is a single press on its heading. It works without scripts.

2026-09-28 — A card's film on the big screen

  • Every card on a rack at the Lightbulb Picture House can now put its film on that rack’s own screen, in place of the whole programme: Screen One for the first rack, Screen Three for the campfire rack. The button says how long the film runs before you press it, like every other press here.
  • Pressing it takes you to the screen, which says what is now showing, so the film does not start somewhere you cannot see. Putting the programme back does not start it: the screen goes back to its own press-to-play plate and waits.
  • Without scripts the button is not there at all, rather than being a button that does nothing, and every card still plays its film in place.

2026-09-28 — Screen Three at the Lightbulb Picture House: every campfire

  • The Lightbulb Picture House has a third screen, and on it is everything we have watched together at Campfire Learn Together, our Sunday watch-along, newest first. New campfires go on at the top.
  • It has a rack like Screen One’s: a card for every film saying who made it, how long it runs, what it is about and what is in it before you press play, and which campfire it was watched at, with a link to the post we wrote for that campfire. The words are ours, written from each film’s captions rather than from its title; the few films with no captions are described from their own descriptions and from our posts, and the liner notes say which.
  • Three campfires are listed under the rack and are not on the screen, because what we watched is on Vimeo or has since been made private on YouTube. Leaving them out would make the screen look like the whole record when it is not.
  • Checked before it went up: every film plays and embeds, the whole playlist plays inside a frame on this site with captions on, and nothing reaches YouTube until you press play. The page no longer says how many screens it has.

2026-09-28 — A fire in the fire pit at Swaying Sweetgrass

  • Swaying Sweetgrass had a fire pit with talks round it and no fire in it. Now it has one: a whole playlist of real campfires filmed in the forest by Norwegian Fireplace and Campfire, on one large screen the width of the clearing. You hear the crackle, the wind and birds. There is no voice and no music, and the channel says none of it is looped. Nothing loads until you press it.
  • It is the one thing in the clearing that does not say how long it runs, because a playlist the channel keeps adding to has no length. The room says so beside it, and the sentence that said every label there gives a runtime has been rewritten to be true again. Everything told at the fire still says how long before you press.
  • The screen shows no fire until you press it. Before that it is drawn in the meadow’s own colours, because the fire in this room is small and far off and is not what lights the page. The videos’ own titles sell them for sleep and focus. The room repeats none of that.
  • Checked before it went up: the first video in the list plays and embeds, and the whole list plays inside a frame on this site. It is credited in the liner notes and is the first thing on the Now Playing poster for that room.

2026-09-28 — A way in for rooms built with your own AI

  • A room somebody builds with their AI from Your Room’s prompt can now come onto the street through one tool, tools/import-room.py. It reads the note the prompt asks the AI to leave at the top of the page, checks everything it says against the page itself, and holds the room to the same promises every room here keeps. The dial has to work, every word has to be readable against what is actually behind it, nothing plays by itself, nothing is sent or kept, and everything borrowed is credited, the AI included.
  • It measures the room as it would be published, not as it was sent, with the same checks the rest of the street runs, and it sends a room back with every reason at once rather than quietly changing it. The fix belongs where the person and their AI can see it.
  • Sending a new version is importing it again. The room is replaced whole and nothing else moves. What the tool will not do is make a room’s share card or its job for the Adventurer’s Guild: those are still made by hand for every room, and the street will not publish one without them.
  • The prompt on Your Room now asks for that note in an exact shape, a name and a description for the door, and the preview dial in parts that can be lifted out cleanly.

2026-09-28 — Your Room, built with your own AI

  • Your Room has a prompt you can give Claude, or whichever AI you use. It asks you about the room first, then builds it as one page you can keep changing until it is right, and you send us the result. The prompt carries the street’s rules with it: the dial, contrast you can read, nothing that plays by itself, nothing sent or kept, no image generators, and credits for everything borrowed. It also leaves a note at the top of the page saying what we need to bring the room onto the street.
  • It tells the AI to use your own words for anything about you, and not to write your story for you. The page says plainly that whatever you tell an AI goes to the company that runs it, that you do not need one, and that a room made with an AI is credited as such, the way How this site is made says how we use one.

2026-09-28 — A correction in the licence file: not every typeface is OFL

  • The site’s licence file said every typeface here keeps the SIL Open Font License, and that was wrong. Most do, and some are under the Apache License 2.0. It now says so, and points at the Foundry, where each family’s licence is read off its own record rather than typed in.

2026-09-28 — How this site is made, and how we use AI

  • A new page, How this site is made, like the design pages our sibling sites keep. It says why there is no design system here, what every room shares anyway, how the dial works, how contrast is measured, why nothing plays until you press it, and how the site is built and checked.
  • It says how we use AI, in one place and plainly. Ryan Boren and Helen Edgar decide what the street is. Claude, an AI model made by Anthropic, writes nearly all of the code and drafts most of the words, and the drawings are code it writes. No picture here came out of an image generator, no voice here was made by an AI, and nothing a visitor does is sent to one. The page also says which mistakes a language model makes and how the site catches them.
  • Every page’s sign-off links to it, beside the liner notes, so the account is one press away wherever you are.

2026-09-27 — Other people on the Slake

  • The MUD Room is multi-user now, through the CB. Signed on at the Community Center, press Be seen out here beside the map, and you meet whoever else is seen in the same place and hear what is said there. It is off every time you arrive and is never remembered.
  • Seeing is the same switch as being seen, so nobody can watch the Slake without being on it. You meet people only in the place you are in: the map shows you and nobody else, who is here is said in names in alphabetical order, and nothing anywhere lists or counts who is out there.
  • Each place has its own talk, on the radio’s rules. say something, or start with a quote mark: the latest ten things said in a place stay until midnight in Colorado, and the base station can take one off the air sooner. Your tide is still your own, so somebody else waiting does not flood your mud.
  • The privacy page changed first and says exactly what is sent and kept: only while you are seen and the tab is in front, one record of which place you are in, replaced when you move rather than added to, shown to nobody thirty seconds after your last check and deleted soon after. Where you have been is not kept anywhere. It also now says, as it should have, that leaving a pebble sends your pass.

2026-09-27 — A correction in the MUD Room: the CB is on

  • The MUD Room opened saying the CB was built and not yet switched on, and that was wrong. The CB has been on since it was built. The room now says what is actually true: the multi-user half of the Slake is not built yet, and it will run on the CB and its pass.

2026-09-27 — The MUD Room, and the Slake out its back door

  • A new shopfront on the street: the MUD Room, a boot room with a multi-user dungeon out of its back door and nobody to fight in it. Out there is the Slake, a tidal estuary made of words and drawn as a map: a sea wall, a bird hide, a reedbed, a saltmarsh, a tea hut with nothing to buy, a tide mill, a hut full of things found in the mud, a boathouse, a foreshore, a mudflat with a slide, a ferry, a causeway and an island with a striped beacon on it. Go where you like by typing or by pressing. Every button does what a word does.
  • The people out there are each doing something our glossary has an entry for, and if you talk to them twice they tell you its name, linked: two watchers in the hide sitting in parallel play, a warden learning a place by coming back to it, a miller in monotropic time, a boatbuilder whose way of learning is constructionism, a ferry keeper for whom teaching goes both ways, and a mudlark’s finds table that is an affinity space. Bring Ada something off the foreshore and it goes on the table. Nothing quizzes you.
  • Nothing is counted, and the tide turns when you wait for it. No score, no points, no levels and no count of how much of the Slake you have seen. The tide is in or out until you say wait somewhere dry, and it covers the mud and opens the ferry when it comes in. Say home and you are home. The tool that builds it refuses a way on with no way back, and a place with no walk home at any tide it can be reached at.
  • It is words first. Everything that happens is written into a log a screen reader reads out line by line, the map is a picture of the same words, and with scripts off the whole Slake is a guidebook at the foot of the page whose ways on are links.
  • It has one user so far. The other half is not built yet, and it will run on the CB and its pass. Two things about it are settled already, Ryan’s calls: you are seen by your CB handle only if you choose to be, and each place on the Slake gets a channel of its own under the CB’s rules.
  • Castoro and JetBrains Mono, both under the OFL. Small screens and Print moved to §71 and §72. There is a job for the Adventurer’s Guild by the back door.

2026-09-27 — Rebellion Rave Room, turning 08, and the Cooldown Room beside it

  • A new turning off The Outskirts: the Rebellion Rave Room, in an old aircraft hangar at the end of the airstrip road, taken over by the sensory seekers in our community who wanted somewhere to stim loudly and brightly. Scaffolding up every wall, a DJ booth two floors up, a lift to every deck, and festival films on the big screen over the floor.
  • It is the one room on the street with a strobe in it, and that reverses a rule this street has kept since Club Chronic. The reversal is an opt-in and nothing else. The rig is off on every visit. The strobe takes two presses, and the second answers a question that tells you the rate first. Escape, the Stop buttons, another tab or leaving the page switch everything off at once, and nothing about it is remembered. The knob starts at four flashes a second, the most the Health and Safety Executive recommends for a club, and stops at eleven. The warning in the ante-chamber gives the Epilepsy Society’s figures before you ever reach the switch. The flash is white and lands only on the picture of the hangar, unless you choose to take the rig full screen.
  • The Adventurer’s Guild said Club Chronic’s rule held everywhere on this street, and that is not true any more, so its house rule now names the one room where it does not. Every other room’s own promise that nothing in it flashes still holds for that room.
  • The films on the big screen have their own lighting in them, strobe included, and the room says so above the screen, because the rig’s limits cannot reach inside somebody else’s video. The Tomorrowland film is not the festival’s own: a channel called Best Stage Tomorrowland posted it the day before, and the room says so beside it.
  • Through the other door in the ante-chamber: the Cooldown Room, small and soft, with couches, cold water, and a vending machine of snacks made by people from round here. Everything in it is free, every slot says what is in it and how it feels before you press, and nothing is counted or kept.
  • Big Shoulders Stencil and Exo 2 in the hangar, Sour Gummy and Kumbh Sans in the Cooldown Room, all under the OFL. Small screens and Print moved to §70 and §71.

2026-09-27 — The fractal window is on in the Faery Yurt
