  • Helen Edgar said yes, so the Faery Yurt has the fractal window now. Its switch is in the sign-off like every other room’s. It draws a lantern of tracery in her own colours, from the hearth’s dark through timber and tallow to candle, and it never flashes.
  • It is the fourth thing this site has added to her room, after the one colour, the candle stub and the pebble bowl, and the second she asked for. The liner notes say so too. Stay Frosty is still the one room without the window, because its own page promises there is nothing to press.

2026-09-27 — A fractal window in (almost) every room

  • Every page’s sign-off has a new switch: Switch on the fractal window. It opens a small window of its own in the corner of the screen with a slow fractal in it, drawn in your browser in that room’s own colours. No two rooms draw the same picture: Julia sets, the edge of the Mandelbrot set, Newton fractals, a fern, a dragon curve, a crystal. The window has its own speed control (Slow, Steady, Quick), moves between corners, goes full screen, and closes when you switch it off or leave the room.
  • It is not synced to the music, and it says so before you press. Almost everything that plays here plays inside another site’s frame, and a page is not allowed to listen to another site’s frame. The one plain audio player streams from a host that does not let other sites read the sound. So the window is a picture that runs beside the music.
  • It follows the Arcade, not the dial. It runs at every setting because the motion is the thing you switched on, and the dial is not wired to it. At Gentle it starts at Slow.
  • It never flashes, and that is enforced in its code. Every frame, no pixel is allowed to change brightness, or redness, faster than a fixed rate. That keeps every spot well under the three flashes a second that the accessibility rule for seizures (WCAG 2.3.1) allows. Played without that limit, most rooms’ pictures would flash at Quick. A test counts flashes in what every room’s window actually paints, using its own arithmetic, before anything is published.
  • Nothing is fetched until you press. The window’s script and stylesheet download on the first press and not before. It stores nothing and sends nothing.
  • Two rooms do not have it yet, on purpose. Stay Frosty promises on its own page that there is nothing to press in it and nothing moves, so a switch there would make that false. And the Faery Yurt is Helen Edgar’s room: a picture in her colours changes how her room reads, which is hers to decide.

2026-09-27 — Somebody else’s cellars, to leave on, in Big Steep

  • Big Steep Fermentables has three long cellar ambience videos to have running while you are down there: an underground wine cellar, a wine cellar with dripping water and a crackling fire, and a speakeasy wine lounge with soft jazz. Each says how long it runs before you press it, and nothing loads from YouTube until you do.
  • They are not this cellar, and the room says so: there are barrels, wood and fire in them, and none of that is in Big Steep, which is glass and steel on bare rock. Their titles are their makers’.
  • The speakeasy plays on into YouTube’s own mix once it finishes, Ryan’s call. Its label gives the speakeasy’s runtime and then says the mix has none and that nobody here has checked what it plays.

2026-09-26 — Glow Go Gee Gaws opens on level B3

  • Glow Go Gee Gaws is open in the caverns: glow-in-the-dark toys and trinkets, in every colour glow comes in, blue included. Its button on the lift panel is lit, and every room down there is open now except Buoyant Bodyminds.
  • Every toy is dark until you charge it. Hold one up to the rail and it glows, then gives the light back until it has none; or hold everything up at once and watch which colours let go first. Green lasts longest and red and orange go first, which is true of the real pigments; the order in between is ours.
  • The glow stick is the odd one out: it is chemistry, not stored light, so you bend it once and it glows until you leave.
  • A change to the caverns’ plan, in the open. The brief had this room glowing orange only and ruled out blue as Light It Up Blue. Ryan’s call reversed both: Light It Up Blue shouldn’t take blue away from us, and other rooms shouldn’t limit what this room does. Nothing here flickers at any setting, and at Gentle a toy stays lit for its time and goes dark in one step, with the same words.

2026-09-26 — Derek Sarno cooks mushrooms in Mycelium Munchies

  • Mycelium Munchies has Derek Sarno’s mushroom cooking videos: his whole Mushroom Masterclass playlist on one press, and the most recent videos in it in a rack, newest first, each saying how long it runs before you press it. Nothing loads from YouTube until you do.
  • Want to grow your own? The room points at North Spore and their guide to using a mushroom fruiting block kit. It is a shop, which is unusual for a link here; we are not affiliated with them and the link carries no tracking.
  • The rack is a copy of the playlist taken on 26 September 2026, and says so. His titles are quoted as he wrote them, and everything is credited in the liner notes.

2026-09-26 — Big Steep Fermentables opens on level B1

  • Big Steep Fermentables is open at the top of the caverns: a brewery and cellar in the rock for wine, beer and kombucha, lit from behind so the light comes through the drinks. Its button on the lift panel is lit.
  • Try our house styles: three beers, two wines and two kombuchas, and every one comes as two pours. The alcohol-free pour comes first and is the same size, because it is not the lesser one.
  • Every pour says how strong it is before you take it. The cellar says what alcohol-free means for a beer or a wine, and that kombucha is never quite zero: its fermented pour gives the most it can be, and its alcohol-free pour is the tea not fermented, with none at all.
  • Nobody asks why you are not drinking, nothing counts how many, and nothing claims to be good for you. The pours with alcohol in them are for adults, and everything else is for everybody.

2026-09-26 — Mycelium Munchies opens on level B2

  • Mycelium Munchies is open in Arlesglad Caverns: a mushroom farm with a restaurant at the end of the growing rooms, lit by the heat lamps over the pass. Its button on the lift panel is lit.
  • On the pass: crispy fried oyster mushrooms, lion’s mane nuggets and mushroom steaks. The rest of the menu serves every mushroom the farm grows, all kinds of ways, and every dish says what is in it before you order.
  • Everything on a plate was grown here, and the growing rooms say what each mushroom looks like on the shelf, what it is like cooked, and what it grows on. Nothing is served raw.
  • Nothing here helps anybody identify a wild mushroom, nothing is named in Latin, and nothing is sold as good for your brain. A mushroom here is dinner. The room is here because of a line of our own about composting, and it links the post it comes from.

2026-09-26 — Stay Breezy remembers your usual

  • Stay Breezy now remembers your usual: the speed you last set on each fan, which fan is on your table, and which fans you last had on. It is kept in your own browser and nowhere else, and the privacy page lists it.
  • It never switches a fan on by itself. Your speeds come back when you arrive, and the fans start only when you press Switch on my usual, which says which fans and speeds before you press it. Forget my usual clears it.

2026-09-26 — Stay Breezy, a room full of fans behind Stay Frosty

  • Stay Breezy is through the door at the back of Stay Frosty: a pale hall full of fans, for anybody who loves moving air and the noise it makes. It is Ryan’s, and his words for why are on the page.
  • Pick a fan off the floor and switch it on, or as many as you like, each with three speeds. Or take a room of your own, with its own ceiling fan and a table to perch a fan on; it has a door, so the hall’s fans go off while you are in it.
  • Every fan says what it feels like and what it sounds like before you switch it on, and nothing makes a sound until you do. The sound is made in your own browser and none of it is a recording. Switching more fans on makes the hall fuller, never louder than its slider says.
  • Every ceiling fan’s lamp hangs below its blades, so nothing flickers. At Gentle the blades stay still and the fans keep running. Stay Frosty itself is still silent, and says so beside its new door.

2026-09-26 — Stay Frosty opens at the bottom of the caverns

  • Stay Frosty is the first room open in Arlesglad Caverns, at the bottom of the lift. Its button on the panel is lit.
  • A quiet place to chill, literally: chill as in calm, not cold. It keeps the National Park Service’s measurement for Carlsbad’s lowest point, 68°F (20°C) all year, and says where that came from on its thermometer.
  • Nothing to press, nothing moves at any setting, nothing makes a sound, and nothing in there is for anything. It is not cold therapy, the room counts and keeps nothing, and the CB radio stays upstairs.
  • It is lit only by reflections: the handrail’s light reaches the bottom as a point in every bead of damp on the rock. Credits are in the liner notes.

2026-09-26 — Arlesglad Caverns, under the street

  • Arlesglad Caverns is a new area under the street, for everything that needs things to stay the same. The way down is a lift beside the stoop with the stair beside it, and the lift is the ordinary way, not the alternative to the stair.
  • The only light down there is the handrail: one strip at hand height along the route, so the rock is lit where the path goes and nowhere else. Nothing in the passage moves at any setting.
  • The lift’s panel is the area’s board, one button for every room: Big Steep Fermentables, Mycelium Munchies, Glow Go Gee Gaws, Buoyant Bodyminds and, at the bottom, Stay Frosty. None of them is open yet, and each unlit button says so rather than doing nothing when pressed.
  • The name is a play on Carlsbad Caverns, with a glad spin and with ARLES in it, the Stimpunks Design Method, whose ladder starts at the bottom. Stay Frosty’s 68°F is the National Park Service’s own measurement of Carlsbad’s lowest point, credited on the page and in the liner notes.
  • The front page, the map and the Adventurer’s Guild know about it: a lift head on the pavement, a lower board at the foot of the model, and a job that sends you down. The guild’s class III now names all three ways off the street, and it did not grow a fourth class for the one that goes down.

2026-09-26 — Ryan’s welcome in the Hermitage

  • The Solarpunk Hermitage is Ryan’s place, and it now opens with his portrait and a short welcome, the way Helen’s Faery Yurt does.
  • The photograph is the one on Enid’s wall, with Ryan’s own description. It hangs in a cedar frame like the rest of the cabin’s woodwork, and is not cropped or filtered, which is the wall’s promise wherever one of its photographs is hung.

2026-09-26 — A sound system at the Hermitage’s campfire

  • The campfire has a sound system: a long low console against the wall behind the chairs, with a tall speaker at each end, a subwoofer in the console’s cupboard, an amplifier and pre-amp, a CD player and a turntable, all drawn in the room’s own colours.
  • Ryan’s records are beside it, each a whole album: John Coltrane and Johnny Hartman, Charles Mingus’s The Black Saint and the Sinner Lady, Pink Floyd’s The Dark Side of the Moon in its 2023 remaster, and AURORA’s What Happened to the Heart?
  • Every record says how long it runs before you press it, like every channel on the television, because an album is finished and has a length. Nothing plays until you press.
  • Where the performers wrote none of the songs, the songwriters are named on the record, and the years are first releases rather than the dates on the uploads. All of it is credited in the liner notes.

2026-09-26 — A speaker in the Hermitage’s cave

  • The Solarpunk Hermitage’s cave has a speaker at the foot of it, tucked into the curtain by the chair, playing Ryan’s playlist Oblivion Slip: Erik Satie, Philip Glass, Steve Reich, Brian Eno, Nils Frahm and others. It is one of the stimming and coping playlists on our Our Stims page.
  • Nothing loads until you press it, like every other player on the street. It starts at the top, in Ryan’s order.
  • It says no runtime, on purpose. Every documentary at the campfire says how long it runs before you press it. A playlist keeps growing, so any total would soon be wrong, and the button says it runs until you stop it.
  • It is credited in the liner notes, and Now Playing shows it as what the Hermitage puts on first.

2026-09-26 — Squelch on the CB

  • With Speak new messages aloud on, every message now comes over the air the way it would on a CB: a burst of static as the station keys up, the words, and the tail of static as it lets go. Messages take turns, one station at a time.
  • The static follows the dial and the words never do. At Gentle the radio reads the message with no static round it. There is never static without a message being spoken, so with Speak off the radio is as silent as it always was.
  • It is made in the page, out of filtered noise, and nothing is fetched to make it.
  • Your browser only lets a page make a sound after you have pressed or typed something on it, so after walking into a new room the radio says so rather than going quiet without saying why.

2026-09-26 — The CB can speak

  • A new switch on the radio, Speak new messages aloud, for those of us not using a screen reader. Switch it on and the radio reads each new message out as it comes in, handle first, in your own device’s voice, which is what a CB sounds like. The screen reader switch is still there and still separate, and it is labelled New messages to a screen reader now, so the two cannot be mistaken for each other.
  • It is off until you turn it on, it never reads out what was already on the channel when you tuned in or came back to the tab, it never reads your own messages back to you, and folding the radio away stops it mid-word.
  • Only voices on your own device. Some browsers offer voices that are a speech service somewhere else, which would send the words on the channel to a third party. The radio will not use those, and the privacy page says so.

2026-09-26 — The Doom Scoop: daily news, usually bad

  • A new shopfront, The Doom Scoop: an ice cream parlour after closing, with the only light coming from the dipping cabinet, and a tub in the cabinet for every morning of the last week. Each tub holds what Ryan’s sources put out between five the morning before and five that morning, Mountain time: MeidasTouch for the independent news, A Closer Look, the Late Night monologues, Jimmy Kimmel Live and The Daily Show for late night, and Jason Salmon, Texas Paul, Tennessee Brando, Lawyer Oyer and Hawk’s Podcasts for the YouTubers.
  • A week, and then it is gone. The eighth morning is not archived. It goes from here and stays on the channel that posted it. Every morning is a flavour, and the flavour is the day of the week.
  • Nothing plays until you press it, and every scoop says how long it runs, when it went up and whose channel it is on before you do. Shorts play upright. A video whose channel will not let it be shown on another site is a door out to YouTube, and says so.
  • Nobody here watches any of it first. A timer fills the cabinet every morning, and the page says when. The headlines are the channels’ own, word for word. Nothing is ranked or counted.
  • Ground News and MeidasTouch News publish no feed a timer can read, so they are doors on the counter rather than tubs in the cabinet.

2026-09-26 — A wicker basket in the Faery Yurt, Helen’s first pebble, and her soundtrack

  • The Faery Yurt’s soundtrack is no longer coming soon. It is Helen’s own playlist, Faery Yurt’s Yarn, from her own channel: one press puts it on and it runs until you stop it, and nothing reaches YouTube before the press. The songs on it are listed underneath, with who plays them and how long each runs, as the playlist stood the day it was read. Every one was checked that day: it plays, and it may be embedded here, and the whole list was played from inside the yurt before it went up.
  • The yurt is on Now Playing, the bill on the poster column, which noticed on its own that the room had something to play.
  • Helen Edgar filled her basket, and asked for it to be a basket: a wicker one, drawn in her own browns with a handle over the rim, with each pebble a smooth stone sitting in it. Press a stone and it takes you where it goes. The name on a stone is a real link, so it reads and tabs like any other.
  • The first pebble is the amethyst cave at Jupiter Artland, near Edinburgh: Anya Gallaccio’s The Light Pours Out Of Me, a 30-second video on the Yoga & Healing Fairy channel, which is not Helen’s. The line on the stone is hers, and so is the wish for a magical glimmer day.
  • The basket’s look is the yurt’s own. The Hermitage’s basket stays a list: two rooms do not share a basket any more than they share anything else. Nothing in it moves at any setting.

2026-09-26 — Pebbles by the door, in the Hermitage and the Faery Yurt

  • The Solarpunk Hermitage and the Faery Yurt each have pebbles by the door. A basket of things the host has put out for anybody to take, and a bowl for leaving a pebble of your own: a line in your own words, and a link if the thing is somewhere else. It was Helen Edgar’s idea, the day she came to the Hermitage to watch a film.
  • Anybody can see what is in a bowl; leaving a pebble takes a CB sign-on, and a pebble stays seven days, the chalkboard’s rules. A link shows its whole address, so what you read is where it goes. The privacy page says what the bowls keep.
  • Nothing is counted: not what was taken, not what was left, not who came to the door. Leaving a pebble is the only footprint there is, and nobody has to leave one. Both baskets are empty until their hosts fill them.

2026-09-26 — The CB’s teleporter

  • “CH 19” on the CB’s bar is a Teleport button now. Press it and a list of every room on the street opens under the bar; type to narrow it, use the arrow keys to move, and press Enter to go. The room you are in says you are here. Escape puts it away.
  • Teleporting is an ordinary link: it sends nothing to the channel and tells nobody where you went. The button hides while the radio is folded away, because folded sends nothing at all.

2026-09-25 — The CB can be made small

  • A Small button on the CB’s bar shrinks the radio to the bar and the newest message, so you can keep an eye on the channel while you watch something in a room without the radio covering the screen. It stays tucked into whichever corner you put it in, and it stays small until you press the button again, even after you move to another room.
  • Small is still switched on: it hears the channel exactly as the full-size radio does, and a screen reader still hears what arrives. To send a message, make it full size again. Folding it away is still the way to switch it off.

2026-09-25 — The CB: rooms by #name, and a log that stays put

  • Name a room on the CB with a #. Type # and the start of a room’s name, like #den, pick it from the list, and everybody on the channel sees #The Den, linked, so you can ask somebody to come and find you there. It works from the keyboard: arrows to move, Enter or Tab to pick, Escape to leave your # alone. The list is every page on the street, in the street’s own order, and a new room is on it the day it opens.
  • A room’s full address in a message, like stimpunks.world/the-den.html, is a link too. An address anywhere else stays plain words you can copy, because the channel is strangers’ words and it should not be able to send anybody off the street without their noticing.
  • Scrolling up the channel stays where you put it. The radio used to jump back to the newest message every few seconds; now it only follows new messages when you were already at the bottom, or when you have just sent one.
  • The radio fetches the list of rooms from this site once, the first time it listens. The privacy page says so.

2026-09-25 — The Broadsheet Broadside’s sheets are CC0

  • Every sheet on the press is dedicated to the public domain under CC0 1.0, like the Stimpunks broadsides they are modelled on: print a stack and give them away, with no permission needed and no credit required. Each sheet says so on the paper, and asks for its credits to stay on it anyway.
  • The dedication covers the sheets’ words and layout and nothing that is not ours: the typefaces keep their own licences, and Danny the Street’s name is DC Comics’. The room around the sheets stays under this street’s licence, CC BY-SA 4.0, and the licence file says which is which.
  • Printing a sheet no longer comes out with blank pages in it when you are signed on to the CB, or when a browser extension has added something to the page.

2026-09-25 — The Broadsheet Broadside, and its first sheet

  • A new shopfront on the street, The Broadsheet Broadside: manifestos and mementos served across your bow. A broadsheet press in the side of a ship, printing broadsides the way the Stimpunks broadsides and whysheet.press do: one sheet, printed both sides, ink on white, on a page that fits both A4 and US Letter. Side A carries one thing you can read across a room, to pin up; side B does the work, to hand over.
  • No. 1 is an introduction to Stimpunks.World: one street with no two rooms alike, the dial, nothing playing until you press, where to go, what stayed careful while the street got loud, and whose idea the street is. Print the page, or press the button over the sheet, and only the sheet comes out, one side to a page.
  • The storefront is the side of a ship in the Nelson chequer, seen from a boat at the waterline, with a sheet of paper in every open port where a gun would be. No gun is drawn, nothing is fired, and nothing moves at any setting. The only light is the sun thrown back up off the sea. The ship’s name is painted in Bodoni Moda; the sheets are set in Atkinson Hyperlegible Next and Mono, the faces the Stimpunks broadsides use. All three are read off their own records and are on the Foundry’s shelves and in the Mopery’s picker.
  • Every sheet is checked by printing it. The street’s print check now renders the press to a real PDF and refuses anything but two pages a sheet in the sheet’s own two inks; its first run found the page margins printing in Chrome’s dark canvas, which is fixed. The press’s own tool refuses a sheet that is not two sides, a claim line too long to read across a room, two sheets sharing a pair of inks, a link that does not read as its own address, and a sheet that tells its reader what they must do.
  • A job marker, a share card, the map, and a slot on the Community Center’s service board, because the press takes ideas for its next sheet.

2026-09-25 — The Live Room, and Looming Rocks goes outdoors

  • A new shopfront on the street, The Live Room: a recording studio seen from the control room, over the desk, through the glass, with the red light on. It is for sessions played in a studio rather than at a gig, and it opens with three studios: Austin City Limits, a television studio with a crowd in it, and the live rooms of two radio stations, KEXP in Seattle and KCRW in Santa Monica. The desk takes suggestions.
  • Every session is a channel strip, its name written in marker on masking tape, with where and when it was taped, whose channel it is on, a line of ours, and a play button that says how long it runs before you press it. Nothing reaches YouTube until you press. Every video was checked on its own watch page first, and every date and venue on a strip was read off that video’s own description. One Austin City Limits upload is titled a full concert and has six songs cut from it, and its strip says so before the press.
  • The glass is two panes set out of parallel, the way studio glass is, so everything lit in the live room is drawn twice, the second time faint. The red lamp is steady at every setting and nothing in the room moves. Nobody is drawn in the live room. B612, drawn for aircraft cockpit displays, carries everything you read; Just Another Hand writes the names on the tape. Both are read off their own records and are on the Foundry’s shelves and in the Mopery’s picker.
  • Looming Rocks is for outdoor music in general now, not only for Red Rocks, and Noah Kahan’s set at Austin City Limits Music Festival 2023, in Zilker Park, is on its bill. Every act says where it was played, and the room’s tool refuses one that was not played outdoors. That rule arrived in the same message as nine Austin City Limits television tapings, which are indoors, so they went to The Live Room, whose tool refuses the opposite: a session that was not played indoors.
  • Three of Looming Rocks’ notes had stopped being true as the bill grew, and they were fixed: one said a film was last on the bill when it was sixth, one said the oldest show was nearly forty years older than the rest when it is thirty-four, and one said nobody else on the bill had been born yet, which was not true of every band on it.
  • A job marker, a share card, the map, a slot on the Community Center’s service board, and a place on the Now Playing poster.

2026-09-25 — Cavendish Coworking: the Hang Suites, up the stairs

  • Cavendish Coworking has Hang Suites, for when you just want to hang out or work on something together: the Cave, a quiet room for co-presence with no spoken chat; the Campfire, for small groups working, watching or listening together; and the Watering Hole, for come-and-go chat where random and off-topic are on-topic. Each is a Proton Meet room behind its own door.
  • Every door says how things go in there before you knock, in the room’s own norms, and none of them has a time on it: they are drop-in rooms. Lurking with your camera and mic off is fine in all of them.
  • They are named for David Thornburg’s caves, campfires and watering holes, the three kinds of Cavendish Space, and the room says whose they are. A note under them, on communication access: you do not need to speak to belong here.

2026-09-25 — Cavendish Coworking is Henry Cavendish’s house now, and every door in it is shut

  • Cavendish Coworking opened this afternoon as a glass office with every door propped wide, and that was the wrong idea of open. Ryan’s correction: transparency does not mean an open floor plan and always-open doors.
  • It is a Georgian house now, after the one in Bedford Square where Cavendish kept his library and opened it for borrowing: green throughout, and every door a shut mahogany door in a stone case with a fanlight, a brass plate and a note saying what is behind it and when. None of them is locked. You decide whether to knock.
  • The blinds are down, and you can see through them. That house’s mahogany blinds were lined with green transparent canvas, so the shelves show through them in the drawing. And its rules were his too: he signed the register for every book he took out of his own library.
  • Everything about his houses is from Christa Jungnickel and Russell McCormmach’s Cavendish: The Experimental Life, credited on the page. The type is Caslon for reading as well as for the name, his century’s letter; Inter has left the street. The doors, their words and their times are unchanged, and the tool that builds them now refuses the open-plan vocabulary too.

2026-09-25 — Cavendish Coworking: every door propped open onto one of our calls

  • A new shopfront on the street, Cavendish Coworking: glass all the way along, and every door in it opens straight onto a Proton Meet room. Our Operations and Editorial meetings are open to the world, and until now the only way in was our Discord, which a great many people will not use. The room our events meet in is there too.
  • No Discord, no account and nothing to install. Each door was opened as far as Proton’s guest page and no further: it asks for a name, names the room so you can check it, and lets you in with your microphone and camera off. The room says that Proton’s own terms apply over there, before you press.
  • They are doors and not windows, and that was measured. Proton Meet only lets Proton’s own apps frame it, and this street gives no page your camera or microphone, so a call inside the page would have been a blank box. Nothing reaches Proton until you press a door.
  • Every line on a door is our events page’s, word for word, and the times are Central as that page gives them, with the same hour worked out on your own clock underneath, in your browser. Nothing is stored or sent. A new tool builds the doors, reads the events page again every time, and refuses a line that has gone from it, a door without its room’s password, a door out of the page’s order, and the call framed in the page. Broken on purpose first; all eight caught.
  • Named for Cavendish Space, and Henry Cavendish, who wanted to stand off to the side soaking everything in: there is a chair by the wall for anybody who would rather come in that way. Libre Caslon Display and Inter, both OFL and read off their own records, on the Foundry’s shelves and in the Mopery’s picker. A job marker, a share card, the map and a street door.

2026-09-25 — Plural Mural’s chalkboard: a note for whoever comes by next

  • Plural Mural has a chalkboard leaning against the foot of its wall, on the pavement and not on the paint. It is Helen Edgar’s idea, after the board at her floatation tank place: somewhere to leave a note for whoever comes by next.
  • Anybody can read it, and only people signed on to the CB can write on it. That is what keeps it in the community’s hands. The box you write in says, above it, that the board is public.
  • A note stays up for seven days, and the board holds thirty, so a full board loses its oldest note. The base station can rub one out sooner. There is no archive and no copy, and every read asks search engines not to keep what is on it.
  • The privacy page changed first. It has a section on the chalkboard now, and every sentence on it that said the CB was the only thing that sends anything, or that the pass only travels when the radio listens, says what is true with a chalkboard in it. The Community Center’s account of the radio says it too, since the same sign-on writes on the board.
  • Three small functions beside the CB’s, sharing its code: one to read the board, one to write on it and one for the base to rub out. They use the same conditional write as the channel, which was tested with fifteen people chalking at once: every note told it had gone up was on the board. Nothing a person chalks is logged, and no address is kept.

2026-09-25 — The Playhouse’s secret word: a new one every day, and the room screams

  • The Playhouse’s secret word changes every day now, at your own midnight, working through a rota of words out of our glossary. It said MONOTROPISM and nothing else since the street opened. The whole rota is printed under the sign, so a page with no scripts still has every word.
  • And it does what a secret word is for: when anybody in the room says it, everybody screams. The toys talk, so when Chairy, the word clock, the telephone or the infodump says today’s word, the scream goes on the end of the sentence and the room makes a noise it makes nowhere else: five voices, not the yell button’s one. Or press SAY IT OUT LOUD on the sign. The words are the same at every setting; at MAX GLITTER the sign also jumps. Nothing flashes, and nothing is counted: no tally of screams, no streak of days.
  • make-toys.py builds the sign and refuses a word nothing in the room ever says, because a secret word no toy can say only screams when somebody presses the sign, which is not the joke. It matches with the same pattern the script does, so the two cannot disagree about a hit. Broken on purpose first: an unsaid word, a word on the rota twice and a word that is not one word were all refused.
  • The bit is Pee-Wee’s Playhouse’s and the sign says so on its face, and also that Groucho Marx had a secret word first, on You Bet Your Life. The first draft of the credit said Paul Reubens made it up, which is how an idea’s history gets shorter.

2026-09-25 — Community Library, L-Space and Oook: a public library, the space behind it, and a world behind that

  • A new shopfront on the street: the Community Library, our Library in this street’s clothes, and it opens with a celebration of the public library: free to all, a patron and not a customer, the front desk, a palace for the people, Ranganathan’s five laws, library joy, and the library economy. Our collections are laid out on the front desk. Nothing requires a card. It is a public reading room at eleven in the morning with its windows set high over the shelves and the sky in every one of them, which makes it the one room indoors on this street where you can see the sky.
  • Behind it, through a gap in the stacks: L-Space, Terry Pratchett’s idea that every library is joined to every other by the weight of what is in them. A deep violet room where every shelf bends towards the well in the middle and the only way through is a ball of string, with the anti-library, Books of the Edges, and threads from our glossary to pull.
  • And at the far end of the string: Oook, a Discworld homage in the Librarian’s own colours, rust and banana. The Disc on its elephants and its turtle, the rules of L-space, and why we find the Librarian of Unseen University relatable: his body changed, he found it suited the work, he declined to be changed back, and he kept his job because he was the only one who knew where all the books were. He is not drawn anywhere on this site.
  • Every quotation in the three rooms says how it was checked, and for every one the answer is: read off our Library page, not against the book. Two of them have no source on that page at all and say so where they stand. Our Library page links the 2000 graphic adaptation of Guards! Guards! for lines out of the 1989 novel; these rooms link the novel, and the tool refuses the other record. The Linda Lindas’ line and the pictures of the Librarian stay on our Library page, because a lyric and those licences are things this site cannot carry.
  • Marcellus SC and Andika in the library, Gravitas One and Source Serif 4 in L-space, Sniglet and Libre Baskerville in Oook, all under the SIL Open Font License and on the Foundry’s shelves and in the Mopery’s picker. A job marker in each room, a share card for each, a street door, and the map now draws a room behind a room behind a room.
  • GNU Terry Pratchett. Every page on the street now sends X-Clacks-Overhead: GNU Terry Pratchett, the clacks tradition from Going Postal that people began keeping in their websites’ headers after he died: send it on, do not log it, turn it round at the end. Oook says so, and the line in _headers says Oook depends on it.
  • New: community-library.html, l-space.html, oook.html, data/library.json, tools/make-library.py.

2026-09-25 — Plural Mural: an end wall that will not stay one colour

  • A new shopfront on the street: Plural Mural, an end wall with a mural on it, and then another. The street is Danny’s, and Danny repaints. The first murals are the lines of the street’s own tagline, one to a wall: queer without fear, interdependent and here, divergent and proud, living out loud, plucky pluralism, and becoming and belonging.
  • At MAX GLITTER the wall repaints itself every three minutes, one mural dissolving into the next, and you can keep the one you like up for as long as you stay. At Gentle and Regular it holds still until you turn it over yourself, because a picture that changes without being asked is something moving on the page. Every mural is listed under the wall in words at every setting, with a button to put it up.
  • Propose a mural of your own, in words, and the house paints it and puts it up in turn, credited to you. Nobody real is painted on the wall, nothing is voted on, and it comes down the moment you ask. It has a slot on the Community Center’s service board.
  • Seen from across the road at golden hour, with the sun behind you and the lamppost’s shadow lying up the wall, the same over every mural. Chango and Commissioner, both under the SIL Open Font License, are on the Foundry’s shelves and in the Mopery’s picker. A job marker, a share card, and a place on the map.
  • New: plural-mural.html, mural.js, data/mural.json, tools/make-mural.py.

2026-09-25 — Now Playing: a poster column beside the Pebble Board

  • Street furniture on the pavement, beside the Pebble Board: a poster column with one bill pasted round it, Now Playing. Every room with something in it to press and play, on the street, behind its doors, in the campgrounds and down the road out of town, with a line about what is on there and what each one puts on first when you get there.
  • What is on first is read off each room’s own page, every time the poster is printed: the first thing in there you can press, with its name and running time exactly as that room prints them. When a room changes what it opens with, the poster changes with it. Where a room puts on a whole playlist, the poster says so, and says what the room says about it.
  • Beside the board and not on it, because the glass holds what our people brought each other, and a bill printed off every room is not a pebble.
  • Nothing on the poster plays, nothing on it is counted, and there is no headliner: every room’s name is the same size, in the order you would walk to them.
  • Screenprinted in two inks and no black, pasted on a painted iron column. Rammetto One and Instrument Sans, both under the SIL Open Font License, are on the Foundry’s shelves and in the Mopery’s picker. A job marker, a share card, and a place on the map.

2026-09-25 — A look of your own, at the Dress-Up Den

  • The Dopamine Dress-Up Den takes looks from our community now. Tell us what you wear, in your own words, and it goes on the peg with your name on it, or as somebody in our community: your words exactly as you give them, prints and colours picked off the rails to match, and anything you wear that is not on a rail yet drawn and hung up for anybody to wear. Words, not a photograph, because nobody’s body goes on the stand. Nothing is rated, and it comes down the moment you ask.
  • It has a slot on the Community Center’s service board, beside everything else the street takes in, held to the room’s own words so the two cannot come to disagree. Taking something back now lists your look as well.

2026-09-25 — Dopamine Dress-Up Den: try it on, and nothing comes in a size

  • A new shopfront on the street: Dopamine Dress-Up Den, a boutique for dopamine dressing. The room and its name are Helen Edgar’s idea. Put an outfit together on a wooden valet stand out of the rails — something on your head, something over the top, something underneath, the bottom half, your feet, and a feather boa or a long scarf — in any print and any colours: florals, big blocks of colour, patchwork, stripes, polka dots, gingham, moons and stars, sequins, or plain. Or press Surprise me.
  • The rails start from Ryan’s wardrobe: Thai fisherman pants, button-down shirts, a haori over the top and beanies and slouchy beanies in every colour there is. Then Helen’s cardigans and flowing dresses, and then the full show, with a big wig, a velvet cape, a sequin gown, platform boots and the boa, for anybody in our community who wants all of it at once. Each of those is a look off the peg you can put on the stand and change. And Crocs, on Ryan’s ask the same day.
  • Every garment says how it feels and how it goes on and comes off, because our glossary defines dopamine dressing as colours, textures and styles, and Ryan wears his pants for sensory and comfort reasons first. The fastenings follow his own field guide page on dressing: every jacket says how you get out of it, and the knot on fisherman pants is not accessible to everybody, so every other bottom half does up without one.
  • Nobody’s body is drawn. The clothes hang on a stand rather than on a paper doll, because a doll is one shape, one skin and one gender handed to everybody who walks in. Nothing comes in a size, no rail is for men or for women, and nothing is rated, kept or sent; the copy button puts what is on the stand on your own clipboard.
  • Black is a colour in there too. Every rail comes in all black, because our Dress Codes entry has a school that banned black clothing on the grounds of mental health, and a room that told people colour was the cure would be that dress code with a nicer name. The room also makes no claim about brain chemistry: dopamine is in the name because that is what people call it.
  • The light is the only light on the street that has been taken apart. The sun comes in the front window white, which lets every colour on the rails look like itself, and a crystal hanging in the window throws small rainbows on the lavender wall. At MAX GLITTER they drift a little as it turns, and nothing flashes or sparkles on its own at any setting. The dial never takes anything off the rails.
  • Two new typefaces: DynaPuff, by Toshi Omagari and Jennifer Daniel, for the sign and the headings, and Plus Jakarta Sans, by Tokotype, for everything you read. Both are on the Foundry’s shelves and in the Mopery’s font picker, with their designers named.
  • Changed: dopamine-dress-up-den.html, dressup.js, data/dressup.json and tools/make-dressup.py (all new), og/dopamine-dress-up-den.png (new), fonts/, index.html, love.css (§52 is the new room; small screens and print are §53 and §54 now), data/quests.json, liner-notes.html, the Foundry and the Mopery’s picker, and the map.

2026-09-24 — A placemat and some crayons at The Small Hours

  • Every table at The Small Hours now has a paper placemat and a sleeve of crayons. Pick a crayon, then press a shape to colour it in, or switch to scribbling and draw wherever you like, over the lines included. The wax has the paper’s tooth in it, so it looks like crayon rather than paint.
  • Every part can be coloured in by name from a list under the placemat, with a keyboard or a screen reader, and each name says what colour that part is now. Scribbling is the one thing that needs a pointer or a finger.
  • Nothing on it is kept. A fresh placemat throws the old one away, and leaving does the same. Take it home saves a picture of it to your own device and sends it nowhere; the house rule saying nothing is kept now says so about the placemat too. tools/make-small-hours.py refuses the room’s script if it ever starts storing, sending or reading the clock.
  • With scripts off, the placemat is still there to print out and colour in with real crayons.
  • Changed: small-hours.html, small-hours.js, data/small-hours.json, tools/make-small-hours.py, love.css (§2 has the paper and the crayons; §44 has the placemat), tools/check-contrast.py, liner-notes.html, README.md, llms.txt, CLAUDE.md.

2026-09-24 — A documentary’s title at the campfire, corrected

  • Corrected: on the Solarpunk Hermitage’s campfire listing and in the liner notes, the title of Coastal Seas showed three stray characters where “World’s” has its apostrophe. YouTube’s own page had it right; our copy of the title had been read in the wrong encoding. It is YouTube’s title again, and tools/make-hermitage.py now refuses text read that way anywhere in the room’s data.

2026-09-24 — The community service board

  • The Community Center has a community service board: a painted steel sorter on the wall by the desk, with a slot for every room on the street that takes something in from our community. A photograph for the Polaroid Wall in Enid’s Room, a collection for The Collection Collection, a yell for the Playhouse, a pebble for the Pebble Board, a quotation for the Zibaldone, a page for the zine, your samefoods, something to watch or listen to for the rooms with a screen or a playlist, a poem for the Doomscroll, an idea for Your Room, and an idea for a new shop, pitch or turning.
  • Two slots are for when something is wrong: one for anything we got wrong, including anything you cannot read, reach or press, and one for taking back anything with you in it. That comes down when you say so, and coming down means gone.
  • Nothing on the board sends anything. Every slot says how its own room already asks to be reached and points at the room’s own words. The CB is not a way in: it forgets at midnight and everybody on it can read it.
  • A slot repeats another room’s terms, so it is held to them. tools/make-community.py reads each room’s published page and refuses if the room has stopped saying what its slot says, so the board cannot keep promising something a room has changed. It also refuses a form, a route no room offers, and any hint of a score: nothing on the board counts who gave what.
  • The rooms with a playlist or a screen now say so themselves. Dance, Punks, Vital Plant Living and Club Chronic take song suggestions, and Laughingstock, the Lightbulb Picture House, Looming Rocks, Black Leather Lagoon and the Hermitage’s campfire take recommendations, each in its own words, and the board is held to every one of those sentences the same way. The Collection Collection already said how to put something in a cabinet, so its slot points there now.
  • Changed: community-center.html, data/community.json, tools/make-community.py, love.css (§51), tools/check-contrast.py, every room named above, and an anchor on the heading each room already had for sending something in, which changes nothing you can see.

2026-09-24 — The Community Center, and the CB

  • A new shopfront on the street: the Community Center, a painted block hall at ten in the morning with the blinds half open, the chairs stacked against the wall and a radio on the front desk. It holds the house norms for the CB and points at our covenant.
  • The CB is a chat channel patterned after citizens band radio. Sign on at the front desk with a handle and this week’s password, and the radio rides along in the bottom right corner of every page until the password changes or you sign off. Drag it by its bar, or press Move and use the arrow keys. It keeps the latest ten messages and deletes everything at midnight, Colorado time. There are no alerts: no sounds, no notifications, no badges, and nothing saved up for when you come back. Open, it listens; folded away or in another tab, it sends nothing.
  • It is the first thing on this site that sends anything, so the privacy page changed first. What the channel keeps, what it never keeps (no IP address, no list of who signed on, nothing written into a log), where it lives and who can read it are all written out there. The security policy now lets a page talk to this site and nowhere else (connect-src ’self’), and the radio’s script is never downloaded by anybody who has not signed on.
  • The radio stays out of the Healing Checkpoint, which promises it writes nothing down about you. In the rooms where it does go, a few promises that had been about the whole page now say what they were always about: the Arcade’s cabinets, Quill Drift, the Guild’s stamps, the Repeater’s open line, and the headset and the stage in Dance, Punks and Looming Rocks.
  • Two new typefaces: Varela Round, by Joe Prince, for the letterboard, and Radio Canada, by Charles Daoud, Coppers and Brasses, Alexandre Saumier Demers and Jacques Le Bailly, for everything you read. Both are on the Foundry’s shelves and in the Mopery’s font picker.
  • Changed: community-center.html, cb.js, cb.css, netlify/, package.json, love.js, love.css (§51 is the new room; small screens and print are §52 and §53), privacy.html, SECURITY.md, _headers, _redirects, and the rooms named above.

2026-09-24 — Vital Plant Living: build your own bowl, and everything in it touches

  • A new shopfront on the street: Vital Plant Living, a plant-based kitchen. Build your own bowl or wrap out of Ryan’s own pantry, the list he cooks from at home, in his three steps — base, texture, flavour — or start from one of the combos on the board, each standing on a flavour base from somewhere in the world that is off his list: the Cajun holy trinity, a sofrito, a mirepoix, garlic, ginger and scallion, and the rest.
  • Copy your bowl or wrap to the clipboard and take it shopping: everything in it comes out one per line, under the pantry’s own section names. Nothing is stored and nothing is sent anywhere; closing the page empties the bowl.
  • Everything in the room is drawn cut through the middle, the way a cook sketches a dish to show what is in it: your bowl, wrap or sandwich changes as you tick, with every layer sitting on the one under it and the sauce running into the rice. It is Samefood Cafe the other way up, a few doors along, where nothing on a plate touches anything, and the house rules send anybody who needs their food apart there by name. The pots on the shelf over the counter are cut open too, and every one of them is something in the pantry.
  • The room was briefed as Ital Plant Livity, and it says where the name went. Both words are Rastafari’s, and Rastafari has been a big influence on Ryan; but this kitchen cooks with salt, MSG and things out of packets, which many people who keep Ital do not, and how Ital is kept varies from person to person and is not ours to settle. So the name over the door is the plain-English one, the menu never calls a dish Ital, and the room says all of that near the top rather than in a footnote.
  • The stereo plays Ryan’s playlist of songs about food, most of them reggae and dancehall, and nothing plays until you press play. Every song is listed with who made it, how long it runs and who uploaded it, because several were put up by fans rather than by the musicians.
  • The menu makes no claims. Nothing on it is called authentic, nothing is good or bad for you, and nothing is counted; where a combo is thinking about a dish usually made with something the pantry does not keep, it says so. tools/make-vital.py refuses a combo that brings in anything the pantry does not keep, and refuses all of that vocabulary in the room’s own voice.
  • Two new typefaces: Bagel Fat One, by Kyungwon Kim and JAMO, for the kitchen’s name and its headings, and Be Vietnam Pro, by Lâm Bảo, Tony Le and ViệtAnh Nguyễn, for everything you read. Both are on the Foundry’s shelves and in the Mopery’s font picker, with their designers named.
  • Changed: vital-plant-living.html, vital.js, data/vital.json and tools/make-vital.py (all new), og/vital-plant-living.png (new), fonts/, index.html, love.css (§50 is the new room; small screens and print are §51 and §52 now), data/quests.json, liner-notes.html, the Foundry and the Mopery’s picker, and the map.

2026-09-24 — The Map: the whole street in white card, and a Where am I? on every page

  • New street furniture by the front door: The Map, the whole street as a model in white card on a cutting mat. Every shopfront stands where it stands, on the side of the road it falls on, with the rooms behind rooms standing behind them; the garden’s gate is halfway down; the campground’s field is off the top of the board past the treeline and the road out of town is off the bottom past the last streetlight. Every building, tent, sign and tag on it goes to the place it stands for.
  • Every page now has a Where am I? at its foot, next to the liner notes. It opens the map at the page you were on, with a flag on it saying you came from there. Nothing is stored and no script is involved: the page’s own name is the address of its place on the model.
  • Nothing on the model is painted. Every room here has its own light and its own colours, and a map that painted them all would be telling you they belonged to one set. So it is one white card, and each room is left to be itself when you get there.
  • Nothing on it was placed by hand. The model is built out of the street itself — the front page’s row of shopfronts in the order they stand, the campground’s board and the road’s signs — so it grows when the street does, and a new room cannot open without going up on it, because the build refuses until it has. On a phone both sides of the road are kept, because which side a room is on is the point of a map.
  • Two new typefaces: Architects Daughter, by Kimberly Geswein, for the lettering on the model, and Albert Sans, by Andreas Rasmussen, for everything you read. Both are on the Foundry’s shelves and in the Mopery’s font picker, with their designers named.

2026-09-24 — The Collection Collection: the house brings the furniture, the light is theirs

  • A new shopfront on the street: The Collection Collection, a dark gallery of our people’s collections. Every collection stands in a cabinet of its own under a lamp of its own, and no two lamps are alike: a daylight lamp over Ryan’s pens, paper and ink, because a warm bulb lies about the colour of an ink, and a strip light inside his perfume cupboard’s door, because light spoils perfume.
  • A cabinet with nothing in it yet stands with its lamp off, and its label is exactly as easy to read as a lit one. Ryan’s pens, paper and ink are in, under their daylight lamp, and his perfume cupboard is still dark, waiting for its photographs. Norah and her husband’s leather swatches and stamping tools, Chelsea’s house plants and Helen’s books and rocks each have a cabinet with their name on it, say that Ryan relayed them, and stay empty until they send something. Their lamps are theirs to choose.
  • The house describes every photograph so that nobody has to. Writing a title, a description and a caption for every picture is work, and work is what stops people sending a picture at all. So Claude writes them, looking at each photograph, and every cabinet says so on its label. It describes only what it can see and never names a maker or a model from what a thing looks like; an owner’s own words win wherever they give any. This is the opposite of the polaroid wall on purpose: a description of a person is theirs to write, and a description of a pen is not a description of anybody.
  • Nothing is counted, priced, valued or ranked, and nobody asks how many.
  • Every photograph is stripped before it reaches the site. A new tool takes phone photographs in, turns them the right way up, removes the location and every other scrap of metadata a phone writes, makes them smaller without cropping them, and leaves a record that stops the room building until somebody has described each one and looked at the whole frame for a person or an address. The room’s own tool refuses a photograph that still carries any metadata, a filter on any photograph, and two cabinets with the same lamp.
  • The photographs are not under this site’s Creative Commons licence, so that anything can still come down when its owner says so.
  • Two new typefaces: Gloock, by Duarte Pinto, for the room’s name and every label’s heading, and Hanken Grotesk, by Alfredo Marco Pradil and Hanken Design Co., for everything you read. Both are on the Foundry’s shelves and in the Mopery’s font picker, with their designers named.
  • Changed: collection-collection.html, data/collection.json, tools/make-collection.py and tools/intake-collection.py (all new), og/collection-collection.png (new), fonts/, index.html, love.css (§48 is the new room; small screens and print are §49 and §50 now), liner-notes.html, adventurers-guild.html, foundry.html, the-mopery.html, LICENSE, .gitignore, tools/make-og.py, tools/make-guild.py, tools/make-webp.py, tools/check-contrast.py, tools/check-classes.py, tools/structured.py, tools/make-sitemap.py, sitemap.xml, llms.txt, feed.xml.

2026-09-23 — The feed keeps a busy day in order

  • Entries from the same day used to arrive in any order. Every one of them carried the same time, noon, so a reader had nothing to sort them by. On a day with more than ten, stimpunks.org’s feeds page showed ten of them in an order of its own and left out the newest.
  • Now the top entry of a day keeps noon and each one under it is a minute earlier, so every reader lists them in the same order as this page. The dates are still true. The minutes are only the order, and not a claim about when anything was written.
  • Nothing reappears as new. Each entry keeps the identifier it was published under.
  • Changed: tools/make-feed.py, feed.xml.

2026-09-23 — The street is Stimpunks.World now

  • The site lives at stimpunks.world, and it is called Stimpunks.World. We had been calling it Stimpunks World for a while, as it grew past one street into rooms behind rooms, a campground past the treeline and a road out of town, so the domain was registered to match and the name moved with it: every page title, the feed, the favicon, the share cards, and the wordmark on the front door, whose pink second line now says .world.
  • Every old link still works. stimpunks.love stays ours and sends you, page for page, to the same place here. The certificate covers both.
  • Your feed reader should not see anything twice. Each changelog entry keeps the identifier it was published under, so moving house did not turn a week of entries back into news.
  • The older entries below still say stimpunks.love where that was where the street was at the time. We did not go back and rewrite them to match.
  • Changed: every page’s head (title, canonical, Open Graph, structured data), index.html’s wordmark, every share card in og/, sitemap.xml, llms.txt, feed.xml, robots.txt, site.webmanifest, favicon.svg, _redirects, /.well-known/ (the agent skill moved to stimpunks-world, and the old path redirects), data/arrivals.json, data/foundry.json, and the tools that write any of them. tools/make-feed.py keeps its guids on the old domain.

2026-09-23 — Samefood Cafe: your favourite, the way you have it

  • A new shopfront on the street: Samefood Cafe, for samefoods and safe foods, and for understanding food aversion rather than correcting it. The kitchen knows its regulars: it keeps what each of them has, the way they have it, and is ready for the day their samefood changes with the season. The menu is whatever the regulars ask for, a dish goes on when somebody asks, and nothing comes off because somebody else got bored of it. Samefoods have seasons, and the room says so.
  • Nothing touches unless you want it to, and that is the room. Separation is offered, never imposed: the house’s divided plate keeps different foods apart and the ramekins keep red things off everything, and a bowl is one person’s dish, the way they eat it, touching as much as they like — some of us need everything apart and some of us do not mind at all. It is drawn from directly above the table, under one even light that is the same at every hour, so every object sits in its own soft ring of shade. Every plate on the page is the same plate — the menu, the book and every tray — which is Covenstead’s mismatched china turned inside out, one door along.
  • The menu is three entries from our glossary, Samefood, Safe food and Food aversion, each with one short line from somebody who has written about it: C.L. Lynch at NeuroClastic, Kari Burroughs Kraakevik at Thinking Person’s Guide to Autism, and Matthew Broberg-Moffitt. Two were read against their own pages; the two from the book could not be, and the room says so beside them.
  • On the counter: Color Taste Texture, by Matthew Broberg-Moffitt, with its details read off Open Library’s record, and a way to find it in a library as well as a shop.
  • The regulars’ samefoods are on trays of their own, written down exactly as they were given and not tidied, sorted, ranked or added up. Ryan’s, Helen’s and Ronan’s are the first, each with a bowl of their samefood of the moment — ramen with fresh green onions, pesto pasta, chicken nuggets; more are being collected in our community’s Discord. Every list comes down when its person asks, and every food drawn on the plate in the window is off one of them.
  • We default to structure, separation and predictability, and every guest is accommodated individually — never regarded as fussy, picky or too much, just quietly and professionally accommodated. Nobody’s way imposes on anybody else’s plate: Ronan has red things in a ramekin of their own, so his ketchup comes that way, and the room’s tool holds his food, and nobody else’s, to it.
  • Two new typefaces: Fredoka for the café’s name and headings, and Inclusive Sans, drawn so that no letter can be taken for another, for everything you read. Both are on the Foundry’s shelves and in the Mopery’s font picker, with their designers named.
  • Changed: samefood-cafe.html, data/samefood.json and tools/make-samefood.py (all new), og/samefood-cafe.png (new), fonts/, index.html, love.css (§47 is the new room; small screens and print are §48 and §49 now), liner-notes.html, adventurers-guild.html, foundry.html, the-mopery.html, data/quests.json, data/foundry.json, data/foundry-faces.json, sitemap.xml, llms.txt, tools/make-og.py, tools/make-guild.py, tools/make-sitemap.py, tools/check-contrast.py, tools/check-classes.py, README.md, CLAUDE.md, DECISIONS.md.

2026-09-23 — The Small Hours gets a jukebox, and one song printed in full

  • The Small Hours plays both of Josephmooon’s albums, So Far So Good and Hand in Heaven, every track, streamed from the band’s own site and hosted nowhere here. Josephmooon is the music project made for the lyrics of Ronan Boren, a Stimpunk, and Stimpunks helped produce it. Every button says how long its song runs before the press, measured off the file itself; only one song plays at a time; and the two album buttons say how many songs and how long before they start several.
  • “Up All Night” is on the counter with its words printed in full, a song about Autistic insomnia, with its credits: Ronan Boren, all lyrics; Bill Paige, vocals and guitar; Ronnie Nice, guitars, keyboards, bass, drums and programming; Ian Nice, piano, keyboards and backing vocals. That is permission rather than quotation: every other room quotes a line or two of a song or none, and this is the one song on the street published whole, because Stimpunks holds permission to distribute it and its lyrics. make-small-hours.py refuses a lyric without a written-down permission, the words are excluded from this site’s licence in LICENSE, and the agent skill’s “no lyrics” line now names the one exception.
  • A recording from somebody else’s server needed its own origin list. love-embed.js has an AUDIO_ORIGINS array beside its frame list and builds the players; make-csp.py reads it into a new media-src, path and all, because without one the policy falls back to 'self' and the jukebox would be a row of buttons that press and play nothing. The privacy page lists the band’s site among the services a press can reach.
  • Changed: small-hours.html, small-hours.js (new), data/small-hours.json, tools/make-small-hours.py, love-embed.js, tools/make-csp.py, _headers, love.css §4 and §44, privacy.html, LICENSE, the agent skill, liner-notes.html.

2026-09-23 — Four new turnings off The Outskirts, each lit a way the road had not been

  • Turning 04, Dance, Punks: a silent disco on an old airstrip, lit by nothing but the headsets. Three channels that are our Dance, Punks playlist started in three places — the room says so, and never calls it shuffle — and tuning is silent while the headset is off. The volume is on the player, so the loudness this whole street hands the visitor is handed to every dancer. The three channel colours are held at least 1.5 to 1 apart in brightness so they stay three headsets in greyscale, and nothing on the runway strobes or moves at any setting. Each channel’s opening song was pressed and heard starting where it says.
  • Turning 05, The Small Hours: an all-night diner seen lit from the dark car park, for bodies that keep other hours. Crip time in Alison Kafer’s words, a clock with no hands, a menu with no prices, and no sleep advice anywhere, for a reason one of its quotations gives. The sign does not read your clock. The crayons passage in our Sleep entry is Sarah Kurchak’s, and this room nearly credited it to somebody else from memory: our entry links the book without naming its author, so every author here was resolved against a library record.
  • Turning 06, The Repeater: a radio mast on the ridge at the far end of the road, where everything is a silhouette against the glow of everybody else’s light. Our Shared-Signal Space entry is its argument, a log confirms every kind of contact and counts none, and a line you can leave open is a quiet noise made in your browser that sends nothing and listens to nothing. The Autistic Rhizome was first credited here to Helen Edgar alone; our own entry shows DGH Neurodivergent Consultancy named it, and both are credited now. That article’s own address now serves a holding page, so the room links our entry instead.
  • Turning 07, Nothing For Sale: a free market in a lay-by, lit by a ring of cars that pulled up nose-in and left their headlights on, so everything in the middle throws one shadow for every car. Solidarity, not charity; tables of free things of ours, each checked against its own page; no proof of need; nothing counted. Our mutual aid grants are pointed to rather than laid out, because they have an application and a waiting list and a table would promise otherwise.
  • A new tool for each, and every refusal in them was broken on purpose and fired. Two were wrong on their first run and were narrowed rather than excepted: the disco’s animation check refused its own section’s comment describing the refusal, and the lay-by’s charity check refused the room’s sentence explaining what charity is.
  • check-jukebox.py had not been able to run since Laughingstock opened. That room’s data file carries acts as well as sets, the branch for acts came first, and the comics were read as videos with no artist, so the whole run refused before checking anything. sets is tested first now, and the disco’s three opening songs are in its lists. The diner’s jukebox is not, because none of it is on YouTube.
  • Syncopate is under the Apache License 2.0, not the OFL, and the first draft of two credits said otherwise; the pulled record caught it. Looming Rocks’ board, description and house rules still said six concerts when the bill has ten, and say whole concerts now rather than a number.
  • Changed: four new pages, scripts, data files and tools; love.css §1, §4 and §43–§46 (Small screens and Print are §47 and §48); the-outskirts.html; looming-rocks.html; fonts/; data/foundry.json; data/foundry-faces.json; data/quests.json; tools/make-guild.py, make-og.py, make-sitemap.py, check-contrast.py, check-classes.py, check-jukebox.py; liner-notes.html; four new share cards.

2026-09-23 — The Lightbulb Picture House: every screening is a relaxed screening

  • A new shopfront on the street: The Lightbulb Picture House, a picture house showing films about autism, ADHD and neurodiversity, most of them made by the people they are about. It is named for Thriving Autistic’s short film Lightbulb Moments: Being Autistic, which is on its first screen.
  • Two screens, both our own playlists: Animated Introductions to Autism, ADHD, and Neurodiversity and More on Autism, ADHD, and Neurodiversity. Each plays whole and runs until you stop it, and every film on them was checked on its own watch page before it went up.
  • The rack has a card for every film on Screen One, in the order it plays: who made it, where it was published, how long it runs, a line of ours about it written from the film rather than from its title, and what is in it, said before you press — a panic attack, a bullying scene, jokes about suicide, a sponsor segment. Where one of our own pages already carries the film or the idea, the card links to it.
  • Every screening is a relaxed screening. The house lights stay up, the captions come on by default, and you can move, stim, sit on the floor, come in late and leave early. The drawing of the auditorium is lit all over, with a floor cushion in the aisle and a pair of ear defenders on a seat.
  • Nothing in the building is blue. A lightbulb theater lit blue would be Light It Up Blue, the awareness campaign autistic people have spent years refusing, and Amy Sequenzia says why on the page. The bulbs are warm, the ornament over the screen is the gold infinity, and tools/make-picture-house.py reads the room’s palette and refuses a blue — along with the vocabulary of awareness in our own voice, a film with no note of what is in it, and a pronoun for any maker, because none of the films says what theirs are.
  • Two new typefaces: Limelight, an Art Deco fascia face, for the name and the headings, and Outfit for everything you read. Both are on the Foundry’s shelves and in the Mopery’s font picker. At MAX GLITTER the popcorn in the second row pops; no bulb ever flickers.
  • Changed: lightbulb-picture-house.html, data/picture-house.json and tools/make-picture-house.py (all new), og/lightbulb-picture-house.png (new), fonts/, index.html, love.css (§42 is the new room; small screens and print are §43 and §44 now), liner-notes.html, adventurers-guild.html, foundry.html, the-mopery.html, data/quests.json, data/foundry.json, data/foundry-faces.json, sitemap.xml, llms.txt, tools/make-og.py, tools/make-guild.py, tools/make-sitemap.py, tools/check-contrast.py, tools/check-classes.py, tools/check-jukebox.py, README.md, CLAUDE.md, DECISIONS.md.

2026-09-23 — Laughingstock: a comedy club where only the comics stand in the light

  • A new shopfront on the street: Laughingstock, a comedy club down a ramp rather than a flight of stairs. It leads with our own writing from the Campfire Learn Together Being Disabled Can Be Pretty Funny: comedy is a form of knowledge, and disabled comics take the stage on their own terms, in their own voice, controlling the frame.
  • The stage is our own Disabled Comedy playlist, put on whole and running until you stop it, and beside it is the bill — every set on that playlist, one press each, the comics named on every one and the runtime said before you press. Josh Blue, Tina Friml, AJ Wilkerson, Benny Feldman, Steve Way, Ahren Belisle and Fiona Cauley. Nothing plays until you press play.
  • The light comes from behind you, and the only thing that stands in it is what the comics said. One follow spot throws a hard-edged pool onto a brick wall, and each of Josh Blue’s and Tina Friml’s lines stands in an oval of that light with their name under it. Everything of ours — the write-up, our notes, the house rules — is out in the dark with the audience. Nobody is drawn on the stage: a mic, a stool, a glass of water, and a ramp up to it.
  • House rules: no cover and no two-drink minimum, nobody in the front row made part of the act without asking, talk to the comic and not to whoever came in with them, bombing is allowed, nobody is called brave, and nobody headlines. tools/make-laughingstock.py refuses the vocabulary of inspiration and of ranking in the room’s own voice, and skips the comics’ own lines, so that “I suffer from people” is allowed to say suffer.
  • Two new typefaces: Yellowtail, a sign-writer’s script, for the neon and the comics’ names, and Bricolage Grotesque for everything you read. Both are on the Foundry’s shelves and in the Mopery’s font picker, with their designers named. At MAX GLITTER the water on the stool goes sparkling; the neon never flickers.
  • Changed: laughingstock.html, data/laughingstock.json and tools/make-laughingstock.py (all new), og/laughingstock.png (new), fonts/, index.html, love.css (§41 is the new room; small screens and print are §42 and §43 now), liner-notes.html, adventurers-guild.html, foundry.html, the-mopery.html, data/quests.json, data/foundry.json, data/foundry-faces.json, sitemap.xml, llms.txt, tools/make-og.py, tools/make-guild.py, tools/make-sitemap.py, tools/check-contrast.py, tools/check-classes.py, tools/check-jukebox.py, README.md, CLAUDE.md, DECISIONS.md.

2026-09-23 — Dead Tired Society: the big lights off, the door ajar, and nobody in the light

  • A new shopfront on the street: Dead Tired Society, peer support by and for people in burnout and people on the far side of it. The name is a play on Dead Poets Society, and the name is the only thing taken from the film — no line, no scene, no character.
  • It is a borrowed room after hours with the big lights off and the door left ajar, and the only light is the corridor’s, lying across the floor through the gap. Every word on the page stays in the shade on purpose: masking is performing under a light, and this is the room where nothing has to. Nobody is drawn in the picture of it, because nobody in there is on display.
  • By the door there is a row of pegs for what people carried in — masking, minority stress, epistemic injustice, justice sensitivity, moral injury and neuronormativity, and more named further along the wall. Each says what is hanging on it, gives one short line from somebody who has written about it, credited to them, and adds a line of ours. Every line was read off our own glossary, and where a source was named only by title or initials the authors were looked up rather than guessed.
  • Standing orders nobody is standing for, and a talking piece you can pass, because passing is a whole turn. What you type into it goes nowhere and is not kept.
  • What it will not do is written on the page and enforced by its tool: no advice nobody asked for, no tips, nobody praised for resilience, no timeline, nothing counted, nobody at the front. It is not a meeting and says so, and points at our community and at mutual aid. Near the top, a line for anybody whose night is worse than tired, pointing at our Help and Coping pages.
  • Two new typefaces: Sorts Mill Goudy for the society’s name, and Lexend for everything you read, which was drawn to ease reading fatigue. Both are on the Foundry’s shelves and in the Mopery’s font picker, with their designers named.
  • Changed: dead-tired-society.html, dead-tired.js, data/dead-tired.json and tools/make-dead-tired.py (all new), og/dead-tired-society.png (new), fonts/, index.html, love.css (§40 is the new room; small screens and print are §41 and §42 now), liner-notes.html, adventurers-guild.html, foundry.html, the-mopery.html, data/quests.json, data/foundry.json, data/foundry-faces.json, sitemap.xml, llms.txt, tools/make-og.py, tools/make-guild.py, tools/make-sitemap.py, tools/check-contrast.py, tools/check-classes.py, README.md, CLAUDE.md, DECISIONS.md.

2026-09-23 — The Guild signs off once

  • The Adventurer’s Guild ended with two lines of links once every page got its sign-off: its own, on the desk beside the credits, and the shared one under it. The desk’s line is gone. The desk keeps its credits and the dial; the way back to the street is the link at the top of the page, as it is in every room.
  • Changed: adventurers-guild.html.

2026-09-23 — Lighter pictures, where that is measurably true

  • Fifty-six of the street’s JPEGs are WebP now, and the pictures on those pages weigh about 29% less — 2.4MB across the scans, prints, engravings, photographs and thumbnails. The biggest single waste went with it: the Pebble Board’s video thumbnails were stored 1280 pixels wide and are never drawn wider than 170, and they are 512 now, which still covers a phone screen at three times density.
  • Twenty-four stayed JPEG, each for a reason written down. Every file was converted at the lowest quality that measured as faithful to its source, and swapped only if that saved at least a tenth. Most of Doré’s and Tenniel’s engravings did not clear that bar: fine hatching is the one thing lossy formats are worst at, and at the same fidelity WebP was no smaller than the JPEG, sometimes larger. Converting them anyway would have meant a heavier file or a softer engraving, chosen for the format’s sake. data/webp.json holds every decision and its numbers.
  • The first attempt measured the wrong thing and was undone. It compared colour as well as detail, and WebP always stores colour at half resolution, as most JPEGs do — so on small, bright thumbnails it concluded no quality was good enough and left the oversized ones exactly as they were. It had deleted JPEGs under that rule, so everything went back to the last commit and ran again measuring detail, which is what the eye looks for.
  • Nothing about the pages changed but the files. WebP goes into the same image tags, so no room’s layout moved; AVIF was often smaller still, but serving it safely means wrapping every picture in a new element, and at least one room’s layout depends on its images not being wrapped. The share cards and icons stay PNG, because link previews and home screens do not all read anything else. Every picture on the affected pages was loaded and checked afterwards, and the photographs still carry no location or device data.
  • Changed: images in raven/, oracle/, alice/, pebbles/ and photos/, data/mopery.json, data/oracle.json, data/rabbit-hole.json, data/pebble-board.json, data/webp.json (new), the pages those generators write, og/oracle-deck.png, tools/make-webp.py (new), tools/make-og.py, README.md, CLAUDE.md.

2026-09-23 — Honest sitemap dates, three videos unsquashed, and a consent date for every voice

  • The sitemap stopped saying the whole street changed every time it was rebuilt. Every page’s lastmod used to be the day the sitemap was generated, which tells a crawler that everything is new whether or not anything is — the exact mistake the specification names. Each page’s date is now the day of the last commit that touched it, read out of the repository’s history, and the tool refuses to run where there is no history to read rather than guessing.
  • Three videos on the Pebble Board were squashed. Every play button takes the shape its player will have, and three of the board’s videos are older and four-by-three: their thumbnails were being stretched to fill a sixteen-by-nine box, every face in them a third wider than it is. They are fitted now, with the board’s own night either side, which is what the player shows once you press it. Not cropped: those frames have no bars to cut, and taking a quarter off a music video to fill a box would be the same fault the other way round.
  • Every recorded voice on the street now has a consent date, the readings in Hear Queer Here included. The yells and the Faery Yurt’s recordings always carried one; the readings did not, which is why the privacy page had to word its promise around them. They were agreed to on the day each recording landed, and that date is now read off the commit that added each file rather than typed. make-readings.py refuses a recording without one, as the other two audio tools do, and the privacy page makes the plain promise it could not make before.
  • Changed: sitemap.xml, tools/make-sitemap.py, love.css (the Pebble Board’s section), data/readings.json, tools/make-readings.py, privacy.html.

2026-09-23 — Structured data, and a note for any agent reading the street

  • Every page describes itself in JSON-LD, the format search engines and agents treat as fact. Everything in it comes out of that page’s own head — its title, its description, its address — so it cannot say anything the page does not. The publisher is marked with the same identifier Star Stuff uses, so two of our sites describe one Stimpunks Foundation rather than two. The licence is on the site, not on each page, because the photographs of people are excluded from it. The missing-address page gets none: it has no address to describe.
