Measured rather than assumed: on Rye, a requested italic renders at the roman’s own advance widths; on Cardo, which has a real italic, oblique 12deg comes out at 399.65px against the italic’s 377.42px, so the slant really is the roman pushed over.
  • The slant is a font setting and never a transform, deliberately. check-gentle.py reads rotation and skew out of the computed matrix and cannot tell a leaning page from a leaning letter, so a skewX() here would be reported — correctly — as this room’s decoration outranking somebody’s Gentle setting. There is no transform anywhere in §32, which is the call arcade.js made with left/top and the Oracle Deck made with its lozenge.
  • A weight is offered only where the bytes differ. Seven of these families ship as variable fonts, so love.css declares one file against two or three weights — and a bold that renders the same drawings as the regular is a control that does nothing, which is make-chappell.py’s typo that never becomes a video arriving as a dropdown. The puller records a sha for every variant and the bench groups by it. Hashing the whole shelf also turned up Space Grotesk stored three times over, byte for byte, on the shared base that every page loads; that is logged separately rather than fixed in this commit.
  • Four ink and paper pairs, each printing its own measured ratio on its own control, and the tool refuses one under 4.5:1. This is the one room on the street where somebody other than us picks the colour of the text, which makes it either the place contrast quietly stops being ours to keep or the place it becomes a thing you can watch being kept. There is no colour picker and there is not going to be one.
  • The light moved rather than the palette. The north light was a fixed full-height wash to begin with, which composites the floor to #596267 — and against that the room’s link brass measures 3.14 and its fine print 3.34, both under the bar. Worse, fixed, that composite could arrive under any paragraph on the page depending on how far somebody had scrolled, which is the shape The Rabbit Hole’s scrolling ground already taught this stylesheet. It is 340px tall and absolute now: the light comes in at the top of the room and the page scrolls out from under it, which is also what happens in a building. A light that follows the viewport is not a light, it is a veil in front of the reader. The only words standing in it are the topline’s, and they are bone at 5.19 for that reason.
  • The proof prints and the room does not, and what prints carries a colophon naming the family, the person who drew it, the licence and every setting it was pulled at — because printing somebody’s typeface is exactly where the credit should travel with it. It comes out black on white whatever pair is on the screen: paper colour is a screen decision and a printer would spend your toner on it. Measured rather than asserted: the rendered PDF is one page with two fonts embedded in it, the proof’s face and the colophon’s, which is how you can tell the shelves did not print.
  • Nothing here describes a typeface, and that is a refusal rather than an omission. A line of appreciation beside every family on this street would mean inventing type history at scale in the format most likely to be believed — the herbarium’s refusal to name a pressed plant to species, arriving where there is one to write for every face. What the room says truthfully is who drew it, what licence it travels under and which room here sets it, and that last claim is checked against love.css: make-foundry.py refuses a line the stylesheet does not support. A derived version got it wrong on its first run, because the street letters each door in that room’s own face.
  • Nothing is counted. Not the faces, not the proofs pulled, not the sizes tried, and there is no favourite and no ranking of one designer’s work against another’s. This is the room most likely to want to boast about how much type it holds, and the generator refuses the sentence as well as the habit.
  • Two new faces. Stardos Stencil sets the nameplate and the headings and nothing else — a stencil letter is a hole rather than a mark, a shape cut out of metal so it can be used again, which is the idea of type arriving four centuries early and in the wrong material, and it is what gets painted on a workshop’s crates. Anton is the street’s other condensed display face and is Club Chronic’s: solid, on a black wall, under a marker. Work Sans is the prose and is a workhorse on purpose, because a room whose content is other people’s typefaces cannot have a voice that argues with the window. Both under the SIL Open Font License.
  • New: foundry.html, foundry.js, data/foundry.json, data/foundry-faces.json, tools/pull-foundry.py, tools/make-foundry.py, tools/check-faces.py, §32 of love.css, Stardos Stencil and Work Sans self-hosted, a share card drawn as a galley with its letters mirrored the way cast type is, and a single sort lying on the workshop floor with a job on it. Changed: index.html, the-mopery.html and tools/make-mopery.py so the nook’s picker is generated, the liner notes, data/quests.json, tools/make-guild.py, tools/make-og.py, tools/make-sitemap.py, tools/check-contrast.py and tools/check-classes.py’s own cross-reference. Small screens and Print renumbered to §33 and §34.

2026-09-22 — The Healing Checkpoint, room 429, and the first room lit from the floor

  • The guild’s bed exists: The Healing Checkpoint, room 429. Ryan’s brief — a guest room for the Adventurer’s Guild, patterned after the Healing Checkpoint, leading with an appreciation of rest built out of our own Rest entry. A save room: an arched alcove with a spring in the floor, a bed in it, and nobody at the desk.
  • It is a shopfront on the street AND a back stair out of the guild, and the two doors are the argument. A room saying rest is owed rather than earned cannot be reachable only by walking through the place that hands out the work — that would make it structurally something you get to after the jobs. So the front door is on the street, where anybody arrives at it having done nothing, and the guild’s unattended counter is the back way up. It is the first room here with two entrances. No job sends you to it; the one job that lives in it is a question about a status code.
  • The light comes UP, and that one physical fact is the whole separation. Every other world on this street is lit from somewhere else and no two from the same place — the Jungle Room from above through leaves, the Zibaldone from one side at desk height, Swaying Sweetgrass from behind and a hundred feet off, The Feed through a mask in front of a lamp, The Garden from directly overhead, the guild from nowhere at all. Here it comes out of the floor: every top surface is dark, every underside carries a rim, the shadows run up the walls, and the room gets lighter towards your feet.
  • The warm enclosed family is the collapse to watch and by now it has arrived six times. The Faery Yurt, The Latibulum, The Den, the Hermitage’s cave and The Mopery are every one of them lit by a flame you could put your hand over. There is no flame in here; the light is under water and you could put your hand in it. All five are brown, amber or candle-gold, and there is no warm ground anywhere in room 429 at all — only one warm ink, which is what pale stone looks like with a cold light on it. The guild is its other door and its exact opposite: manila, square, ruled, hairlined, lit like a photocopy from nowhere. There is not a straight edge in the checkpoint anywhere. A board and an inn share a building and nothing else.
  • The generator refuses instrumental rest, which is a refusal nothing else here makes. tools/make-checkpoint.py sweeps its own data and the published page for the vocabulary of rent — earn, deserve, recharge, productive, bounce back, so you can — with a negation window so the room can still say out loud that it will not. Star Stuff’s zine No. 58 refuses rest so you can produce in as many words and otterly.js already holds that line for one cabinet; this is where the whole street says it. The friendly edit here arrives from somebody being kind, because every wellness page on the internet is written the other way round, and one encouraging sentence would turn rest from a thing owed into an investment.
  • Nothing in it is counted and nothing is stored. No total, no streak, no visits, no local storage, no server — the pebbling cabinet’s refusal of a tally arriving where a counter is the most obvious improvement anybody could propose, and one link above a job board that does keep a list in your own browser. A checkpoint that remembered you would be keeping a record of how often you needed one. The trope’s other two promises are refused in the room’s own copy: it restores nothing, because you are not a health bar, and there is no hard part waiting on the other side.
  • The tally check was copied wrong and its first run caught it. make-guild.py narrowed points years’ worth of care ago — “a book it points at” is the verb — and this file reproduced the flat version from the identical shortcut. What refused it was save point, which is what a healing checkpoint is. The narrowed pattern was taken over rather than the phrase excepted, which is check-counts.py’s first-run lesson arriving a fourth time. Every refusal was then broken on purpose before being believed — a long quotation, an off-site trail entry, an entity in a field, a missing checked date, and an instrumental sentence planted in the page itself.
  • Five Retry-After slips, and they send nothing anywhere. 429 Too Many Requests is Nottingham and Fielding’s, RFC 6585, April 2012, and it says the rate is wrong rather than that the sender is, which is a thing a great many Disabled people have spent a great deal of energy trying to explain to somebody holding a deadline. Each slip is a <details> that works with scripts off; checkpoint.js only adds a copy button, which ships hidden so a page without JavaScript shows no dead control. It is the spoon drawer’s shape — it hands out the sentences and not the thing itself — and the Zibaldone’s clipboard, for the Zibaldone’s reason. The answer lands on the slip you pressed, not at the top of the page, which is the Playhouse’s lesson, and it was measured rendered at 653×28 rather than asserted from the DOM.
  • Two new faces, and the crowding is written down rather than hoped about. Quicksand is display only — the third geometric sans here, kept apart from Jost and Josefin Sans by job and by its semicircular terminals, which is what lets it carry a room whose every corner is a long radius. Rubik is the text face, and The Latibulum is the collision: that room’s Nunito is a humanist rounded face paired with a slab, and this is a grotesque with eased corners and flat-cut terminals. Two warm-shaped text faces kept apart at the stroke, which is the only place they could be. Both under the SIL Open Font License.
  • Nothing flashes at any setting, which is Club Chronic’s rule holding in the room whose entire subject is not being jolted. The steam over the water drifts at MAX with a translate and a scale — rotation and skew are all check-gentle.py can read — and is gone at Gentle. The light in the floor is not the dial’s and is on at every setting: it is not an effect, it is where the light in the room comes from, and a Gentle that unlit the room rather than quieting it would be the lite-version mistake with a torch.
  • Two things on the Rest entry are named here and deliberately not copied onto this street: Kristina Daniele’s poem, and three illustrations of Disabled people resting by Jonathan Soren Davidson and Campbell Royales for Disabled And Here. A page built out of somebody else’s page should say what it did not take. The three lines it does quote are Fergus Murray’s and Tricia Hersey’s, each under the thirty-word limit the tool enforces and the room prints, each naming the page and the day it was read off the mirror. Tricia Hersey and The Nap Ministry are credited in the room’s own copy rather than in a footnote, which is what the guild promised when it said the bed was not built yet.
  • Added: healing-checkpoint.html, checkpoint.js, data/checkpoint.json, tools/make-checkpoint.py, §31 of love.css and a share card of its own. Changed: index.html, adventurers-guild.html, the liner notes, data/quests.json, tools/make-guild.py, tools/make-og.py, tools/make-sitemap.py, tools/check-contrast.py, and tools/check-classes.py’s own stale cross-reference. Small screens and Print renumbered to §32 and §33.

2026-09-22 — Ivy and stars instead of a fence, because Helen asked

  • The boundary in The Garden’s two plots was a woven fence this morning and is winding ivy with stars caught in it now. Helen Edgar’s call, on a drawing of her own gardens: my first thought is fenced in though. Her sites, her say — the same rule the Faery Yurt has kept since it was pitched.
  • She was right about more than the picture. A fence says KEPT OUT. Ivy says THIS IS AS FAR AS WE CAN SEE. That line was never marking the edge of where anybody may go; it marks the edge of what we know, which is why the drawing stops there in the first place — we do not get to draw the roots of somebody else’s garden. The second reading is the only correct one on a page about somebody else’s work, and the picture had been quietly making the first one.
  • The refusal underneath it is unchanged and still bites. below_the_fence() is below_the_ivy() and FENCE_Y is IVY_Y; the walker still refuses a coordinate past the line, re-broken on purpose with a frond pushed under it. The rename was scoped to the one section that owns those names — the fence in §20 of love.css belongs to another room and was left alone — which is the rule this repository has now written down three times and broken twice.
  • The stars are glimmers and not a sky. This room is midday and stays midday: nothing is lit by them, no ambient layer was added to the page, and the street’s one daylit garden is not quietly turned into a night one. They are limewash white carried by an outline, because white on that path measures 1.07 and what has to separate is the edge — the ghost pipe’s answer in Queering.Earth’s bed, arriving in a star. No new colour anywhere in it. Threads, glimmers, and soft, weird spaces is More Realms’ own line about itself, which is where the word comes from; they are not the meadow’s fireflies, which are many small lights carrying a dark field.
  • Every ivy leaf is turned by baked coordinates rather than by a transform, because check-gentle.py reads rotation out of a computed matrix and a room must not ask a checker to take its word for something — arcade.js’s call about its sprites and quest.js’s about its sparks, arriving in a leaf.
  • The correction is written down in five places — make-garden.py, data/garden.json, the room’s own copy, the liner notes and here — for the reason the one colour of hers this repository ever changed is written in four. Except that this one runs the other way, from a contributor to us, and a correction in that direction is worth recording at least as loudly as one we made to her work.

2026-09-22 — The garden next door, which is not a bed and could not be made one

  • Autistic Realms and More Realms are in The Garden, over the fence. Ryan’s call. They are Helen Edgar’s own sites — the grounded one and the wilder ground beyond it — and they are named, linked and credited to her on the face of the page rather than in a footnote.
  • A bed is a site WE publish, so neither of these could be one. The garden’s roster is data/arrivals.json, whose order is stimpunks.org’s own feeds page, and the only way to plant one of hers would have been to write it into that file — which would put it on The Feed as one of our wires and claim her work as ours in the one place this room may not invent anything. So the two refusals in make-garden.py now face each other: a bed that is not on the roster is refused, and a plot that is on it is refused, and no edit to the garden’s own data file can move a site across that fence.
  • You can see over the fence and not into the ground, and the drawings say so before the words do. Every bed shows its soil and most of them show what is under it. A plot shows a woven hurdle across the foot of the frame and nothing below it, because we do not get to draw the roots of somebody else’s garden — the tool walks every coordinate and refuses one that reaches under, which is the soil-line walker with the ink filter taken off. It was broken on purpose first, with a frond pushed under the rail, a circle whose edge dipped below and a relative path that walked down; it caught all three. And nothing over there casts a shadow, although everything in the beds does: the sun has not moved, the floor a shadow would land on is on the far side of the fence.
  • A plot must say what of it is already in this garden, and the tool refuses one that does not. Without that line the section is a list of a friend’s websites on a page about ours. With it, it is the other half of every with line in the beds above: those say who helped us, and this says whose garden that help walked out of. So the link rule widens by exactly our own roster — a plot may point at its own ground or at ours, and a third garden is still refused.
  • A plot’s name is not on a limewashed board, and that is the whole visual difference. Every bed’s label is a stake driven into our own ground, and nobody gets to go and put a label in somebody else’s garden, so the name over there is simply written down. The rule under a plot is double where a bed’s is solid — two rails repeating the hurdle, as a bed’s heavy plank repeats the plank along its soil — and the plots stand as narrow columns side by side, which is the fourth geometry this site has for a list of places. No new colour anywhere in it; the fence, the plants and every word are in the room’s own inks on the room’s own path.
  • One habit each, across the fence as well as along the path. Autistic Realms is a nurse tree, planted to take the weather off whatever is growing under it until that can stand in its own light — her argument rather than our metaphor, since her page puts feeling accepted and understood before the flourishing. More Realms is a crozier, a fern still coiled, the one thing in either garden that grows by unwinding rather than by reaching, after that site’s own phrase for how it moves. The drawing dedup now spans both sets, because two drawings alike is two drawings alike whichever side of the hurdle they stand on — which is also what keeps the crozier off the Monotropic Map’s tendril.
  • What each of them is was read off its own pages, in the Knowledge System mirror rather than from memory, and the phrases in quotation marks are theirs and are marked as theirs. Counts, ranking and dates are refused in a plot exactly as in a bed, and the ranking refusal matters more here: ranking a neighbour’s garden against ours would be worse than ranking our own beds. Changed: data/garden.json, tools/make-garden.py, the-garden.html, §28 and the small-screen block of love.css, and the liner notes.

2026-09-22 — The Rabbit Hole, and the first room that carries a song without its words

  • A new storefront, and it is not a room: The Rabbit Hole. Ryan’s brief. Every other door on this street opens onto somewhere you can stand; this one opens onto a shaft with no floor at the bottom of it. It leads with our own glossary entry, Rabbit Hole, and the argument is that one: for monotropic minds the rabbit hole is not a detour, it is the destination, and three hours you did not mean to spend is not a failure of discipline. It carries all seven of that page’s embedded videos, six wood engravings from 1865, a feed your head section, and a trail out.
  • The glossary entry prints the whole lyric and this room prints none of it, which is the one place a room here deliberately does less than the page it came from. Song lyric reproduction is a minefield in the United States in a way prose is not — music publishing enforces on quotation where prose publishing shrugs, the licensing is separate from the recording, and one line is a far larger fraction of a song than of a novel — which is the reasoning that already makes the Zibaldone refuse a song outright. The friendly edit here is real and it will arrive: a room whose spine is one song, whose source page has the words on it already, and whose section headings are phrases from it, reads thin without them, and whoever pastes them back will be right about the page and wrong about everything else. So tools/make-rabbit-hole.py refuses a lyric field and any value shaped like verse — several short hand-broken lines, which is what a lyric looks like in a JSON string and what a sentence never does. The recordings are offered instead, with Grace Slick’s own published account of writing it, and the two headings that are her phrases are credited to her where they stand.
  • The Latibulum is the hardest collapse this street has had, harder than any subroom, because these two rooms are literally both holes in the ground. Every earlier version of the temptation came with a structural excuse attached — it is only a subroom, an area should look like an area, they are both cosy — and this one needs no excuse at all: the floor plan is the same. What keeps them apart is that the burrow is somewhere you arrive and the hole is somewhere you keep going. The burrow has a floor, walls, furniture, a lamp at knee height and a shelf; it is horizontal, it is at rest, and almost every word in it sits dark on lit plaster. This has no floor, no walls to lean on and no furniture whatsoever; it is vertical, the light is above and behind you and there is less of it the further down you read, and every word is pale on the dark. The burrow is earth and is brown. There is no brown in this room at all — it is wet slate — and if these greys ever warm up, the two rooms have become one and it is this one that moves.
  • The ground darkens down the document, which is a shape of ground the contrast tools had not met. The Zibaldone’s rule is a flat background-color under the gradient, and its flat colour is the darker end; here it is the lightest end, because that room sets dark type on pale paper and this one sets pale type on a dark wall. In both cases the flat colour is the worse case for the type standing on it, which is the actual rule and is what makes the live checker’s answer the pessimistic one. And because the gradient runs the height of the document rather than of a sheet, the colour under a word depends on how far somebody has scrolled — so every block that carries text sits on a flat panel of its own and the wash carries nothing but the header’s lines.
  • Two grounds, two link decisions, made at the room. Brass measures 6.74 and up on all three dark grounds and 1.83 on the paper mats the engravings sit on, so a single room-level link colour would have shipped an illegible provenance line under every plate. That is the hole The Doomscroll left and the Playhouse had been carrying since it opened — a colour nobody decided leaves no pair for check-contrast.py to hold — caught this time before the room shipped rather than by somebody looking at the page.
  • Six engravings by John Tenniel, 1865, public domain, shown rather than linked because each scan says so. Tenniel was born in 1820 and died in 1914 and the book came out in 1865, so they are out of copyright everywhere, and every file used carries an explicit public domain statement on Wikimedia Commons — which is exactly what the Mopery’s Library of Congress facsimile did not have and the reason that one is still a door and not a window. The 1865 edition captioned none of its illustrations, so unlike that room’s Doré plates there is nothing printed to quote: every word beside an engraving here is ours, which engraving sits beside which paragraph is our reading and the room says so, and the generator refuses a plate with no sentence of ours, no rights page, or no file on disk. Nothing was cropped, tinted or filtered, and every file was re-encoded with no metadata carried over.
  • One performer is not named on their own upload and the room does not guess. The trailer edit describes itself as an edited remix of the Jefferson Airplane recording and credits no remixer in its title, its description or on its channel, so the plate says what the upload says and says the hand is unnamed there — which is the herbarium’s refusal to guess at a binomial, applied to a credit. And the running order is the glossary page’s own, which interleaves the videos with the quotations they sit under, so it is an argument somebody made rather than a playlist to re-sort — the rule the Jungle Room keeps about its cams. Three of the seven are published by a rights holder and four are re-uploads on individuals’ channels; each plate says which, because that changes how long a link lasts, and the generator refuses a track that does not say.
  • The room carries our own entry’s warning as well as its argument. Be wary of the holes you rabbit, and the reason: the appetite a recommendation engine farms is the same appetite, and what changes is who chose the next thing. So there is no autoplay in there, no next-up, and nothing that decides for you what comes after — seven things, all named, and nothing reaches YouTube until you press it.
  • Nothing in that room moves at any setting, and it has no transform in it anywhere. Not a tilt, not a rotation, no flicker and no strobe; at MAX GLITTER the wash of daylight from the mouth of the hole is a little stronger and that is the whole of it. A rotation is indistinguishable from a tilt to check-gentle.py, which can only read the computed matrix, so a room with no transform in it cannot get that argument wrong — the call arcade.js and The Oracle Deck both already made. The trail’s stepping indent is a padding for the same reason. New: data/rabbit-hole.json, tools/make-rabbit-hole.py, rabbit-hole.html, alice/ with six 1865 scans in it, §30 of love.css (Small screens and Print renumbered to §31 and §32), Playfair Display and Jost self-hosted, the room added to check-jukebox.py’s lists in the same commit that opened it, a share card drawn as a hole rather than as a place, and an engraver’s block on the shaft wall with a job on it.

2026-09-22 — The Zibaldone, and a quote bank that refuses to become a book

  • A new storefront, asked for by somebody in our community: The Zibaldone. The request was a quote bank — quotes for any state of mind and emotion, seeded by us and added to by anybody. It is built as a commonplace book, which our own glossary has had an entry for since 2022, and named for the Italian miscellany notebook: a zibaldone is a heap of things. Montaigne kept one and invented the essay out of it; the Meditations is one.
  • A quote bank is the most dangerous shape of page this site has built, and the danger has no moment anybody could catch. Nobody decides to republish a book. They add one good line, then another, each individually reasonable, and a year later the page is the work instead of pointing at it. So tools/make-zibaldone.py refuses a quotation over thirty words, the number lives in the tool, and the room prints it — because “we quote lightly” is not something a page can promise and not enforce. Where the line is not enough, the book is linked.
  • No song lyrics, refused structurally rather than by care. Ryan’s reply to the person who asked: song lyric reproduction is an intellectual property minefield in the US. Music publishing enforces on quotation where prose publishing shrugs, the licensing is separate from the recording, and one line is a far larger fraction of a song than of a novel. This street already says that nothing musical is hosted here, and a quote bank is exactly where that promise would have been broken in the way that looked most harmless. The first thing that check ever refused was a false positive — the word song matched Whitman’s Song of Myself — and the pattern was narrowed rather than the poem excepted, which is check-counts.py’s own first-run lesson happening for the third time.
  • Every line names its printing, not its title, and says how it was checked and when. A title is not a source: these texts differ between printings, and a quotation nobody can trace is the thing The Doomscroll dropped a poem over. The Emily Dickinson is the proof it matters — it is in the Second Series of 1891, edited by Mabel Loomis Todd and T. W. Higginson, not the first, and the only thing that found that out was reading the title page above the poem instead of remembering it. The Thoreau is the other proof: the version everybody quotes has somebody marching to a beat, and he wrote neither word. The public domain lines were grepped out of the transcriptions each card names; the contemporary ones were read off our own published pages.
  • A translated work has to clear the public domain twice — a trap this repository had not met before. make-doomscroll.py’s test is the author dead more than seventy years, everywhere rather than in one country; but a translation is a copyrightable work of its own, so a Roman emperor in a modern English is a modern book. The Meditations here is Meric Casaubon’s 1634, and he is credited on the card. Seneca is not in the room at all and the data file says why: our glossary quotes him on words becoming works, that wording is a modern translation the entry does not name, and the public domain one does not read that way. A gap somebody has already checked is worth more than a line nobody can place.
  • There are no faces in that room, and that is the whole answer to “any state of mind”. The obvious build is a grid of moods you pick from, and the obvious drawing for a mood is a face — which on a Disabled people’s site is the emotion flashcard autistic people spend their childhoods being drilled with. The Playhouse settled this for its sound board and the answer is the same here: each card says what it is for in a sentence about a situation, and the tool refuses an emoji in that field. Nothing is ranked, counted or voted on either, checked with make-guild.py’s negation window so the room can still say so out loud.
  • The quotations are set in type and only the margins are handwritten. The room varies the face per entry, which is what was asked for — EB Garamond for the oldest, Newsreader for the nineteenth century, Lora for the living — and Kalam is the hand and never carries a quotation, refused by the tool. A passage in a handwriting face is an access failure wearing atmosphere, which is the lesson The Doomscroll’s blackletter already carries one room over. The margin note on every card is ours, and the tool refuses a quotation mark in it, because margins that could hold quotations would quietly become a second uncited quote bank running down the side of the cited one.
  • The room it could collapse into is every other paper room on this street, and the separation is that all of those are PRINTED. The Mopery is a cold building you stand up in, with type punched into the page by a press. The Doomscroll is one unbroken machine-set roll of cold grey newsprint with nothing cut and nothing rotated. The zine table is a photocopy made in a hundred copies to give away. This is one object at arm’s length, seen from above, written into by hand, and bound — there is a fold down the left of every sheet, and the fold is what makes it a book rather than a page. And where the guild is lit like a photocopy and the garden’s light has come through leaves, this is the one room lit by something in the room with you: a lamp on the left, at desk height, which is why the paper has a tooth and the fold has a shadow in it.
  • The attribution slip is TASL plus the three fields a quotation also needs, and it sends nothing anywhere. Creative Commons’ Title, Author, Source, Licence is the right skeleton for a photograph, where the thing you point at is one file at one address; a quotation comes out of a work, in a printing, and the person copying it found it somewhere else again — and that last field is the one that catches a misquotation, because most of them are copied in good faith off something already wrong. There is no form action and no endpoint: the button writes a block of text to your clipboard and you send it however you already talk to us. Its fields are generated from the same list the cards are checked against, so the day somebody adds a field to an entry cannot be the day the form stops asking for it.
  • A colour under the bar, and a tilt with no reset, both caught before it shipped. The pencil grey carried every citation, margin note and provenance line and measured 4.43 on a pasted leaf; check-contrast.py refuses a colour it has never seen, which is how it surfaced, and it was darkened. check-gentle.py then found all seven leaves still lying at their pasted angles at Gentle: :where() guarantees a tilt cannot be out-ranked but does not conjure a rule to outrank it, and §3’s reset names the shared tilt classes and nothing else. The room resets its own now, which is the fault that shipped on the Faery Yurt’s windowsill. New: data/zibaldone.json, tools/make-zibaldone.py, zibaldone.html, zibaldone.js, §29 of love.css (Small screens and Print renumbered to §30 and §31), EB Garamond, Newsreader, Lora and Kalam self-hosted, the only pale shopfront in the row, a share card that is a page lying on a desk, and a steel nib laid down beside the book with a job on it.

2026-09-22 — The Garden, and a palette that splits at the soil line

  • A gate in the wall, and a garden behind it: The Garden. Ryan’s brief. Our knowledge garden as a place you can walk: an intro to what knowledge gardening is and how this one is tended, one bed for every site we publish, the Garden Spider, and everything needed to start a garden of your own. Every bed links straight out, because each of those sites is already its own room — so it is not a door in the grid but an opening, and it is the one place on a night street where the sun is up.
  • The roster is not in the garden’s own data file. Which sites exist, in what order, with what names and addresses, is read out of data/arrivals.json — the same list The Feed runs on, whose order is stimpunks.org’s own feeds page. So a bed cannot be invented, a site cannot be quietly left out, and re-sorting the garden would mean overruling a decision made somewhere else, which is the rule the Jungle Room keeps about its cams. data/garden.json holds nothing but our own writing about each site.
  • The Feed is the room this one could collapse into, and both rooms list every site we run. So the division is structural rather than tonal: a board is a timetable — a title, a date, a destination and the minute it was set — and this is a planting plan: what a site is for, whose hands are in it, and nothing that changes when somebody publishes. Hence no network in tools/make-garden.py, no pull step beside it, and a refusal of dates in a bed, because that is the edit that would turn one room into the other.
  • The drawings are told apart by FORM, which is the campground’s grammar inverted on purpose. Out in the field every pitch gets one lit colour of its own — the yurt emits, the hermitage catches, the meadow carries — and that runs out at about the fourth pitch, because there are not many kinds of light. There are as many kinds of growth as you like: a rhizome, an umbel in flower, a plant with no chlorophyll in it, a frame with seedlings under glass, a runner rooting a new plant beside itself, a dry seed head, a scramble over the path, a tendril. Each one says something true about its site, and every habit is also written out in a sentence beside its bed, because the drawings are decoration a screen reader never reaches and the habit is the argument.
  • No bed says how much a site holds, and nothing is ranked. No glossary-term count, no sheet count, no biggest or busiest. A number like that is wrong within the week and authoritative-looking meanwhile — the fault The Feed spends a paragraph avoiding — and a garden measured by volume is an inventory. Both are refused in the generator, the ranking one with make-guild.py’s negation window so the room can still say that it does not rank anything.
  • Green on that earth measures 1.38, and no brown can fix it. check-contrast.py put the two greens at 1.38 and 2.14 against the soil, and because they are only 1.55 apart from each other no soil colour clears 3:1 against both at once — so there was no palette fix available and the drawings changed instead. The palette splits at the soil line the way a plant does: green above it, pale below it, so every root, rhizome, mycelial thread, spilled seed and blanched shoot is drawn in the path’s own colour, which is also what a shoot looks like before it reaches the light. make-garden.py walks every coordinate of every green element and refuses one that crosses the line — broken on purpose first, with a stem pushed two pixels under and a leaf circle whose edge dipped below, and it caught both.
  • The two rooms it could collapse into are the two other daylit ones, and the separation is physics. The Solarpunk Hermitage’s light lands on a warm plaster wall, with everything standing in front of it and the shadows falling sideways off a low sun. The Adventurer’s Guild has no light source at all, lit like a photocopy, with nothing casting anything. Here the sun is overhead and has come down through leaves: every pale surface is tinted green, and every shadow is small, hard and directly underneath the thing that made it. And the palest thing in the room is not the ground — a limewashed board is brighter than the path it lies on, where both neighbours put their panels darker than their paper.
  • It sets no display face at all, which is the room’s one claim about type. Every other world here has something that shouts — a woodtype, a condensed grotesque, a fat slab, a blackletter, a pixel face — and a garden has no signage in it, only labels. So Faustina sets the garden’s own name and the label on every bed in the same breath, Mulish carries the notes, and the loud things in the room are the plants. Both are under the SIL Open Font License.
  • The Garden Spider is an orb weaver and not a mascot, and its panel is careful in our own words rather than softer ones: a spider is a guide to the garden and never a replacement for it, its best answer is an entrance, it can be wrong, and every trail it puts you on ends at a page a person wrote. Those lines are from our Ask page, quoted from ourselves, with the ethics pages linked beside them and a way to reach a human instead.
  • Every bed says whose hands are in it, on the face of the bed. Half of these sites were made with Helen Edgar of Autistic Realms; Star Stuff is a collaboration with More Realms; the Cavendish model is David Thornburg’s by way of Steve Silberman’s NeuroTribes; monotropism is Dinah Murray, Wenn Lawson and Mike Lesser’s; the pebbling locution is Amythest Schaber’s. The generator refuses a bed with nobody on it, and refuses a link inside a note that points somewhere that is neither ours nor that bed’s own site — make-hermitage.py’s rule about the Star Stuff table. The with line is the exemption, and that is the whole reason it exists.
  • The leaf shadow is still at Gentle, drifts at Regular and drifts further at MAX. The light is never taken away, because the light is not the wobble — the campground’s own call about its grass. Both are keyframes, so §3 kills them at Gentle with !important; nothing flashes, pulses or strobes at any setting, which is Club Chronic’s rule holding in the brightest room on the street. New: data/garden.json, tools/make-garden.py, the-garden.html, §28 of love.css (Small screens and Print renumbered to §29 and §30), a gate on the street, Faustina and Mulish self-hosted, a share card whose row of labels is read off the beds, and a plant label lying on the path with a job on it.

2026-09-21 — The Feed, and a board that says when it was set

  • A new storefront: The Feed. A dark station concourse with a freestanding split-flap indicator board standing in it for every site we run, read off their own RSS feeds. Ryan’s brief. The object is a Solari di Udine machine and the one in there is drawn from nothing in CSS; it makes no sound.
  • It cannot be live, so it says the minute it was set. None of those hosts sends a browser the header that would let our page read its feed, and the ways round that are a proxy we do not run and a third-party reader we would be handing visitors to without asking. So tools/pull-arrivals.py reads the feeds, tools/make-arrivals.py draws the board, every plate carries the minute its own wire was read, and the hall carries the minute the board was set — both generated from the same file the rows come out of. This is make-jungle.py’s refused runtime upside down: a live camera has no length to give and publishing one would be a number that goes quietly wrong, and a board has a time it was set, so leaving that off would be the identical fault the other way up.
  • A row is a title, a date and a destination, and the tool refuses one carrying a summary. A departures board does not read you the contents of the train — which is the metaphor and also what keeps that page from republishing everybody else’s writing onto one surface it does not own. That edit is a friendly one and it will arrive: the rows look bare, somebody helpfully pastes the opening lines back in, and the board has become a reader. Refused rather than merely not done, the way make-sweetgrass.py refuses a reading marked as a screen.
  • Nothing is ranked and no site’s activity is added up. No busiest, no quietest, no rows-per-week, nothing saying which of our sites has been getting on with it. A board that showed that would turn publishing into a race between our own people, most of whom are Disabled and several of whom are one person — the pebbling cabinet’s refusal of a tally, arriving between sites rather than inside a game. The vocabulary is refused in the room’s own copy, with the negation window make-guild.py uses so the room can still say that it does not rank anything.
  • Each wire names which feed it carries, because two of those sites publish more than one. “The latest from Star Stuff” is an ambiguous sentence when Star Stuff keeps a feed of the pieces and a feed of the reasoning, and a board that picked quietly between them would be answering a question nobody knew it had been asked. The running order is not ours either: it is the order those sites appear in on stimpunks.org’s own feeds page, which is the Jungle Room’s rule about its cams. And this street is on its own board, fetched over the network like every other wire, so the board can be out of date about this site too.
  • The seam through the middle of the letters had to go, twice. A real flap splits straight through the glyphs, so that is what it was: one near-black hairline at 50%. On the share card the row lettered NEVER read as a word struck through. Two solid faces with a lit fold between them fixed the card and then failed worse on a phone, where a destination wraps to three lines and 50% of a tall row lands inside somebody’s second line. The reason is structural and is not going away: a real board’s rows are one line of fixed-width characters and cannot wrap, and ours carry the titles people actually gave their pages. There is no proportion of a row that is not inside a sentence, so the lower leaf sits at the foot of the flap, where no width can push it into a word.
  • The dial governs the clatter and never the arrivals. Still at Gentle, one downward settle per row at Regular, a riffle before settling at MAX. Both are keyframes rather than static transforms, deliberately — §3 kills animation at Gentle with !important and nothing out-specifies that by accident, so the fault check-gentle.py exists to catch has nowhere to live here. Neither animation touches opacity, because three fast opacity steps in a row is a flicker whatever it is called, and Club Chronic’s rule holds on the whole street.
  • The room it could collapse into is the Adventurer’s Guild, and this is the hardest case since The Den. Both rooms are made of ruled rows, both are square, and in both of them nothing is rotated and nothing overlaps — and that room’s own section says its tidiness cannot spread. What holds them apart is not the layout, because the layout really is the same: the guild has no light source and no depth, lit like a photocopy of a document, with nothing casting anything, and this is nothing but light and depth. A clerk filed that; an engineer bolted this together and switched it on.
  • And the light is a third kind of transmitted light, because a neighbour already owns the idea. Swaying Sweetgrass is lit through a translucent body — diffuse, no edges, nothing with a lit face. This is lit through an aperture: a hard light behind a mask, so every letter has a crisp edge and every flap throws a hairline shadow. New: data/arrivals.json, tools/pull-arrivals.py, tools/make-arrivals.py, the-feed.html, §27 of love.css (Small screens and Print renumbered to §28 and §29), Michroma and Space Mono self-hosted, a share card, and a job marker on the concourse floor.

2026-09-21 — a contrast checker that reads the page instead of the intention

  • check-contrast.py is a list of pairs somebody wrote down. tools/check-contrast-live.py is the page. It renders all 29 and walks every piece of text against the ground it actually sits on — what check-gentle.py already does for motion, and what nothing did for colour. It exists because the pair list was blind three times, each found by a person looking: a link colour nobody ever decided, a decided colour that lost the cascade, and three hexes that had never rendered at all. Same shape every time — the colour that renders is not the colour that was written down.
  • It found the skip link unreadable in twenty-one rooms. .skip carries its own yellow ground and near-black ink at (0,1,0); every room sets .room-x a at (0,2,0). So the first control a keyboard user meets took each room’s link colour on a yellow box: 1.26 in the chapel, 1.10 in the arcade, 1.00 — yellow on yellow — on every plain room. Nobody had seen it because it sits off-screen until focus and nobody screenshots a focused skip link. It carries !important now, for the reason §2’s [hidden] guard does.
  • And it found two of the Hermitage’s bean bags under the bar. The chairs take their colour inline out of data/hermitage.json; the checker had three lighter hexes in it that the page has never used, so every run passed while the magenta sat at 2.79 and the teal at 3.38 against the 4.5 their copy needs. The constants are read from the page’s own values now and the failure is written into the checker’s notes beside the Pink Pony Club’s, because which colours those chairs are is a choice about the room rather than a checker’s to make.
  • Three dead custom properties came out of :root with it. --bag-1/2/3 were referenced by nothing — .bag reads var(--bag), singular, which only ever exists on the element. A dead declaration is not harmless when something else is checking against it, and the old checker’s own guard caught them the moment the constants were corrected.
  • Its first run reported 314 failures and most of them were the tool’s fault, which is the point of breaking a checker before believing it. About a sixth of this site’s text stands on a linear-gradient with no background-colour under it — the Doomscroll’s newsprint, The Den’s shag — and walking up to the body reported 222 pieces of pale text as near-black on near-black. It declines rather than guesses now: a gradient in the chain makes a piece of text unmeasurable, counted and reported as such, and left to the hand-written list where those grounds are recorded with their measurements. It also unhides every [hidden] element and opens every <details> before measuring, because the bug it was written for was in a control that ships hidden.
  • Both findings are fixed and the sweep is clean: 0 under the bar on 29 pages. The Hermitage’s magenta and teal chairs were lightened rather than re-coloured — hue and saturation untouched, all three now sitting together at 4.80 to 4.82. Taking the lighter palette the checker had been assuming would have put the teal at 7.34 and made one chair a different weight from its neighbours, in a room whose own comment says no two of them are the same colour because nobody furnishes a room like this by agreeing. The Pebble Board’s slug ink was darkened by four thousandths of lightness to clear 4.41. New: tools/check-contrast-live.py.

2026-09-21 — six more toys, and three links in that room that measured 1.31

  • The blank column in the Playhouse is full. Ryan looked at the page and asked for more buttons. The toys grid was 353px of tiles beside 1293px of prose and dial, so six more close it: THE ECHO, THE DOUBLE EMPATHY TELEPHONE, THE SAFE FOOD TIN, THE SPOON DRAWER, THE OFF SWITCH and THE INFODUMP. Ten tiles, ten fills, none repeated.
  • Every toy is an idea out of our own glossary wearing a costume, and a line under the tile says which idea. Echolalia, the double empathy problem, safe food, spoon theory, infodumping. The slugs were checked against the Knowledge System mirror rather than fetched, because on stimpunks.org a 301 is a failure rather than a pass and a live fetch will cheerfully confirm a page that does not exist — curb cut effect was dropped for being in our posts and not in the glossary. A toy is not an explainer: the tile does the thing, the line says where the thing came from, and nothing teaches, quizzes or asks anybody to show they have understood.
  • The credit is on the face of the tile and make-toys.py refuses one that is not. Damian Milton on the telephone, Christine Miserandino on the spoon drawer. A credit recorded in a data file and not in the copy is a credit nobody reads, which on this site is the one habit kept when the other three were dropped.
  • The spoon drawer hands out the sentences and not the spoons. The obvious build gives you a spoon every time and never runs out, which is generous and quietly deletes the limit Miserandino wrote the essay to describe. It holds the things you can say to somebody across a table instead, which is what she invented spoons for. Nothing is counted and nothing is deducted — the tool refuses the scoring vocabulary on every tile, because the stim box already says “nobody is counting” as a joke about counting and the joke does not survive a second toy repeating it straight.
  • The double empathy telephone never says which account was right, because the idea it is wearing is that neither was wrong. Two handsets, one encounter, two honest reports of it; pick up both ends and the next press is a new call. Its tile is a <div> and the handsets are the buttons, since a button cannot be nested in a button and the alternative was one control pretending to be two.
  • The off switch has no are-you-sure. It stops every sound on the page at once, closes the audio context so nothing the sound board has scheduled survives, and says so. A stop button that asks whether you meant it is not a stop button, and that room can now stack nine sound-board keys and two recorded yells with no single way out.
  • An aria-live region does not announce text it already holds, and two of these toys exist to repeat themselves — the echo says the last thing again, the tin says the same sentence forever. Written the obvious way both are silent to the one reader who most needs them while looking perfectly correct on screen, which is a failure with no symptom for anybody watching. The region is cleared and set back on the next tick, which is what makes a repeat a change.
  • An HTML entity is right in a blurb and wrong in a payload, and nothing in a data file shows which is which. The blurb is written into the markup as HTML; anything that becomes a data- attribute is written by the script with textContent, so &ldquo; there arrives on the page as five literal characters. The spoon drawer shipped its first draft that way. The tool refuses an entity in a payload field now.
  • The generator caught a fill collision on its first run. The newcomer had taken .toy--violet, which the word clock has owned since the room opened, and two rules in one section would have settled it by source order in silence. That is check-classes.py’s collision arriving inside a section, where that tool deliberately does not look. The newcomer was renamed rather than out-specified.
  • And that room had three live contrast failures nothing could see. .room-play a was yellow, which is right on the near-black boxes — and every link on the page sits either on the blue ground or inside a white card. The house-rules links measured 1.31, the quest marker’s hand-in link 3.88, on a page published since the street opened. check-contrast.py holds a pair for every colour somebody decided on, and an inherited colour was never decided, so there was nothing to hold a pair for — the exact hole the Doomscroll left, found again in an older room. The room decides at each ground it actually has now: white on the blue at 5.08, the room’s own blue on the white cards at 5.08. Every link in there was re-measured afterwards.
  • Every toy makes a noise now, because six of them making none read as breakage. In a room where nine other things answer out loud, the quiet ones feel faulty rather than quiet — Ryan's report. Each one is its own short synthesised sound, no two alike and none alike to the sound board's keys: an echo of a blip, a line opening, one unchanging note for the safe food tin, a teaspoon against a mug, two notes down for the off switch, and a fast run for the infodump. make-toys.py refuses a toy without one. The off switch's noise comes after the silence, which is the only order that makes sense for that button, and its copy says so.
  • And the echo plays the last sound back as well as the last words, which is Ryan's idea and makes the toy the thing its name says it is — including sounds made by the board next to it. The mechanism is that a toy's noise is a factory: called once to choose, handing back a function that plays that choice. The obvious version, calling the toy's noise again, silently advances anything that varies, so the echo of the stim box would be the next note up the scale and nothing would look wrong. Measured: two stim presses give 659 then 784, two echoes give 784 twice, and the next press resumes at 880; a recorded yell echoes as that person's yell rather than a fresh draw.
  • Four of the new answer boxes were rendering near-black on near-black at 1.00:1, and three more between 1.28 and 1.48. .toy--orange span was written when a tile held one span; the readouts arrived later, also spans, and that rule is (0,2,0) against .toy__said at (0,1,0). Styling by element is a rule that cannot see what it will hit. The blurb has a name now and every fill rule is scoped to it. check-contrast.py passed the whole time, because it holds pairs somebody wrote down and not pairs that render — the pair was declared correctly and lost the cascade. That is the second time in a day the pair list was blind, after the inherited link colour, and both were found by a person looking at the page.
  • The first attempt at that fix missed a tile, and so did the check on it. The rescope was a regex expecting one space before span; .toy--green  span had two. The grep that verified the work carried the same assumption and reported clean. Break the check differently from the fix.
  • And then the buttons looked broken, because the room answered somewhere nobody could see. Every press wrote into the one live region at the top of the page, which is off the screen by the time anybody has scrolled to the toys — let alone to the sound board underneath them. Ryan pressed the toys he had asked for an hour earlier and thought they were faulty. Nothing caught it because every check, ours included, was reading the box: the handler fired, the text was right, the live region updated, and the person pressing saw nothing. That is the 0×0 iframe again in a different shape — the DOM correct and the screen wrong. Every toy answers on the tile you pressed now, and the sound board in one strip under its keys, since a key is a hundred pixels wide and a sentence does not fit on one. One readout at a time, all of them aria-hidden with #playhouse-says still the only live region, and all of them shipping hidden so a page without scripts shows no empty boxes.
  • Chairy’s blurb stopped being a hand-typed number. It said “one of twenty-eight things”, which had been right by luck since the day the sayings stopped being five; it is generated from data/chairy.json now. New: data/toys.json, tools/make-toys.py. The tool carries Chairy’s sayings and the yell button’s recordings across untouched, because a generator that silently empties another generator’s output is the _headers trap with two tools in it instead of one.

2026-09-21 — a sound board in the Playhouse, and a row of moods with no faces on it

  • The Playhouse has a sound board on the wall. Ryan’s brief: everything in that room is a button, so a three-by-three board of stimmy noise keys, with the bottom row holding mood keys that toggle between happy, sad and angry noises, and a different visual fanfare for each key at MAX GLITTER. A click, a pop, a rainstick, a chime, a purr and a squeak; then a hum, a spring and a drum with three moods each.
  • The bottom row is not a feelings chart, and that is why there are no faces on it. A face is the first thing anybody sketches for a mood key, and a row of happy/sad/angry faces on a Disabled people’s site is the emotion flashcard — the thing autistic self-advocates spend their lives being drilled with. What a mood is here is the shape of a noise: a hum that lifts a third and stays up, the same hum sagging a tone and a half, two drones fourteen cents apart buzzing against each other. Each one is a word, a drawn waveform and a sound, and the room says so in its own copy rather than leaving the absence for somebody to helpfully fill in. It also says that a sad noise is a perfectly good thing to want, and that you can leave a key on angry and go and do something else.
  • A mood key shows the mood it is about to play. Press it and you get that noise; the key then offers the next one. The face of a button should describe what pressing it does, which the alternative — naming what you just heard — does not. The flourish keeps the colour of the noise that just happened while the label has already moved on, which is why that button carries two attributes and not one.
  • Every noise is synthesised in the browser and nothing is fetched, so the room’s own house rule stays true: it is silent when you walk in and silent if you never touch anything. Oscillators, filters and a buffer of white noise, built inside the click handler — the AudioContext cannot start without a gesture, which is the platform enforcing the promise rather than us making it. A bell is 880 and 2.76 times 880, which is the whole difference between a bell and a sine; a purr is a trill at twenty-two on the gain rather than on the pitch; a bubble goes up, which every first draft of that sound gets backwards.
  • Every key says what it sounds like, in words, on the press. A board made of sounds is the one thing on this street that can lock somebody out completely, so the room’s live region gets the noise described rather than just its name — the Jungle Room’s rule that colour is never the only channel, in a room where the channel is audio. make-soundboard.py refuses a key without that line, and refuses two keys that claim to sound the same.
  • Three files have to agree about every key and none of them is near the others, so the tool reads all three: the markup is in the page, the noise is a registered voice() in love.js, the colour and the flourish are rules in love.css. A key whose voice is missing is not an error anywhere — it is make-chappell.py’s typo that never becomes a video, arriving in a room where the press is the content. A key whose flourish is missing is quietly ordinary at MAX, which nobody would notice because nobody screenshots MAX. Both refuse now, along with two keys sharing a flourish, a mood key anywhere but the last row, and a mood set that is not the same three in the same order.
  • Nothing on it is counted. No total, no streak, nothing kept between visits. A board of nine keys is exactly the shape of thing that grows a press counter, and the stim box one object over already counts its own presses as a joke about counting — a joke that does not survive being repeated by a second object with a straight face. The pebbling cabinet’s refusal of a tally, arriving a third time.
  • Nine flourishes and no two alike, at MAX GLITTER only. Rings, bubbles, falling hairlines, a burst thrown eight ways, widening bars, darts, flat arcs off the bottom edge, a stretching coil, confetti — each clipped to its own key, so the flourish belongs to the button that made the noise. Everything moves with a translate or a scale and nothing rotates, which is arcade.js’s call and quest.js’s: rotation and skew are all check-gentle.py can read, and a checker must not be asked to take a room’s word for something. Nothing flashes and nothing strobes — Club Chronic’s rule, in the loudest room on the street. At Gentle nothing is built at all and the words are identical; measured at all three settings, along with the rendered boxes of the pieces themselves, because “the elements exist” is a question about the DOM and this was a question about paint.
  • Red was the obvious colour for angry and it measures 3.06 on the mood keys’ near-black. Angry is orange at 8.03, sad is cyan at 10.7 and happy is yellow at 14.5. The mood ink is on the word as well as on the line, so these are body-text pairs rather than graphics ones. Seven new pairs.
  • A media block written above the rules it overrides loses exactly the declarations the base rule also sets, and keeps the rest. A new failure shape here, and it renders as a layout nobody wrote: the phone block sat before .stimpad, so flex-flow and a font size lost to the base rule while a flex basis the base rule does not set went straight through. Half a breakpoint. It looked like a stale stylesheet and it was source order.
  • And it is three across where there is room and one column on a phone. Held at three columns at 375px, a key gets about seventy pixels of text column and every blurb sets one word to a line. Hiding the blurbs to keep the grid would have been the lite-version mistake arriving through a breakpoint, so the grid gave way; the sentence describing the board’s shape is generated and carries its own caveat. New: data/soundboard.json, tools/make-soundboard.py.

2026-09-21 — The Adventurer’s Guild, and the one tidy room

  • The Adventurer’s Guild has opened a shopfront on the street. Ryan’s brief: the trope with the inn and the job board, jobs ranked by difficulty class, sending people on quests across the rooms; a discreet clickable drawing in each room as the quest target; a code revealed at the target and redeemed at the board; hints; and fanfare that follows the HOW LOUD DO YOU WANT IT? dial.
  • The room is tidy, and that is its entire costume. On a street whose architecture is that nothing matches, somebody has come in and filed everything: manila, printer’s ink, hairline rules, ruled rows, a class in the margin, a stamp. Nothing in §26 has a gradient, a shadow, a rounded corner or a rotation in it. It is the second room you can read by daylight and it has no light source in it at all — the Hermitage has a sun in it, with a direction and shadows falling away from it; this is lit the way a photocopy of a document is lit, evenly, from nowhere. And it is the second pale ground on a dark street: the Doomscroll is one unbroken roll of foxed newsprint with nothing ruled on it, and this is made of rules.
  • It states its own link colour, at the room, which is the hole the Doomscroll left the day before: that page declared a colour for one line and every other link fell through to the base stylesheet’s pink, at about 2.4 on paper, and the contrast checker had nothing to hold a pair for because nobody had decided anything.
  • Every room on the street carries a marker, and no two markers are alike. A staple on the zine table, a token on the arcade carpet, a spindle adapter on the den floor, a tent peg in the field. make-guild.py holds one drawing per room and refuses a room it has no drawing for — make-og.py’s refusal, for make-og.py’s reason, because the failure mode of guessing is one shared glyph quietly appearing in every room on a street whose whole point is that the rooms share nothing. Each marker is drawn in its room’s own declared colours and every one is held against the floor it lies on at the body-text threshold, the Jungle Room’s quills rule: a marker you cannot pick out of the floor is a control you cannot use. The Playhouse lost that argument to its own floor — its yellow measures 3.88 on that blue, so the block is white at 5.08.
  • The marker is a native <details>, and the way out of a question is another one. So the code, the hints and the “give me the answer” escape all work with JavaScript off; the scripted part is only the answer box, which ships hidden and is revealed by quest.js, so a page without scripts shows no dead control. Discreet means visually quiet and nothing else: every marker is a full-size target in the tab order with a real name on it, which is The Den’s door rule. And every marker is on the floor of its room, said out loud on the board, because scattering them at a different depth in each room would have made finding one a test of eyesight and of a mouse rather than a walk between rooms.
  • The marker is never the play button, although the example in the brief starts from one. A facade is two elements and love-embed.js replaces the pressed <button> with a bare <div class="facade">, so anything it was wearing evaporates — the fault that shipped Swaying Sweetgrass’s players at 0×0 the day before. A play button that also does a second thing hands a popup to somebody who only wanted the song, and gating a quest behind a press would put it behind consenting to a third-party frame. So the George Jones job is a spindle adapter on the den floor and its question is what makes you read the tile: who wrote She Thinks I Still Care, which the room credits to Dickey Lee and Steve Duffy on the cut itself.
  • Nothing is scored, and the tool refuses the vocabulary. No total, no percentage, no streak, no rank you climb, nothing that expires. A job board is exactly the shape of thing that grows a score, and a score beside a walking tour of a Disabled people’s site turns a wander into a workload — the pebbling cabinet’s refusal of a tally, in the room most likely to talk itself out of it. The difficulty class is how far you walk, enumerated in the tool and defined by geography, so it says nothing whatever about the person doing it.
  • The first thing that tool ever refused was a false positive, and the pattern was narrowed rather than excepted. “A book it points at” is the verb, and a scoring pattern that cannot tell it from a points system is check-counts.py’s first draft refusing “no two rooms alike”. A point is only a point now when it is being awarded or counted.
  • The fanfare follows the dial and the words never do. Gentle says its piece and adds nothing; Regular chimes; MAX chimes properly and throws sparks that rise once and fade. The sentences are identical at all three, and nothing flashes or strobes at any setting — Club Chronic’s rule. The sparks move with a translate rather than a rotation, deliberately, because rotation and skew are all check-gentle.py can read and a checker must not be asked to take a room’s word for something.
  • A marker’s link carries its code to the board and stops there. It fills the box; the visitor still presses. That press is what the fanfare is consented by, and it is the accessible path for anybody who finds typing expensive, which is most of the reason it exists.
  • The board holds a checksum of each code rather than a second copy, and the tool says out loud that this is a courtesy and not a lock. Every code is printed in plain text in the markup of a public page — that is the mechanism. What the checksum buys is that opening the source of the board is not a spoiler sheet for every room at once.
  • check-quests.py reads the published HTML and never the data file. The generator writes both surfaces, so they cannot disagree the moment it runs; this is about every moment after that, when a generated line sitting in a committed file gets edited by hand. That is the frame-src shape exactly. It found a bug in itself on its first run: it sliced each marker at the next </details>, the marker’s own hints are <details>, and it reported eighteen missing escapes that were all present. It slices on the generator’s end marker now.
  • check-classes.py caught this page wearing two of the street’s classes — pavement and masthead__dial — which is the tagline leak again, and the newcomer was renamed rather than out-specified. The guild ends in its own voice, like every other room.
  • The marker does not print. It is a control whose whole content is a code to type into a page you cannot type into on paper, and the zine room’s one-sheet promise has been broken by a single paragraph twice.
  • Pitch 01 got a marker and it is ours, not Helen Edgar’s. A candle stub on the Faery Yurt’s windowsill, drawn in her own declared colours, added on Ryan’s call so that her pitch is not the one room on the street with no job in it — and written into the liner notes beside the one colour of hers this repository has changed, because a change to a contributor’s room should be visible somewhere other than a diff. Helen has the final say on whether it stays.
  • Two pages carry no marker, on purpose and by name in the tool. The changelog is a dated record and writing new furniture into a log is the same instinct as rewriting a September entry to satisfy a linter; the guild is where jobs are handed in, so a marker there would be a job that sends you to the room you are standing in. Everything else refuses to ship without one.
  • Two faces nothing else here sets — Rye, a woodtype face for posted bills, and Public Sans, which was drawn for United States government forms, which is the room’s joke set twice. Thirty-five new contrast pairs and one measured ornament. New: data/quests.json, tools/make-guild.py, tools/check-quests.py, quest.js. love.css gains §26 and the two cross-cutting sections renumber to §27 and §28.
  • Still to come: the bed. A guild is an inn as well as a board, and this one has no room over it yet. When it does it will be about rest as something owed rather than earned, which means it will owe a great deal to The Nap Ministry and Tricia Hersey and will say so in its own copy. The board says that out loud rather than leaving a gap.

2026-09-21 — Swaying Sweetgrass, and a book we are not going to serve

  • Swaying Sweetgrass is pitched on pitch 02, which had been open ground since the field opened. Ryan’s brief: a meadow of sweetgrass left alone, except for a natural-surface path a wheelchair can use, running to a clearing at the centre with a communal fire pit under open sky.
  • The light is behind the grass, and that is the only thing keeping this out of a family it would otherwise join. The Faery Yurt, The Latibulum, The Den, the Hermitage’s cave and The Mopery are every one of them lit by a flame you could put your hand over. Here the sun is low, a hundred feet off and behind everything, so nothing has a lit face at all — the blades glow because the light is coming through them. That is why the greens go yellow while the ground stays dark, and it is a different warmth from tallow or varnish. There is no furniture in a meadow either; the only built things are the path and the fire ring.
  • The fire is painted in the room’s own two golds and given no colour of its own, deliberately. A flame with an orange to itself would have made this the sixth flame-lit room on the street by the back door.
  • Ryan’s brief pointed at 21 chapters of Braiding Sweetgrass read aloud, and they are published as doors rather than screens. The playlist is uploaded by The Anarchist Audio Library and is an unauthorised reading of a book that is in copyright — Milkweed Editions, 2013, Robin Wall Kimmerer living, publishing, and narrating the real audiobook herself. Every chapter plays and every chapter embeds, so nothing in the machinery would have stopped them going in beside the fire: love-embed.js only asks whether an id is well formed and check-jukebox.py only asks whether a video works. What stopped them is that this room’s whole argument is the honorable harvest — never take the first, never take more than you need, ask — and a page making that argument cannot serve somebody else’s entire book off its own surface while doing it. Ryan’s call once the provenance was put to him: keep both, her voice as the fire and these as a way out. make-sweetgrass.py refuses a reading marked as a screen, so the decision survives being tidied by somebody who thinks the doors look broken next to the presses.
  • What is at the fire instead is Kimmerer in her own voice — her publisher, Bioneers, TEDxSitka, Harvard, the New York Botanical Garden, On Being, the Museum of Science, the Natural History Institute, the Commons KU — shortest first, so three minutes and an hour and a half are both a real option. Every id was resolved against YouTube on the day: status, playableInEmbed, length, title and channel, off one fetch of each watch page. That check is the only reason the two lists are two lists; the readings arrived looking exactly like the talks and the channel is what told them apart. The build refuses a talk whose channel is the readings’ channel, in either direction.
  • The 21-strand teaching is somebody’s, and the video does not say whose. Seven generations behind, seven sacred laws, seven ahead. It was matched to its source rather than guessed at — the transcript panel was opened and read against the words, and it is Sweet Grass Teaching, published by Shawenim Abinoojii Inc., at 0:53, which was already in Ryan’s own field-guide list. The description says only “Made for Shawenim Abinoojii Inc.”, there is no name card at the end and the channel does not say, so the room credits the organisation and states plainly that the Elder is unnamed there rather than inventing a name. That is the herbarium’s refusal to guess at a binomial, applied to a person. make-sweetgrass.py counts the bundles and refuses one that is not seven — the opposite of check-counts.py’s rule and for the opposite reason: a count that belongs to somebody else is a fact to protect, not a total that goes stale.
  • Nothing on that page teaches sweetgrass. Six teachings from Indigenous organisations, institutes and nations, Ryan’s selection, each linked and credited with one line of ours saying what it is about — and nothing anywhere instructing anybody how to harvest, braid or burn it, because that is not ours to hand out.
  • A correction we owe is on the page rather than in a commit message. Our own glossary attributes a framework called reciprocal ethical unity to Barry Lopez and he never wrote the phrase; Star Stuff’s zine No. 72 went and read the essay. The room is quoting from the pages that carry the error, so it says so near the top instead of further down. When two of our own pages disagree, the one that checked wins.
  • Three of the four passages were checked against the mirror and one could not be. The sheaf, the braid of stories and “becoming indigenous to a place” are verbatim on our own Nature, Place-Based Education and Offline pages. The Cajete and goldenrod-and-asters passage is on no page of ours, and archive.org’s search-inside was unreachable and Google Books rate-limited every attempt, so it is published on Ryan’s word and the liner notes say so rather than letting it sit among the checked ones looking identical. Zine No. 72’s own caveat on a fourth is still open and is recorded too.
  • Ryan’s ten seconds of backlit bunchgrass is in there, and it is not sweetgrass. He said so when he sent it. It is already published by us on the Nature entry, underneath the line about immediate contact with the outdoors, which is exactly the argument: place-based means the ground you are standing on, not the sacred meadow somewhere else. The plant is deliberately not named to species — nobody here determined it, and a plant named on a page reads as a determination.
  • VideoPress is the third origin this site will build a frame for, and getting it there took two goes. It was added to _headers by hand, confirmed in the file, and shipped — but that CSP line is generated by make-csp.py from a string literal of its own, so every later run put the old list back. The dev server serves no headers at all, so the room worked locally and the live site refused the frame; Ryan found it by pressing the button. The allowlist is one place now: love-embed.js’s ORIGINS array, which make-csp.py reads to build frame-src and make-sweetgrass.py reads to refuse a bad URL at build time. Both refuse outright rather than falling back to a default if the array is missing, because a silently narrowed frame-src is a room full of blank boxes and a silently widened one is a hole.
  • Verified in a network panel: zero iframes and zero external requests on load, with eighteen presses on the page; one press produced exactly one iframe, at the right URL, with the right referrer policy, and left the other seventeen alone.
  • The field’s sign for the pitch needed a light of its own, because the two on that board are taken. The yurt emits and the hermitage catches, so this one is carried: fireflies low in the grass, many small lights and no source you could point at, with the path going in and a thread of smoke where the clearing is. The fire is not drawn lit, because from the treeline the grass is taller than the clearing and what you can see from there is what gets drawn.
  • Two name collisions, one of them caught by a tool and one by six sudden failures. check-classes.py refuses a custom property declared twice; and an unprefixed GLOW in check-contrast.py’s own Python silently repainted the Faery Yurt’s ember composite, turning six of Helen’s pairs red in a room nobody had touched. That is the --leaf collision arriving in a second file. The newcomer was renamed, scoped to its own block.
  • Two layout faults the room’s own argument caught. The rule under each recording sat wherever its text happened to stop, so a row of two had its ground at two heights — in a room whose stated structure is that things stand on a line. Same for the three bundles of the braid. Both now fill their row and push the press to the foot.
  • Two faults in one press, found by Ryan pressing a button. A facade is two elements — a <button> before the press and a <div> after it — and love-embed.js gives the replacement only the class facade, so anything the button was wearing evaporates. The portrait clip came out landscape and letterboxed; worse, the fire’s players rendered at 0×0 while the sound played, because align-items: flex-start on that column stopped the div stretching and the iframe inside it is absolutely positioned, so it contributes no size.
