The column defaults to stretch now and only the button opts out, and the portrait box is scoped from its container the way the Pebble Board already did it. Both had passed a check that confirmed one press produced one iframe at the right URL — which is a question about the DOM, where this was a question about paint. The rendered boxes are measured now: 16/9, 16/9 and 9/16.
  • Two faces nothing else here sets — Eczar, whose serifs flare like a blade opening, and Alegreya Sans, a sans with a handwriting skeleton still in it. Sixteen new contrast pairs and two measured ornaments. New: data/sweetgrass.json, tools/make-sweetgrass.py. The room joins check-jukebox.py’s lists in the same commit that opens it. love.css gains §25 and the two cross-cutting sections renumber to §26 and §27.

2026-09-21 — The Mopery, and a facsimile this site is not going to copy

  • The Mopery is open — a dark academia library, reading room and parlour, with two rooms behind it. Ryan’s brief: goth, brooding, vampire librarian, used book shop, moon phases, eldritch tomes.
  • It is the first COLD enclosed room on this street, and that is the whole reason it can stand beside the other four. The Faery Yurt, The Latibulum, The Den and the Hermitage’s cave are each lit by one warm source and the light reaches the walls — canvas, plaster, panelling and curtain all go amber. This room has candles the whole way along every top shelf and the walls stay blue-black stone, because that is what a candle actually does: it lights about a foot of a very large room. Cosy is what those rooms are. This is what it is like to be let into a building after everybody has gone home.
  • The type is the other half of it. IM Fell English is a face that was punched — Fell’s Oxford types with the ink spread and the battered counters left in — against the yurt’s Cormorant, which is high-contrast, clean and cut yesterday. Two warm serif rooms kept apart at the stroke rather than at the palette, which is the only place it holds.
  • One scan is shown and one is pointed at, and the difference is a single line of metadata. The reading nook was meant to show a scanned page beside the text. The Library of Congress’s facsimile of The Raven in Poe’s own hand is the obvious candidate and it carries “reproduction prohibited without permission” along the foot of its own first leaf — that printing was made in 1949 for an exhibition at Yale, and loc.gov answers a script with a bot check rather than with its rights statement, so the notice is the only rights information anybody here had actually read. Copying it onto a CC BY-SA site would be relicensing somebody else’s asset under a licence it may not carry, which is the Penguin Pebbling problem with a different deck. Ryan then found one that says the opposite in as many words: an 1865 Tyndale & Mitchell printing, illustrations engraved on wood by Scattergood, digitised by Lehigh University and published by the Internet Archive under the Public Domain Mark 1.0. Three leaves of it are in the room and the whole book is linked. The facsimile is still linked and still not copied, and the nook says which is which and why.
  • All twenty-six of Gustave Doré’s engravings are in the poem, from the 1884 edition by way of Project Gutenberg’s ebook #17192. Doré died in 1883, so they are out of copyright everywhere — the same test the Doomscroll applies to its poets. Ryan asked for them.
  • Where each plate sits is our decision and the page says so. That edition printed the poem straight through and put the plates after it, each captioned with the lines it illustrates. Here every plate stands at the lines its own caption quotes, which is a reading decision rather than a reproduction of the 1884 layout — so the room states it rather than letting anybody assume they are looking at that book.
  • And the captions caught a textual difference nobody was looking for. Matching twenty-six captions to stanzas by their opening words failed on exactly two, because the 1884 edition reads “’T is some visiter” where our text reads “’Tis”, and “Be that word our sign of parting” where ours reads “Be that our sign”. The captions are printed verbatim and the difference is left visible rather than smoothed out. Two printings of one poem are not one text, which is the fact the Doomscroll is built on and the reason The Darkling Thrush is not on it.
  • Every image in the room carries alt text and the build refuses one that does not — twenty-six engravings, three scanned leaves and thirteen plates in the deck, each described rather than left as a rectangle. Two new mounts were measured while they were at it: the plates and the leaves sit on paper-coloured grounds, because a near-white engraving dropped straight onto blue-black stone floats with no object under it.
  • The poem is set in whichever voice you like, and the picker holds every typeface on the street. That is the one place here where borrowing all of them is the joke rather than the failure: nothing of the Playhouse arrives when you read The Raven in Titan One, only the letterforms. @font-face is lazy, so choosing a face is what downloads it and a reader who never opens the picker never pays for one. The choice is not remembered between visits — the only thing this site stores is somebody’s tolerance, and quietly re-applying a typeface next week would be the page deciding it knew how you wanted to read.
  • One screen, many versions of one song, and tuning is not playing. The parlour carries Paint It Black nine ways behind two guttering candles — the Rolling Stones’ own record, then eight people answering the cello arrangement Danny Elfman and Chris Bacon wrote for Wednesday. The behaviour is the Hermitage’s television: with the screen off, previous and next fetch nothing, and the whole listing can be walked without a request leaving the page. Verified in a network panel — three tunings, zero requests, zero iframes; one press produced exactly one iframe at the right id on youtube-nocookie with the right referrer policy.
  • Every cut names Jagger and Richards, and make-mopery.py refuses one that does not. That is The Den’s rule arriving where it bites harder: eight of the nine are covers, the performer’s name is the entire draw, and a wall of covers is precisely where crediting only the voice would pass unnoticed.
  • Two things the link text got wrong and the data does not. One of those videos is played on a viola, not a cello. Another’s own description credits the song to “Mick Jagger (1943)”, which is his year of birth. And one is an acknowledged unofficial upload — the channel says so in the first line of its own description — so the room says so too, rather than dressing it up as a release.
  • Every book can be borrowed before it can be bought, and the tool refuses a title with no way to. Nine novels, and not one of them is a book our own pages already argue from — the opposite of the Hermitage’s shelf, whose rule is that every spine has a citing page. This is a second-hand shop inside a library and a shop stocks what it likes.
  • The links are searches and the room says why. Open Library’s pages and Bookshop’s product pages both answer a script with a bot check, so no product id was invented to paper over it. Every work id was resolved against Open Library’s search API and came back carrying the right title and the right author — the rule data/hermitage.json records the hard way, where an id written from memory pointed at a different book in the file whose own header forbids exactly that.
  • No content notes, and that is a decision rather than an oversight. Ryan’s call, asked directly, with the dark romance on the shelf named in the question. Written into the data file so nobody “finishes” it later.
  • The Oracle Deck asks and does not answer, and the build enforces it. Thirteen engravings between 1514 and 1891 with a question on the back of each. No reversals, no spread, no yes, no no, nothing about what is going to happen to anybody — make-oracle.py refuses a card that does not end in a question mark and refuses the vocabulary of prediction outright. A deck on a Disabled people’s site that told somebody how their life was going to go would be doing the thing the rest of these pages exist to refuse, in the one room dark enough to get away with it, and it would arrive as a friendly edit from somebody who thought a question was a bit thin.
  • And nothing in that room is behind the dice. The whole deck is face up on the page; drawing moves a copy of one card into a slot. A room that made somebody gamble to reach its contents would have put its contents behind chance, which is the “lite version” mistake holding a deck.
  • Every plate is from one collection, and the tool refuses one that is not. Dürer, Mellan, Piranesi, Meryon, Goya, Redon and Bresdin, all public domain works in the Metropolitan Museum’s Open Access collection, released under CC0 — which is what lets that page make one rights statement somebody could check instead of thirteen. CC0 asks for nothing; every card names its artist anyway.
  • The Doomscroll is a feed of public domain doom on an actual scroll, newest first by first publication, back to 1609. The date is when it went public and not when it was written, and for a third of that scroll the two are decades apart: Hopkins sat in a friend’s desk for twenty-nine years, Hardy’s in a drawer for thirty-one, Dickinson’s until four years after she died, retitled and a stanza short. The tool sorts on the year rather than trusting the file, so an entry pasted at the bottom cannot end up rendered at the bottom of a feed that claims to be chronological.
  • Every poem there is out of copyright everywhere, and that cost the scroll its best opener. The rule is the author dead more than seventy years, checked against the current year rather than a date written into the file. T. S. Eliot’s The Hollow Men would have sat at the top and is public domain in America; Eliot died in 1965 and it will not be in much of Europe until the 2030s. A site that serves everybody cannot publish somebody’s whole poem on a technicality that holds in one country.
  • The Darkling Thrush is not on that scroll either, for the other reason: Wikisource’s page for it names no printing and carries its own banner saying the source document is unknown. These poems exist in several printings and they differ — the 1890 Dickinson is retitled and edited, the 1609 Shakespeare keeps its compositor’s slip. A text nobody can trace is the one thing a scroll built out of dates cannot carry.
  • The zine table is the room the scroll could have collapsed into, and the split is the object. The zine is a photocopy: stark white, pure black, cut with scissors, scattered at angles, and the page is the sheet. This is foxed newsprint, warm and grey, one unbroken roll with nothing cut and nothing rotated, lying on a desk you can see the edges of. The blackletter sets the masthead and nothing else — never a headline, never a line of a poem, because eighteen lines of blackletter is an access failure wearing atmosphere.
  • All three popups are clones of markup already on the page, not renderings of data- attributes: one copy of every book’s note and every poem’s text in the document, so a shelf cannot disagree with its own popup. With scripts off, each room’s <noscript> unhides them — the library becomes a catalogue and the doomscroll becomes more itself, a very long scroll you keep going down.
  • check-gentle.py caught a decorative rotation before it shipped. The deck’s cut-rule lozenge was a square turned 45°, which is indistinguishable from a room’s tilt outranking somebody’s Gentle setting, because all that tool can see is the computed matrix. It is clipped out of a square now — the same call arcade.js made when it moved its sprites with left/top rather than transform.
  • And check-counts.py caught two totals, one of them in a note about Piranesi’s prisons that had nothing to do with this street at all.
  • Six faces nothing else here sets, forty-three new contrast pairs and four measured ornaments. New: tools/make-mopery.py, tools/make-oracle.py, tools/make-doomscroll.py, mopery.js, oracle.js, doomscroll.js. The Mopery joins check-jukebox.py’s lists in the same commit that opens it. love.css gains §22 to §24 and the two cross-cutting sections renumber to §25 and §26.

2026-09-21 — a noticeboard that is not a shopfront, and a rule that failed the person it was written for

  • The Pebble Board is street furniture, not a storefront. A room on this street is permanent and singular; a board is defined by being replaced, edition after edition, with the old ones kept. A shop whose entire stock is swapped out every gathering is a periodical, and giving it a shopfront would quietly have made every other shopfront look temporary. So it stands on the pavement outside the shops, in the same category as the signpost to the campgrounds. Your Room also already holds the one community storefront, and that one means something exact: one person decorates one room.
  • The frame is constant and the paper is not — the second place on this street where two pages are meant to resemble each other, and the second is not a licence for a third. A noticeboard, like the Arcade’s cabinets, is one body with different things behind the glass. Each edition still gets its own share card, because the card is where the shortcut would be invisible.
  • The first edition is Norah Hobbs’s, collected at a gathering on 2026-06-25 and built by her in the block editor before being rebuilt as a page. The cream, the tape, the tabbed drawers and the wording are hers. Two of her three typefaces were substituted and one was kept: Caveat is the Faery Yurt’s hand and Special Elite is the Zine Table’s typewriter, so a punk-paper noticeboard set in the zine’s own typewriter was the exact collapse this street refuses. Atkinson Hyperlegible stayed, because it is the Braille Institute’s low-vision face and swapping the body text would have been an access regression rather than a style choice.
  • Twenty-one hotlinked thumbnails became twenty local files, and the security header did not move. The page arrived pointing every card at img.youtube.com, which img-src 'self' renders as a broken image — and loosening the policy to let them through would have asked Google for a picture on every card the moment the board opened, telling them who was reading it before anybody pressed anything. Cached at build time instead. Verified in a network panel: every request on load is same-origin, and one press produced exactly one iframe at the right id with eighteen buttons untouched.
  • Two of the twenty-one videos were not what the board thought they were, which is the argument for resolving ids rather than trusting them. OK Go’s “And the Adjacent Possible” had gone private since the gathering; the entry is kept in the data with what was measured, the way the Jungle Room keeps its dead cams. NEONI’s “Carry On Wayward Son” plays and refuses to embed, so it is a door dressed as a door rather than a play triangle that cannot play.
  • Four images came off and their cards stayed on. A screenshot of the City of Austin’s Facebook post wrapped around a crowd of about forty identifiable strangers at a Pride event; a screenshot of an Instagram story carrying somebody’s phone status bar; a foundation’s product render credited as a photograph of two people wearing it. Two widely-reproduced memes were dropped for having no attributable source. The stories are all still there, in our own words, which is what the cards were carrying anyway.
  • No total. The board arrived saying how many things had been collected. check-counts.py would have refused a typed one and a generated one would have passed — and still been wrong, because the pebbling cabinet refuses a tally on the grounds that pebbling is explicitly not about volume. Same practice, no game in the room, same answer.
  • A lightbox, because a watercolour spread at 250px is not a watercolour spread. A native <dialog>, so the focus trap, the inert background and the return of focus are the browser’s rather than ours. The description is shown as words under the picture rather than only announced, so it reaches everybody. Escape is handled explicitly anyway: with the lightbox open and focus inside it, a real Escape keydown arrived undefended and the dialog stayed open. That may be an artifact of the browser it was tested in — but a modal a keyboard user cannot leave is not a thing to take on trust, and the fix is four lines.
  • A rule was rewritten because it failed the person it was written to protect. This site said flatly that it does not publish photographs of children, reasoning that a parent can consent but the child cannot take it back in ten years. That reasoning was sound and aimed at the wrong case: it read as a ban on a teenage volunteer consenting for herself, and would have taken her own activity off the board while leaving the two adults standing beside her on it — which is what nothing about us without us exists to refuse, and that motto has no lower age bound. What carries over unchanged: the consent must be the young person’s own and a parent’s does not substitute for it, withdrawal is deletion and works for them exactly as for everybody, and somebody too young to give that consent themselves is still not published with no parental override. Ryan’s call. Rewritten on the terms page and in the data; the old decision is marked superseded rather than deleted, because the old reasoning is why the new one has to say which case it is about.
  • A consent record is not a booking for Enid’s wall. Every photograph here used to hang there, so “has an entry” and “is on the wall” were one fact. The board’s are the first that belong to another room only, and hanging them on her wall to satisfy a bookkeeping rule would have been a tool curating a room it is not about. make-polaroids.py keeps the whole contract and only declines to build the polaroid — and refuses outright if a photo is off the wall and published nowhere else.
  • Sharing a drawing at a gathering is not publishing it. Community artwork now records its maker’s permission as a fact separate from the maker’s name, and make-pebble-board.py refuses without it — make-polaroids.py’s rule about photographers, arriving for the people who draw.
  • New: tools/make-pebble-board.py and pebble-board.js. The board joins check-jukebox.py’s lists in the same commit that opens it, every edition rather than only the current one, because a dead link on a back issue is worse than one on the page people are reading. love.css gains §21 and the two cross-cutting sections renumber to §22 and §23.

2026-09-21 — Club Chronic, and the room that refuses the one effect it obviously wants

  • Club Chronic is open. Freaks to the front: punk music and punk community, the story of scenes that built their own doors, every playlist we keep pasted up by the entrance, and a rack of records along the back wall. Ryan’s brief.
  • It does not strobe, at any setting, and the room says so on the door. A punk club has exactly one obvious ambient effect and it can cause seizures. That looks like the campgrounds’ rule and is a different rule — the field is still because quiet is what it is for, and this room is still because the obvious idea here hurts people. A room whose whole argument is come in as you are cannot have a light in it that puts somebody on the floor. MAX GLITTER buys nothing in here and the copy says that is on purpose rather than unfinished.
  • And it says out loud that a loud room is itself an access barrier. Volume, crowds, no exit, no quiet room, no seat — for a lot of us the gig this room is about is unsurvivable. A scene that lets anybody in and then builds the only entrance out of volume has not finished the job. The honest thing about this being a page is that you can leave mid-song and nobody notices.
  • The zine table is the room this could have collapsed into, and the split is structural. Both are punk, both are photocopied, both are made of paper. But the zine is one sheet you hold, cut up with scissors and scattered at angles, and the page is the paper. This is a hundred small rectangles pasted onto a black wall, flat and overlapping, because that is what a wall by a venue door does over years — collage as sediment rather than collage as violence. One sets a typewriter and a handwriting; this sets a condensed display face, because a flyer crams a whole bill onto one sheet, and a marker that went over the paper rather than being it.
  • The selection in the rack is not ours and the room leads with that. Six racks, their order and their themes come from It Take a Joyful Sound on Star Stuff. Every note was written for this room rather than lifted from that page, which is make-readings.py’s rule about the glossary applied to a sibling site: two of our own sites reciting the same paragraphs makes an argument you can only follow by having read the other one. The essay is linked and is the better thing.
  • Thirty-six ids, every one checked on its own watch page before it went up — status, embeddability and length — and every title and channel resolved against YouTube rather than taken from the link text they arrived with. All thirty-six play, all thirty-six can be embedded, and every button says how long it runs before you press it.
  • Spotify is the first frame on this street that is not YouTube. The signature playlist is on both, and the Spotify one is the complete version, so the stage carries both. That cost an origin in the Content-Security-Policy and one in love-embed.js’s own allowlist — and the generator checks the same list a third time, because a URL that is in neither renders as a blank box with no error anywhere. Three places on purpose: the browser enforces one, the script enforces another, and the build refuses before either can show somebody nothing.
  • Neither playlist says how long it runs, and make-club.py refuses one that does. A song has a length; a list somebody keeps adding to does not, and storing today’s total publishes a number that is wrong next week and authoritative in the meantime. That is make-jungle.py’s rule about live cameras arriving on a record shelf — and this is the first room that holds both halves at once, requiring a runtime on thirty-six objects and refusing one on two others, in the same tool.
  • One builder still makes every embed. The playlists need a whole URL rather than an eleven-character id, and a builder that accepts any URL is a hole — the id check was always really refusing to point this site’s frames somewhere nobody chose. So the URL form is allowed and checked against a list of origins.
  • The counter caught a stale claim in the new stylesheet before it shipped, where a comment called this the second room whose type runs both ways. Rewritten to stay true however many there are.
  • Three faces nothing else here sets, twenty-five new contrast pairs, and one colour retuned: red on flyer paper measures 2.91, so the siren stays on the wall and never touches the paste-up. The staples measured 2.00 and are their own darker grey now — a fixing you cannot see holding up a thing you can.

2026-09-20 — the thirteen unmeasured colours, and a gold room that had been rendering green

  • All thirteen are resolved, and resolving them found a live bug in two other rooms. The list came from a note this site started printing after a colour in the contrast checker drifted from the stylesheet. It is a refusal now: every colour declared in :root is either measured or named with a reason, and nothing else gets in.
  • Five were a palette the pebbling cabinet outgrew. That room once kept its six carryable things in :root; then it decided every fill had to clear 4.5 against the outline that draws it, which squeezed them all light, and the new values went into pebbling.js beside the shapes they fill. The dark originals sat on afterwards, declared and painted nowhere — a palette that looked like the source of truth and had not been true since. They are deleted. Every colour the game actually paints was already measured.
  • One carried text and nobody knew. --peb-glass was the shore’s START button on hover — a real text ground — and it was named after the sea glass in the game, which is a different green entirely. Renamed --peb-press and measured: 4.72, which passes. The misleading name is why nobody had looked.
  • One was Helen’s ivy, and one was her ember. Both are painted with hexes written inline in her page, the way her crown is, so nothing here had ever named them. Her own copy says the ivy came in through the window frame and was never asked to leave, which is as clear a statement as a room can make that you are meant to see it. Measured on all four of the yurt’s grounds: ivy 3.9 to 4.9, ember 6.0 to 7.5.
  • One was a brand strip on the campfire’s television at 1.54 against its own casing. Invisible ornament is not ornament; it is lightened until you can see it.
  • The rest are ornament that carries no text, exempt with their measurements written down rather than waved through. The Chappell’s four stained-glass colours sit at 1.75 to 2.46 on the nave, and they are deep because stained glass is — lifting them to 3:1 would not make the window clearer, it would make it not a window. The campground’s post and board frame at 1.48, in a field that is deliberately the dimmest ground on the street and whose type carries all of it. The dark half of Helen’s ivy at 2.53 against the ivy it shades, which is two tones of one drawn plant. The plank under her bookshelf is at 1.38 and is left as she drew it, with the number recorded so the next person finds a decision rather than an oversight.
  • And then the check found two custom properties declared twice in one :root. The Solarpunk Hermitage had taken --leaf for its green and --sky for its daylight, and both names were already in that block: --leaf is The Chappell’s gold leaf and --sky is the pebbling shore’s overcast. A duplicate there is last-wins and silent, so for as long as that was live a gold room rendered green and a grey sky rendered blue, in two rooms nobody had reason to reopen. They are --sprout and --daysky now.
  • The same collision had already been caught once, in the contrast checker’s own Python, and fixed only there. Nobody thought to look for it in the stylesheet, because the Python failed loudly and CSS does not fail at all. check-classes.py refuses a custom property declared twice now, alongside the class names and the section numbers — three kinds of name in one file, all of them last-wins, all of them silent.
  • The coverage check was vacuous on its first pass and was rewritten. It scanned the whole source for a colour, which meant the exemption list counted its own entries as measured and the run passed while doing nothing at all. It reads the pairs now, and it was tested by adding a colour, by changing one, and by putting a hex in a comment — refusing the first two and ignoring the third, since that block quotes colours in its own prose.

2026-09-20 — the set said it was off while it was on, and the stylesheet was the one lying

  • Ryan found the television reporting the wrong thing about itself. A column of text beside the running video announcing THE SET IS OFF, overflowing its own box, and sometimes naming a different programme from the one playing. Three symptoms, and underneath them one bug and a half.
  • The bug: [hidden] loses. The browser’s own rule for the hidden attribute is [hidden] { display: none } at specificity (0,1,0) — which any ordinary class rule that sets display ties with and then wins, because it comes later in the file. So offPanel.hidden = true did nothing visible. The script was correct and the stylesheet was overruling it, silently. The panel had never once been hidden.
  • It was the third time in one room, which is the actual finding. The way-out link had it, the play button had it, and both were patched one selector at a time — which is exactly how the whole off panel was left still waiting to be found. The guard is in §2 now, for everything on the street, and the per-component patches are gone: doing it per component is what hid the third one.
  • And the half: the panel was only ever written on the path that does not play. Retuning while the set was on built a new player and left the panel holding whatever had been tuned the last time it was off — so the stale text Ryan saw was not a rendering artefact, it was a real second bug wearing the first one’s clothes. What the panel says is derived from the tuned channel on both paths now. A panel that is only correct on one of two paths is a panel that will be found wrong.
  • The overflow was real too, and the fix is that 16:9 is a floor rather than a ratio. A channel title runs to a hundred characters; at phone width that is four lines, and with the runtime and the button under it the panel wanted 215px inside a 152px screen — putting the one control that matters below the bottom of the set. Scrolling inside a television to find the play button is not a fix. The screen is a grid now whose row takes the height of the tallest thing in it, so the set is exactly 16:9 on every channel at normal widths and grows only on a phone, where 16:9 would be too small for the card to fit in. Nothing is truncated and nothing is unreachable, which matters more than a set that never moves.
  • The runtime and the play button moved onto one line, because they are one sentence — how long it runs, and the control that starts it — and because it saves the set a whole row of height where the room is tightest.

2026-09-20 — one television instead of twelve, and two sections numbered twenty

  • The campfire is a room now rather than a grid. Green shag, three bean bags in colours nobody agreed on, a wooden coffee table with rings on it, and one shared television the chairs are all pointed at. Ryan’s brief. The twelve separate players are gone; the listing moved into a Channel Listing that opens and closes, and the set plays whatever you pick.
  • Tuning and playing are two different things, and the set keeps them apart. While it is off, previous and next move the channel in silence — the screen says what it is on and how long that runs, and nothing is fetched. You can walk the entire listing without a single request leaving the page; verified in the network panel at five channel changes, zero requests, zero iframes. Once it is playing, changing channel changes the picture, because by then you have asked for a television and pressing play twelve more times would be a worse room and no more consented.
  • The dial says where it is going before you press it. A next button that only says “next” is the one place this design could have quietly broken the rule that every control on this street names its runtime first — so the slip of paper beside the buttons names the channel it is about to tune to and how long that one runs.
  • The channel that cannot be embedded is not skipped. It sits in the running order like everything else, and reaching it turns the screen into a door: the play button disappears, the runtime says this one will not play here, and a way out to YouTube appears instead. Stepping silently over it would hide a fact about somebody else’s permissions that this room states out loud everywhere else.
  • One place builds a YouTube iframe, and now two things call it. The set retunes, which the press-to-play plate never had to do — but a second copy of those attributes is one copy that gets a referrerpolicy fixed and one that does not, silently, in the security-relevant half of that file. love-embed.js exposes the builder and keeps the contract in one place. Writing that exposed a hoisting bug on the way: naming the local variable frame would have shadowed the function it was calling one line above, and node --check passes that happily because it is valid syntax.
  • The bright chairs cannot carry themselves. Every one of them measured under 2.3 against the rug — a chair you cannot pick out of the carpet, which is the Jungle Room canopy arriving as upholstery. So every object in that scene is carried by its outline, measured against the rug on one side and its own fill on the other. The television is the single exception: it is dark enough to separate from the rug by itself and a dark line round it would only muddy it.
  • And the coffee table cannot carry a sentence in either direction. Body copy on that wood measures 3.29 in the dark ink and 4.20 in the pale one, both under the threshold. It is furniture, not a surface to set type on, so the explaining sentence came off the table and onto the page; what stayed is the buttons and a pale slip of paper, which carry their own grounds with them.
  • Two class names were already taken, twice. .telly and .dials are The Latibulum’s wireless and television, and .tuner is its tuning scale — check-classes.py refused all three in turn. The repair went wrong in a way that tool cannot see: renaming across the whole stylesheet instead of one section rewrote the burrow’s own selectors, leaving latibulum.html wearing a class with no rule anywhere. A class nobody styles is exactly the check that file deliberately does not make, and for good reasons it states. The section was restored verbatim from the last commit rather than patched again.
  • And underneath all of that, two sections numbered §20. The Hermitage was added with the number Print already had, and dropped in between §18 and §19 — so every cross-reference by number in that file had two places to land. check-classes.py read both and reported collisions as “§20 and §20”, which looked like a display bug. It refuses a duplicate or an out-of-order section number now, and the file runs in sequence again.
  • A colour in the checker had drifted from the stylesheet. --cedar was lightened so a drawing’s outline would pass, and check-contrast.py kept the old value — so one pair in that file spent a commit measuring a colour that was no longer on the site. It is fixed, and every run now prints which colours declared in :root the checker has never heard of. There are thirteen, all pre-existing; they are reported rather than refused, because some are genuinely decorative and one is a variable nothing uses at all, and a checker that blocks a deploy on somebody else’s unfinished decision is the wrong shape.
  • The three chairs have something on each of them, and each names a real entry in our glossary. Stimming, parallel play — being alone together, which is what three chairs and one screen and no obligation actually is — and stim-watching, which is Jim Irion’s term and his account, and is the reason nobody here will ask why you have put the hummingbirds on for the fourth time. Two of the three carry somebody else’s words and say so on the chair.

2026-09-20 — three benches in the Hermitage, and a check that stopped guessing at Latin

  • The watering hole is open, which finishes the room. Three tables pushed together under the long window: a herbarium, a solar bench, and a table of Star Stuff. A watering hole is where you learn from the people next to you, so nothing on these benches is a lesson — it is somebody else’s work left out where you can pick it up.
  • One room, three postures, and that is what keeps the three zones apart. The cave is enclosed, round-cornered and dark and you are sitting in it. The campfire is seated too, and its cards are rounded because a bean bag has no corners. The workshop is flat-topped and square — worktops with a lip at the front, things laid out on them, and you are standing up.
  • Every specimen in the herbarium is there because one of our own pages argues from it, and make-hermitage.py refuses one that is not. Dandelion, tulip and orchid off the sensitivity metaphor; a bamboo rhizome for Helen Edgar’s Autistic Rhizome; a fern frond because it is the picture our glossary reaches for to explain a fractal; moss, lichen, mycelium and an envelope with nothing in it, off four Star Stuff pieces. There is no sunflower, although sunflowers are the plant growing outside the door — we have no page about them, so there is nothing to press, and the table says so.
  • Nothing in it is named to species, and the check that enforces that had to be thrown away and rewritten. A Latin binomial typed from memory is a taxonomic claim nobody checked, wearing the one kind of authority a herbarium sheet has. The first version looked for Latin endings — and missed Taraxacum officinale, the single most likely binomial to end up on this table, then missed two more after two rounds of widening, while every widening walked it closer to firing on ordinary prose. A tripwire that is allowed to miss and at risk of crying wolf is the worst of both. So the shape is the check now and the English words are the allowlist: a name of one capitalised word and one lowercase word is refused unless the second word is one this table has declared. That catches every binomial rather than the ones somebody anticipated, and it costs one word added on purpose when a plain name like fern frond arrives. All of this was found by putting a binomial in on purpose, which is the only reason any of it is known.
  • The solar bench is books and videos about the thing on the roof. The videos came off our own Solarpunk entry and the reading off our own Electrify Everything — which is the only item on any of these three tables that we wrote. Every id was checked on its own watch page and every title and channel resolved against YouTube rather than taken from our link text, which mattered here: our glossary credits the first of them as “ft. @Andrewism”, and the channel that published it is Our Changing Climate. Two different facts, and only one of them was written down.
  • Griffith’s Electrify is the second id in this file resolved by search rather than lifted, for the same reason Care Work was, and it was requested and answered before it went up. Twice in one room is a pattern rather than an accident: our pages name an author far more often than they link a book.
  • The Star Stuff table refuses anything that does not point at starstuff.earth. A bench with somebody’s name on it holding something else is a mis-filed thing that reads as a claim. Six pieces, all linked out and none reproduced — including A Field Guide to the Ways of Being Alone, which says a lone wolf is not an outcast but a disperser, and is the keystone of this whole pitch arriving from the sibling site rather than from us.
  • Three of the herbarium’s sheets come off pieces on the third table, and they say so. That is what a workshop with three benches in one room looks like, and it is the argument for putting them in one room rather than three.
  • Nine new contrast pairs, including the first ground on this street that is a lighter card on a darker surface — a pressed sheet laid on a worktop, which is the opposite way round from every card here. 338 pairs, none failing.

2026-09-20 — the first daylit room on the street, and a laptop that frames the street inside it

  • The Solarpunk Hermitage is open, on pitch 03 at the far end of the campgrounds. Ryan’s own place: a wood cabin on wheels bristling with panels, sunflowers taller than the door, an aerial going up out of the quiet.
  • It is daylit, and that is the whole of its separation from everything else here. Every other world on this street is a dark ground with light put on it — neon on black, candle on canvas, lamp on earth, a screen in a dark arcade, shafts through a canopy. This one is the morning. A cabin that runs on the sun and reads as midnight would be an argument against itself, and the panels on that roof are the reason the lamp in the cave never gutters. It also means the arrival works twice in opposite directions: out of the field’s cold dusk into a lit tent at pitch 01, and out of the same dusk into full sun at pitch 03.
  • The cave, the campfire and the watering hole are David Thornburg’s and are named as his. They are the primordial learning spaces from From the Campfire to the Holodeck, which our own glossary documents: the cave is where you learn from yourself, the campfire is where you learn from a storyteller, the watering hole is where you learn from peers. Taking a framework’s shape and not its author’s name is the failure this site keeps attribution for. There is a fourth, Life, where you make something out of what the other three gave you; the room says out loud that it is not in here, it is the door you came in by.
  • And the argument the pitch stands on is Betsy Selvam’s. Lone wolfing is being alone well rather than being left out, and that distinction is hers, credited by name in the room and in the liner notes, not folded into our “we”.
  • The cave is the hardest room this street has had to keep apart from its neighbours. It is the fourth warm enclosed space here, after the Faery Yurt, The Latibulum and The Den — and “it is a cosy reading nook, it should feel like the other cosy places” is the Latibulum argument arriving a second time, which is exactly when it stops sounding wrong. Three structural things keep it: the other three are each lit by one flickering source in the dark and this one is evenly lit at reading brightness, with the candles as ornaments on a room that does not need them; the others are canvas, earth and panelling and this is textile, with no wood and no straight edge in it but the shelves; and you reach the others out of the cold, while you reach this one out of full daylight and it is darker than where you came from.
  • Every book on those shelves is one we already argue from, and each says which page of ours put it there — read out of the Knowledge System mirror’s own frontmatter rather than typed. Butler, Baldwin, Thornburg, brown, Kropotkin, Piepzna-Samarasinha, Chapman, Totton, Silberman, Kelly. A borrow link is required and a buy link is not, and make-hermitage.py refuses a spine without one: our library page says knowledge is infrastructure and access to it is mutual aid, and a shelf that could only tell you where to buy would be a shop with an argument painted on it.
  • One of those ids was assembled from memory and was wrong, in the file whose own header says not to do that. Care Work is the one book our pages link without an Open Library id, and the id written for it pointed at a different work entirely. It was caught by checking, resolved against Open Library’s search, and the whole episode is written into the data file rather than quietly corrected — every borrow and buy link in there was then requested and answered before any of it went up.
  • The campfire is Ryan’s list of documentaries and nothing on it was chosen in this repo. Every id was checked on its own watch page — status, embeddability and length — and every title and channel was resolved against YouTube rather than typed off the links that arrived. Every button says how long it runs before you press it, and make-hermitage.py refuses one without a runtime, which is the exact inverse of make-jungle.py refusing a cam that has one. A live camera has no length to give and a film does; the two tools keep the same promise and must not be made to agree.
  • One of the twelve plays and cannot be embedded, which is the Jungle Room’s lesson arriving in a new room from a new list. It is published as a door out rather than a screen, shaped like a door so it does not promise a picture it cannot show, and saying why on its own face. Its title and channel came off the watch page, because YouTube’s oEmbed refuses it too.
  • The laptop in the cave frames stimpunks.love inside stimpunks.love, and it is the real street — you can walk down it from in there. It still waits to be pressed, for a different reason than the jukebox facades: nothing third-party is involved, but a whole copy of the street loading itself unasked means a second intensity dial underneath the first, every font served twice, and a screen reader walking the entire site again for somebody who came in to read a book.
  • And it refuses to nest. A laptop showing the street is a laptop you can walk back into this room through, and the third one is not a joke any more — it is a page that will not stop loading and an accessibility tree nobody can get out of. A hermitage that finds itself already inside a frame says one screen is enough instead of offering the laptop.
  • The security header had to change for any of that, and it is the only loosening in the policy. frame-ancestors was 'none', which forbids this site being framed by anybody, itself included, and X-Frame-Options was DENY. They are 'self' and SAMEORIGIN now. Clickjacking protection against every other origin is unchanged; what is permitted is exactly one page of ours embedding another page of ours, after a press. The reasoning is in _headers and in tools/make-csp.py beside the line that generates it.
  • The contrast checker had to learn a room that is dark on light. This is the first one on the street whose type sits on a bright ground all the way down, which inverts the usual failure: the dangerous inks here are the pale accents rather than the dim greys. It refused three things before the room shipped — the cabin’s outline on its own timber, the leaves drawn on the ground band, and a sunflower held to the wrong threshold — and it also caught a collision in its own source, where the new room’s green was named LEAF and silently overwrote The Chappell’s gold, failing five of that room’s pairs. A checker catching a bug in itself is the argument for having it. 329 pairs, none failing.
  • Two faces nothing else here sets, because no two rooms on this street share a voice. Fraunces swells and curls; Sora is drawn on a grid with flat terminals. Tech and nature, set in type, rather than one of them in fancy dress.
  • The watering hole has its benches in and nothing laid out on them. A herbarium, a solar bench and a table of Star Stuff, marked as what they are rather than hidden until they are ready — the same honesty the empty storefront and the zine’s empty line use.

2026-09-20 — two drawings on the campground’s board, and a pitch taken at the far end

  • The field’s pitches have pictures on them now. A yurt sat against the trees with its doorway lit, and a wood cabin on wheels bristling with solar panels. They are what you can see of each pitch from the gate, which is what the board has said it is for since the field opened: read the sign before you go in.
  • An open plot has no drawing at all, and that is the whole grammar rather than an omission. What is pitched is what you can see from here; bare ground looks like bare ground.
  • They are signs, not rooms, which is the only reason two of them may sit on one board. Both are drawn in the field’s own inks — spruce and bark for the masses, moss for every line, weight rather than tint doing the hierarchy — because you are looking at them across cold ground at night. Neither one is a preview of the world inside it, and neither one may become one.
  • What tells them apart is one lit colour each, and the two lights do opposite things. The yurt emits: candle amber out of the crown, out of the doorway and onto the grass in front of it, round and soft-edged and strung with lights. The hermitage catches: nothing on it glows except one window, the moon lands on its panels, three sunflowers hold the only warm colour on the pitch, and the aerial is the one line reaching off it. Round and warm against angular and cool. The rule for the next pitch is written beside them in love.css: the field’s inks, one light of its own, and not either of those two lights, because they are taken.
  • And that one window is a screen rather than a flame, which is the pitch’s argument in the only place a drawing can make it. Lone wolfing is not being unreachable; it is choosing the reach. A hermitage lit by a candle would have been a nicer picture and a different idea.
  • Nothing in either drawing moves, at any setting, like everything else out here. The yurt’s fairy lights twinkle on its own page and are still on the board, because this is the field’s drawing of the tent and not the tent.
  • Pitch 03 is taken and has no door yet. The Solarpunk Hermitage, Ryan’s, at the far end — which is where the board already said it was for the people whose whole specification is distance from the street. It is a post with a sign and a drawing and no link, because a campground has that state and a website usually pretends it does not. The honest alternative was publishing a room before it was a room.
  • The card stopped listing the pitches by hand. make-og.py had the plots typed into a string literal, and again into the alt text, in the file whose entire argument is that a card must not be able to disagree with its page. It reads them off the board’s own posts now — middots on the card, commas in the alt text, because a screen reader reads a middot out loud — and refuses to build a field with no plots in it.
  • The drawings are measured, because WCAG does not reach them. The canopy in the Jungle Room is why: it came in at 2.18 against the ground behind it, a smudge with no telling one leaf from another, and nothing was looking. Ten new pairs — every line against open ground and against the ground band, both lit colours against the mass they sit on, the dark disc inside a sunflower, the glazing bars on that screen. 294 in the file, none failing. Holding one ink at one threshold is why there are ten and not thirty: a ladder of dimmer greens would each have needed arguing about.
  • The drawing of Helen’s yurt is ours and is said to be. It is the field’s sign for her pitch, in the field’s inks, seen from outside in the dark; it changes nothing on her page. That is written into the liner notes beside her credit rather than left to be inferred, for the same reason the one colour of hers this site changed is written down in four places.

2026-09-20 — a door at the back of the Jungle Room, and the other Jungle Room behind it

  • The Den is open, and it is an easter egg. There is no door for it on the street. The way in is a quiet line at the foot of the Jungle Room — the same shape as the door through to The Chappell, turned right down: a hairline, small type and the room’s dimmest ink, below everything else in the room. You find it by getting to the bottom.
  • Quiet is a visual setting and not a perceptual one. That door is an ordinary focusable link with real words in it, in the tab order, at full size, read out by a screen reader like anything else on the street. On a Disabled people’s site a secret only a sighted mouse user can find is not a secret, it is an exclusion, so the floor is written into the stylesheet beside the rule: nothing hidden, no one-pixel target, no display:none.
  • It is Graceland’s Jungle Room, which is a real room and a better joke than the pun. Elvis Presley’s den: Polynesian furniture bought after Blue Hawaii, fur over the seats, an indoor waterfall of cut field stone on the north wall, and a green shag carpet. People have been rude about it for fifty years. And in 1976 it was converted into a recording studio, and the bulk of his last two albums came out of it — From Elvis Presley Boulevard, Memphis, Tennessee and Moody Blue. That fact is what turns a pun into a room: a carpet is a joke, a discography made on top of one is a listening bench.
  • Sixteen cuts, in the order they were recorded, with the night each one was made. Twelve from the February week and four from the three nights in October, ending on the last studio session of his life. The track list, the session dates, the songwriters and the album attributions came off the two albums’ own articles.
  • Nothing was guessed and nothing was typed from memory. Each title was searched on YouTube, the candidates filtered to Presley’s official channels, and every id then checked on its own watch page — channel, playability, embeddability, length. Twelve are on the estate’s art-track channel and four on his VEVO. And every runtime was matched against the released one, because searching any of these turns up alternate takes on the same official channels: a room whose argument is these are the records that came out of that carpet cannot quietly hand somebody a rehearsal.
  • The writers are named on every cut. Presley wrote none of these songs. Gatlin, Sedaka and Cody, Mark James, Dennis Linde, Fred Rose, Layng Martine Jr., Lloyd Webber and Rice, and the rest. A room that credited only the voice would be doing the one thing this site keeps attribution for, in the room where the performer’s name is the entire draw.
  • Every button says how long it runs, and that is the parent’s rule inverted rather than broken. make-jungle.py refuses a runtime because a live camera never ends; make-den.py refuses a track without one because a song has a length and withholding it keeps back the thing that lets somebody decide. Two generators, opposite checks, one promise. Read them together.
  • The hardest subroom yet, because this one shares its parent’s name and its parent’s colour. The Chappell at least did not sound like Pink Pony Club. So the split is structural and stated: upstairs the green is the darkness and the light falls from above; down here the room is brown, the carpet is the brightest thing in it, and the track list is set dark on the green. Same hue, opposite job. One is outdoors, open and has no straight edge in it; this is low, closed and rectangular, full of carved frames with the corners notched out. One sets a serif whose strokes swell as they curve; this sets a slab with no contrast in it at all, because one is a botanical plate and the other is a record sleeve from 1976.
  • The share cards invert too, and had to. Its parent’s card is a dark green field with a roof of leaves and light coming down. This one is a brown panelled wall with a slab of bright shag across the foot and the type dark on it. If the two had not inverted, splitting them into separate cards would have bought nothing.
  • And the contrast checker caught the thing the eye does not. A track card is a pane of cream at 78% laid on the shag, so the type on it sits on what those two make and not on the green — measured against the carpet it would have been measuring a ground no word in the room is actually on. The composite lesson again, arriving in the place it is easiest to miss, because the carpet is the thing you see. Twenty-seven new pairs, 284 in the file, none failing.

2026-09-20 — the critter cam comes back as a door, not a window

  • Ryan sent a replacement for CritterVision’s Critter Cam, and it is not dead. The new id answers OK and was live when it was checked — but playableInEmbed is false, on the same channel id as the one it replaces, with oEmbed refusing it with a 401 the same way. CritterVision has embedding switched off across the channel, so no id from them would come out differently. It is published as a way out to YouTube rather than as a screen that would show a refusal where the picture should be.
  • Which is the distinction that room was built around, arriving from outside it. Playing and embedding are two different permissions, and the obvious assumption — a working replacement is an embeddable one — is wrong here. The check is one line and it is now written into CLAUDE.md beside the rule about not picking ids from this repo.
  • Nothing in the room is dark. Both cams that answered UNPLAYABLE have come back from the events page, both were measured before they went up, and both entries carry a replaced note saying what was measured. The jelly cam came back as a screen and the critter cam came back as a link, which is the honest difference between them.
  • A door does not get pressed, either. Every plate in the room ended pressing is what sends the request, which is true of a screen and false of a link-out — following one is. It is generated per state now, and the three notes stopped repeating what the door already says on its own face.
  • And the note in the room had to learn to say that. With nothing dark it said only that every cam is playing — true, and it would have let a reader assume they could all be opened here. It counts the link-outs in the same breath now. That is the second time in two days this paragraph has been wrong about its own grammar: the number word and the noun were glued into one lookup, so the first build of this sentence published “Three cams of them”. Two maps now, and both branches agree with their own counts.

2026-09-20 — the jelly cam comes back, and a generated number with hand-written grammar around it

  • The Live Jelly Cam is in the Jungle Room again. Ryan sent a new id the same day the old one was found dead, which is the process working exactly as DECISIONS.md said it should: the ids on the events page are his, a replacement chosen in this repo would have been a cam nobody chose, and this one came from the person who curates the list.
  • It was checked before it went up. oEmbed returns the same title and the same channel, the watch page answers OK, and playableInEmbed is true — so it is the same cam under a new stream rather than a different thing with a familiar name. That is written into the entry as a replaced note, because a replacement nobody checked is the original bug with a fresh id on it.
  • CritterVision’s Critter Cam is still dark, and the room now says so in the singular without anybody editing a paragraph.
  • Which turned up a small thing worth keeping. The room’s note about dead cams generates its count, for the reason check-counts.py exists — and it generated the count while leaving the rest of the sentence plural, so one dead cam read as “One cam … has gone dark … The entries are … Replacing them”. A generated number in front of hand-written grammar is a sentence that goes wrong the moment the number changes, which is a hand-typed total wearing a better disguise. The whole sentence agrees now. The same edit went through the liner notes, which had the count typed out in full.

2026-09-20 — a viewing room under a canopy, and the one facade on this street that must not say how long it runs

  • The Jungle Room is open. A door on the street that opens under a closed roof of leaves: light coming down in shafts, and screens cut into the shape of the gaps. Every screen is somebody else’s camera pointed at somewhere real, and every one of them is off until you open it. Ryan’s brief.
  • The cams are our own events page’s, and so is the order they are in. Our Watering Hole Hangs puts one of these up in the background every weekday — no slides, no presenter, no outcomes — and this room is that list, in that page’s own groups and that page’s own sequence. It is a list somebody keeps, not one for this street to re-sort. The ids came off the hrefs, read out of the Knowledge System mirror rather than fetched live, because on stimpunks.org a 301 is a failure and not a pass. The train cams are deliberately not here: a cab view down a Norwegian line is a fine thing to hang about in and it is not a watering hole.
  • And this is the one room on the street where a runtime would be the dishonest thing. Every other press-to-play control here says how long it runs before you press it, and make-latibulum.py refuses a track without one, because that number is what lets somebody decide. A live camera has no runtime. The reflex arriving from three other data files is to store the length of whichever stream happens to be up today, which is a number that is wrong tomorrow and looks authoritative in the meantime — so make-jungle.py refuses a cam that has one, which is the exact inverse of the burrow’s check and the same promise. The label says the true thing instead: it runs until you close it. And the room says out loud that the picture may be darkness, rain, an empty waterhole, or a cam between streams, because a camera pointed at somewhere real is often pointed at nothing, and sitting with nothing happening is most of what the room is for.
  • Playing and embedding turned out to be two different permissions, and nothing here knew that. love-embed.js checks that an id is well-formed and builds an iframe; it cannot know that the video’s owner has switched embedding off. The frame loads and shows a refusal where the picture should be — a button somebody presses and presses that never becomes a video, which is the failure make-chappell.py exists to prevent, arriving through a door no checker was watching. Two of these cams are like that. They get a way out to YouTube, dressed as a door rather than as a window, saying why. check-jukebox.py reads playableInEmbed off the same fetch as the status now, for every facade on the street, and a cam already published as a link-out is not reported.
  • Two cams on our own published events page are dead, and that page does not know it. Monterey Bay Aquarium’s Live Jelly Cam and CritterVision’s Critter Cam both answer UNPLAYABLE. They render nothing here and the room says so in its own copy, with their names in it, because a gallery quietly shorter than the page it came from is this site correcting a published page by deleting the evidence. The entries stay in the data file with the date they were found dead on. Replacing them is a curation decision on the events page and not one this street gets to make on its own — Ryan’s call.
  • Nothing was typed off our own link text. Every title and every channel was resolved against YouTube’s oEmbed endpoint, and the three ids oEmbed refuses were read off the watch page’s own owner name. None of these cameras is ours; attribution is the one careful habit this street kept, and a credit taken from a link somebody wrote by hand is a guess wearing a citation.
  • It is night-green under a roof, next to a field that is night-blue under nothing. The campgrounds is the pairing to watch here the way the yurt and the burrow were last time: both are outdoors, both are dark, both are green. What keeps them apart is structural. The field is bare and open with the sky still on it and posts standing alone on the ground; this has a roof, and nothing in it stands alone because everything overlaps something. The field routes its lettering like a park sign, flat and incised; this sets a face whose strokes swell as they turn, because a rainforest is the one place where growing and thickening are the same verb. You are outside in one and inside the other.
  • Every screen is a leaf — two pointed corners, two round ones, the same silhouette on the card and on the aperture, and the player is clipped into it when the button becomes an iframe. It is the reason thirty-odd rectangles of video do not read as a spreadsheet.
  • The contrast checker found the canopy. The band of leaves across the top measured 2.18 against the ground behind it — a smudge, with no telling one leaf from another. WCAG does not reach ambient decoration and it was measured anyway, for the reason the Arcade’s quills were: a canopy nobody can make out is not a canopy, it is a dark strip pretending to be one. All three greens went up. Twenty-six new pairs, and every ink is held against the lit ground as well as the dark one, because the shafts land where they land and a word that only clears in the shade is a word somebody reads in the one place it fails.
  • The light got a layer of its own, and the second reason is the one that mattered. Painted on the text column it stopped at the column’s edges and drew a hard bright line across the page under the canopy — a shaft with a lid on it. But at MAX the light shifts, and whatever carries the light is what fades: on the column, MAX GLITTER would have dimmed every word in the room by a tenth, twice a minute, in a room built for people who came here to stop being asked to work.
  • The share card broke the fit checker, and the fix was in the checker. make-og.py measures where ink lands and refused this card for a crop that does not exist: the canopy’s leaves hang off both sides of their band and are clipped by it, so their boxes run past the edge while nothing is painted there. It skips descendants of a clipping <svg> now — reading the computed overflow rather than assuming it, so this stays a statement about paint — which is the same narrow exclusion check-gentle.py makes for transforms inside a drawing. It also caught a real one on the way: width: 100% resolves against the padding box, so the band’s negative right margin moved nothing and left a visible step 60px in from the edge of the card.
  • Two new faces, and the room is the reason. Yeseva One and Cabin, self-hosted and OFL like everything else here. Cabin is a variable font, so one file is stored and declared twice against the weight axis — the same as Nunito.

2026-09-20 — a burrow under the street, and three class names that were already taken

  • The Latibulum is open. A door on the street that goes down into a hill: round door, lamp, thick walls, a wireless with one station on it, a tube television with one programme, and a shelf of things to do with a bodymind. Ryan’s brief, and his word.
  • The word does both jobs already. Latibulum is Latin, and Lewis and Short give it as “a hiding-place, lurking-hole, covert, den, of animals” before giving the figurative sense and quoting Cicero calling a place the latibulum et perfugium doloris mei — the hiding-place and refuge of his grief. Ryan met it in a Walkabout Saga post; the room checks the sense against the dictionary rather than restating the post, so the two can be read against each other. In our own vocabulary it is a cave, the quiet end of a Cavendish Space, which is what stops it being a theme.
  • It is the second warm brown room on this street, and that is the whole design problem. The Faery Yurt is also candlelit, also earth-toned, also built to hold somebody — and “they are both cosy, so they should feel the same” is the subroom excuse in a woolly jumper. The thing keeping them apart is structural rather than decorative: the yurt reads cream on dark all the way down, and almost every word in the burrow sits dark on a lit plaster wall. One is a pitched column of canvas with lights strung across it; the other is dug into a hill and has no sky in it anywhere. Two new faces for the same reason — a planed slab and a rounded sans, because a smial is carpentry and the yurt is calligraphy.
  • The wireless is Adriel’s and stays where he put it. Ocean Waves is by Adriel Jeremiah Wool, a Stimpunk, and he has given us permission to use it however we like. It is a press-to-play facade anyway, because a permission is not a reason to take a copy of somebody’s seventy-four-minute composition off its own shelf, and because this street does not host anything musical. It is also not a recording of the ocean and the room says so: Ryan’s own line calls it a fractal composition of the nature of the ocean, and knowing that before you press it changes what you do with it.
  • The television is a class you can take lying down. Annette of Itstimefitness, 22:45, seated tai chi and qigong — Repulse Monkey, Parting the Horse’s Mane, Cloud Arms — chosen for our own Campfire Learn Together on somatic practice. Everything in it is seated and not as a modification: as the design.
  • The shelf refuses to be a checklist. Stretch, flap, fidget, meditate, stim dance, body scan, eat, drink, pee — lifted from our own Bodymind Break, and not one of them is a button. No ticks, no streak, nothing that notices which ones you did. A shelf that kept score would have turned a bodymind break into a small unpaid job, which is what the break is a break from — the same argument the pebbling cabinet makes about tallies, arriving in a room with no game in it.
  • MAX GLITTER, down here, is one lamp breathing. Nothing in the room rotates at any setting, so there was no tilt to reset; the single animation is an opacity change over nine seconds on the lamp flame. The room says that on its own face rather than leaving somebody to turn it up and wonder whether it worked.
  • And then the interesting bug, which was in the stylesheet rather than in the room. This site is one love.css holding a set of worlds that share nothing, and a class name is global whatever section it is written in. Four of this room’s names were already taken: .scrawl belongs to the zine table, .shelf to the bookshelf in the Faery Yurt, .knob to the Arcade’s speed control, and .tagline to the street’s six-typeface masthead. Only one of them was visible — the burrow’s trail line came out in the zine’s Rock Salt, which is how it was noticed at all. The other three were quietly inheriting a timber gradient, a button’s border and display: flex from rooms on the other side of the file.
  • So there is a checker for that now. check-classes.py refuses a class claimed by two rooms’ sections of love.css, and — the half that catches the .tagline case, where the stylesheet is innocent and the markup is not — refuses a page wearing a class name another room has claimed. It is the CSS twin of check-ids.py and exists for the same reason: the browser applies the winner and says nothing. A third check was written and thrown away: a class styled in love.css that nothing wears is real dead weight, and it cannot be told apart from a class a script builds through a helper, because svgEl('frond', …) in otterly.js puts a class on an element without the word class appearing near it. It reported seven drawings that are on the screen right now, and a checker that cries wolf is one people stop running.
  • And it found three leaks that were already here, none of them new. Two rules belonging to the quantum room had been left stranded at the bottom of the file, after the print section, which is how the print section came to look like it was claiming .fringe; they are back in their own room. .scrawl turned out to be worn by the zine table, issue #2 and Enid’s room while living inside the zine’s section — three rooms on one class makes it furniture whichever section it was typed in, so it moved to §4 rather than being renamed twice. And the Faery Yurt’s tagline had been quietly taking display: flex and a 940px max-width from the street’s masthead since the room shipped. That one is a class name and not a design decision, so it is .yurt-tagline now; nothing of Helen’s moved, and the room renders pixel for pixel as it did.
  • The share card is two grounds. Earth at the top with the lamp on it and the round door drawn on it, a band of lit plaster along the foot carrying dark type. That inversion is the room rather than a flourish, and a card that left it out would be advertising a different room. New contrast pairs for both halves; the brass moved from #C28F3C to #E3BB6A because a knob measured 1.93 against the lightest of the three woods behind it — a knob you could not see on the cabinet it is screwed to.
  • The burrow’s two facades are in check-jukebox.py. A room with two of them is exactly the size at which somebody decides a list is not worth adding to a checker, and a dead station in a room built for people who are already flat is the worst place on this street to find rot.

2026-09-20 — a pebbling cabinet, and the licence that decided what it could be

  • Penguin Pebbling is in the Arcade. A shore, pebbles on it, three neighbours with nests. Walk into something to pick it up, carry it over, press GIVE. Every gift is taken and every one gets something different said back, because giving is how you find out who somebody is rather than a lock to pick.
  • The first thing to say is that there is already a Penguin Pebbling game, and this is not it. Penguin Pebbling is thirty cards across all five locutions, by Helen Edgar and Ryan Boren, 2026, with Helen’s artwork. Go and play that one. The cabinet takes nothing from it — no cards, no prompts, no artwork, no five-locution structure.
  • And the reason is a licence rather than modesty, which is worth writing down because it nearly was not noticed. The deck is CC BY-NC-SA 4.0. This site is CC BY-SA 4.0, with no non-commercial clause. An adaptation would have dragged NC onto a page that cannot carry it; share-alike means it could not be quietly absorbed; and relicensing is not ours alone to do, because half the authorship and all the artwork are Helen’s. So the cabinet is built on the locution — coined by Amythest Schaber (Neurowonderful), documented by Stimpunks in 2022, written up by Helen at Autistic Realms in 2023 — which belongs to the community. Two of our own things with two different licences is exactly the case attribution exists for, and it is the first time on this site that a licence has changed what a page is rather than what it says at the bottom.
  • It refuses a count, and that is the locution rather than a difficulty setting. No tally of what you gave, no total, no streak, nothing at the end. The neighbours bring you things on their own clock whether or not you have ever given them anything — built as a timer rather than as a reward, because a gift that arrives because you gave first is a trade. A number beside a practice that is explicitly not about volume would quietly make it about volume.
  • The pale screen is the only one on the street, and the contrast work is why the pebbles have shapes. Everything on a shingle beach is a mid-tone including the shingle, so nothing on this screen is carried by its fill: every pebble, penguin and nest is drawn with the same dark outline, and check-contrast.py measures that outline against the three grounds and then each fill against the outline. That is the honest reading of WCAG 1.4.11, which asks about adjacent colours rather than about a fill against a distant background. But holding the fills to 4.5 against the outline squeezes them all light — and a narrow band of light tones is a narrow band of things nobody can tell apart. So the six things you can carry are six different shapes: a round pebble, a smooth one, a shard of sea glass, a speckled stone, a feather, a broken shell. Shape carries it, and the name said out loud on every pick-up carries it again. Thirteen new pairs, 195 in the file, none failing.
  • The card inverts too, and had to. Gold on a pale sky measures 1.9, so the type is dark on light — the only share card on this street that is. It was decided by a measurement rather than chosen.
  • A duplicate id, and a bug that would have been much worse anywhere else. The penguin’s drawing sits in the page’s <defs> as #peng and the player’s sprite was also id="peng", so getElementById returned the definition and the game threw on the first line of start(). It took thirty seconds to find because pressing START does something visible. The same mistake in markup nobody presses would have sat there, which is why the other cabinets’ cloned sprites were built out of <use> against one definition in the first place. All four arcade pages were swept; no others.
  • So there is a checker for that now, and it immediately found a worse one. check-ids.py refuses a page that uses an id twice, and refuses a script that asks by name for an id no page loading it has. Run against the whole site it turned up seven elements sharing id="yurt-says" in the Faery Yurt — make-yurt-sound.py had been emitting a live region inside every sound tile, so six of them were dead markup that had never received a word, and love.js only ever reached the first. It had been live since the regulation nook shipped and nothing had said so, because a duplicate in markup nobody presses does not announce itself. The generator emits one shared region now. Broken on purpose with both real bugs put back; it named both.
  • Two things found by looking at the screen rather than by any check. Every penguin was the same size on the same line, so there was no telling which one you were steering — the neighbours are drawn smaller and staggered up the beach now, and you are the one in front. And the nests were single filled ellipses that read as puddles the penguins were standing in; a nest is a rim with a hollow, which is also what the animal actually builds.
  • And an honest note about the penguins, on the page. Adélie and gentoo penguins do build nests of stones, carry them about, and steal them from each other. The tidy romance — one penguin choosing the perfect pebble for its beloved — is the popular retelling rather than the finding. The locution is named after the image and is not weakened by that. A metaphor does not have to be a finding. There is no stealing in the cabinet, because the game is about the locution and not about penguins.

2026-09-20 — a second cabinet in the Arcade, in which floating gets you nothing

  • Otterly Adorbs. You steer an otter round a kelp bay. It twirls, somersaults, dives and floats on its back; three other otters keep their own rhythms; fish come through at feeding time and go again. Ryan’s proposal, and the name is his.
  • It turned out not to be a blank sheet, which changed the design. The Stimpunks Solidarity Session has been leaving an otter cam running every Saturday for months — cameras on or off, some speaking, some in text, nobody has to watch. Helen Edgar wrote down what that was doing for her in Stimpunks Solidarity and Otters, and Star Stuff built The Stone You Keep (No. 58) on top of it, sourced to the primary literature. So the cabinet is built out of findings rather than out of charm, and otterly.js names the paper beside each one.
  • Floating fills nothing up, and that is the single sharpest constraint in the build. No meter, no stamina, no bonus afterwards — the cabinet says so out loud the first time you roll over. Zine No. 58 refuses rest so you can produce in as many words: the otter is not recharging in order to forage better tomorrow. The friendly version of this game rewards resting, and the friendly version would have put a sentence on this page that another of our pages spends a spread refusing. It is written into CLAUDE.md so the next well-meant improvement gets stopped.
  • A clam takes between 6 and 88 presses, and those are the real numbers. Two observers at Point Lobos in 1964 watched thirty instances over six days: a mussel took a mean of 35.5 blows, range 6 to 88 (Hall & Schaller). The game rolls in that range unrounded and reports your number with no target beside it. The urchin opens in a couple of goes and a stone does not help with it, which is Law et al. 2024 — some otters never carry one, because they are on the prey that does not need one, and the ones who do carry one keep their teeth.
  • Nothing ranks the other three otters. Under food limitation a real population does not converge on one best way to be an otter; it fans out into specialists, each measurably better at its own thing (Tinker, Bentall & Estes, 2008). One of ours mostly floats, one mostly dives, one mostly turns in circles, and there is no scoreboard for any of it.
  • The kelp does the holding. Float in the fronds and you stay where you are; float in open water and you drift. That is co-regulation stated as physics rather than as a metaphor, and it is the one mechanic that is also the reason the Saturday session works.
  • It follows the zine’s correction rather than our own changelog. stimpunks.org’s week-28 entry describes the otter keeping “a favorite stone tucked in a pouch of skin” and returning to it “again and again”. No. 58 went to the primary and found the lifelong favourite rock in an armpit pouch is a popular retelling; what Hall & Schaller support is a stone kept and reused across successive food items within a bout of feeding. The game does the second thing. When two of our own pages disagree, the one that checked wins.
  • A twirl is drawn frames, not a rotation, and that cost real work.
